# Chapter 1: The Count

Nine. Ten. Eleven.

Meera laid each packet on the oilcloth as she said its number, out loud, in the cold top room of the salt house. Her mother had taught her that a number spoken was a number kept. The one time Meera counted in her head, she carried eleven packets over the Long Saddle and called them twelve. Nobody in Ranu let her forget the missing one.

"Twelve."

Redbark, twelve packets of it, each the size of a palm and tied with waxed thread. Enough for one village, if the healer was stingy and the sick were patient. Meera folded the oilcloth over them once, twice, three times, and tapped the roll against her knuckles to check the folds. Three. Good.

The crate sat open at her knee, and she did not like the lid.

A pale patch, broad as a hand, marked the place where the crown stamp should have been. Someone had scraped it down to bare deodar, then rubbed the wood with ash to dull the new colour. The other three lids were the same.

She ran her thumb over the patch. Careful work. It was the work of someone who wanted crown goods to look like nobody's goods.

Then she shrugged. Redbark came to her from the lowlands through a man she had never met, and she had never once asked whose cart it fell off. Questions cost coin. Children's lungs cost more. She pulled the waxed notebook into the lamplight and wrote with the bone stylus.

*Twelve packets redbark. Crate lids scraped, four of four.* Then the price, and the hour.

Kiran called it the most criminal thing about her: a woman who spat at the Closing Law and still kept books like a tax clerk. She called it arithmetic. The count didn't care what she thought of a thing. It only held it.

She turned back to the last delivery. Seven crates had waited at the mill below the village, left by a carter who had given her one instruction and one object. The object was a brass button. Whoever brought the next load would carry its twin, and no twin meant no crate. She had written that down too.

The button was small and domed and bright as a new coin, with a shank so stiff it had never been sewn to anything. It bore no crest. She threaded her needle and stitched it into the spine of the ledger, between the third and fourth gatherings, where only a person who unpicked the binding would find it. A button was a stupid thing to hide a drop in. That was the point.

She was biting off the thread when boots sounded on the outer stair. One set was heavy and one dragged, the gait of two people who had been roped together for days and disliked it.

Roop and Chand came in white to the eyebrows. They were the last pair Meera had sent across and the last to come back. Until the thaw they couldn't stand more than a rope's length apart, and they had opinions about that.

"Kiran's," Roop said, and held out a folded scrap of redbark sacking.

Charcoal, in her brother's slanting hand.

*Didi. Grey cough in Ranu since the new moon. Nimmi's counting: forty-one sick, nine of them under ten, six buried. We boil walnut leaf and it is about as much use as a prayer. Come before the children go.*

Under it, in Nimmi's square hand, a list. *Grey phlegm. Blue lips. Children first. No redbark left.*

And a last line, back in charcoal, because Kiran could not end anything on a bad word. *P.S. Nobody here will kiss me. I've decided it's the ear.*

Meera read it twice. The second time her thumb found the edge of the paper and pressed until the nail whitened.

The ear. Two winters ago he had come back from a crossing with his left ear black along the rim, the colour of burnt bread. He joked about it the same day. The mountain took a bite and spat it out, he said, no taste. He had told that joke forty times since. In all that time he had never said where the crossing was, or who had been on the rope, and when she asked he asked about the goats.

She hadn't pushed. Some things you let a person carry until they set them down.

"Is he well?" she asked.

"He's the one holding the lamp," Chand said. "He coughed once while we were there. Once."

Once was how it started.

Meera folded the note into her coat, then stood and rolled the oilcloth under her arm.

"Wardens come up the valley every tenth day," she said. "Today is the tenth. We're at the cairn by noon, and over the first ridge before dark."

"Roop," said Chand, "she's using the voice."

"Eat something," Meera told them. "Then go sit in separate corners, as far apart as the rope lets you. It'll improve your marriage."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The goat pen under the salt house smelled of warm wool and old dung, and it was the one room in Kothi Shilak that never smelled of salt.

Jassi stood in the middle of it with a hay fork in one hand and a goat's ear in the other, delivering a lecture.

"Pomegranate, I know you've got the cold shoulder, so does everyone. Auntie, don't look at me like that, you bit the last man. Sheriff, move your rump." He saw Meera on the ladder and did not stop. "Third run this month. My knees are as old as the Closing Law and they hold the same opinion of the crown."

"Then stay," Meera said.

"Stay? And who holds Sheriff on the Saddle? Magistrate won't walk beside him. Judge won't walk behind." He dropped the ear, slapped the nearest flank and began harnessing, still grumbling. "My sister keeps asking when I'll come to the orchard. I tell her, after this one. I've been telling her that for six winters."

"You volunteered."

"I did not."

"You were up before me. You've got the harness oiled."

Jassi looked at the harness in his hands as if someone else had put it there. "Forty years old," he said, "and a girl half my age catches me in my own kindness. Ten winters of this, you've done, and you still count your fingers on the stair like a clerk. Twenty-nine and no sense."

"Twenty-nine and paying for your orchard," Meera said. "Stop telling me about your knees."

From the far corner came a soft, tuneless chant. Bindu sat on a wool bale with the red rope across her lap, walking her hands along it and singing the knots under her breath.

*Two over, one under, the third one tells the truth.*

"Thirty-four winters," Jassi said, nodding at her, "and she's never once looked a customer in the eye. Put a rope in her hands and she'd shout down the mountain."

Bindu went pink to the ears and sang louder.

The rope was eight arm-spans of wool dyed madder-red, spun in the house of a Taravan trader, Lata, who sold to them at a fair price and asked nothing. It was the right rope: hand-spun, no longer than the spirits would stand, and soft enough that it did not cut when it caught a wrist. Bindu measured it again, hand over hand.

"Eight," she said. "Still eight. It wanted to be nine."

"It can want," said Meera. She crouched beside her and took Bindu's hands, the knuckles cracked open from the wind. Without being asked, she dug a pot of goat fat from her coat and worked it into the splits. "It'll want a lot of things before the thaw."

Bindu looked at the fat, then at Meera, and said nothing, which was how she said thank you. She went back to her knots.

That was the whole crew, nearly. A man who hated his knees, a woman who sang to rope, two bickering runners and a brother in a fever village.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The Taravan salt merchant was waiting in the weighing room with his boots on the salt scales, which she took as a mark against him.

He wore a good pattu and a better opinion of himself. He wanted a load of rock salt put over the pass to Neshar on the next run, off the Guild's books, and he was willing to pay for the privilege. He set a purse on the scale, then a second one beside it.

"Double your price," he said. "For the first place after this crossing."

Meera looked at the purses. She did not count them. She could see from the way they sat that the second had been added after he saw the stairs.

"Double buys you the place," she said. "It also buys the widow Puja's son a free crossing next run. His mother needs him across before spring. Yours is the coin that pays his rope."

"I wasn't aware I was a charity."

"You aren't. You're a customer who can afford to be."

He raised his eyebrows. "It would help the transaction, you know, if you said please."

"Please costs extra," said Meera. "You've already paid double."

He laughed, which was the right thing to do, and left the purses where they lay. She had left the count and the oilcloth roll on the table upstairs, and she did not like that, but she walked him out through the village herself because she wanted to see the road.

Kothi Shilak had one gate, a gap in a stone wall that kept out nothing but goats. The Closing Law was nailed to the left post on a board gone grey with weather. She didn't need to read it. She had learned it before she learned her letters.

*Between first snow and first thaw, no person shall cross any high pass of Hemkot without the writ of the queen's hand. Whoever so crosses, and whoever shelters, feeds or warms him, shall be hanged.*

The hanging tree stood beyond the post, a single old deodar with a low arm. The wardens had hammered a peg into the trunk for every rope it had borne. The old pegs were grey and split. Two were pale and fresh, from last winter. The bark had not grown over the holes yet.

The merchant looked at the tree and then, very carefully, at the road.

"Good morning," he said, and went down the hill faster than he came up.

Meera stayed a moment longer. There was a third place on that trunk, bare wood, and she knew whose name it was waiting for. The Salt Fox. Hers, and Jassi's, and Bindu's, and Kiran's, but the wardens would start with the head. She turned her back on the tree and counted paces to the salt house instead. Twenty-two.

At fourteen the horns came from the lower road: three blasts, long, then one short. The sound hit the valley wall and came back doubled.

Not noon. Not the tenth day's lazy counting of goats. She was already running.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She took the stair two at a time. The count lay open on the table in the top room, wet ink still shining, with the brass button sewn in its spine and a scraped lid at the foot of the table. She snapped the ledger shut and shoved it inside her coat, hard against her ribs, and belted the coat over it. Then she caught up the oilcloth roll and went down while the horns were still echoing.

Bindu had the bale open when Meera came in. She never said a word and never needed to. The two of them worked as if they had rehearsed it, because they had, four winters running. The roll went into the heart of the raw fleece. Greasy wool closed over it, then more wool, and Bindu sat on top with the red rope in her lap and her face as blank as a stone.

"Twelve," Meera said, once, under her breath, and stood back from the bale. She would not look at it again.

Jassi had a hay fork in his hands and the look of a man who had never wanted to be anywhere else. Roop and Chand had vanished upstairs to be married in a cupboard. The pen was quiet except for the bells.

Boots came up the path outside, and in the yard someone shouted an order. A goat bleated, then bleated again in a different key. Something thudded.

"Corporal."

The voice was not loud. It did not need to be. The yard stopped.

Meera went to the door and put her shoulder to the frame, as if she had only been passing.

A young warden in a grey coat had Sheriff by the harness and one boot drawn back. Another stood behind him with a spear he held as if he'd never asked it a question. Eight wardens in all, snow on their shoulders, breath smoking. At the centre of the yard stood a ninth man, in the same coat, and he was looking at the corporal's boot.

"It is a goat," said the young warden.

"It is a goat," the man agreed, "and it has not been charged with anything." He turned his head. "Put your foot down."

The boot went down.

That was Captain Rohan. She knew him by the grey of his coat and the way the yard bent around him. Three winters she had heard about him: the hunter, the man who hanged two smugglers and seized her crew's loads twice, the one the lowlanders said never slept. She had pictured someone loud.

He did not look like a man who enjoyed anything. He looked like a man who had read the law, checked the arithmetic, and found it came out as written. He was about thirty-five, lean through the face, with a stillness you could rest a ladder on. When he turned, it was all at once, without wasted motion, and he looked straight at her.

It was a short look. It took in her hands, her boots, the doorframe she leaned on, and the way she did not move. He gave her no time to arrange her face. His gaze stopped, very briefly, on the wax at the edge of her thumbnail. The ledger sat against her ribs like a second heartbeat, with the button in its spine and every scraped lid written down inside. Then his eyes moved on.

She pressed the waxed nail into her palm and began, as she always did when she was afraid, to count. Eight wardens. One captain. Six paces from the yard gate to the door. She said the numbers into her teeth.

"I'm told," he said, in the measured voice of a man reading from a page, "that the guide Jassi keeps this house."

Jassi lifted the hay fork a fraction and put it down again. "I keep the goats," he said.

"Then you are the man I am looking for."

Jassi opened his mouth. Meera could see him searching for his sister's orchard and finding only a rope.

"He's got the cold in his chest," she said, and stepped out of the doorway. "He's deaf in the left ear and rude in the right. I answer for him."

The captain regarded her. Not the way wardens usually did, with the flat contempt of a man deciding whether a woman could be ignored. He looked at her the way she looked at a crate lid. Carefully. Waiting to see what had been scraped away.

"Then you will do," he said. "Guide."

Not girl. Not woman. Not mistress of the house.

"What does the captain of the wardens want with a guide," Meera said, "in a month when the pass is shut by his own law?"

"A crossing," said Rohan. "Of the Long Saddle."

Behind her, in the dark of the salt house, a bale of wool sat very still with twelve packets in its heart. Against her ribs, the count held her name for every crossing the law had ever hanged for. The goat Sheriff bleated once and was quiet.

Meera did not say please. She did not say anything at all.

# Chapter 2: The Writ

The fourth plank in the weighing-room floor came up with a groan. Under it lay dust, a dead mouse and nothing else.

Rohan set the plank back and pressed it flat with his boot. He was thirty-five years old and had been in the salt house two hours. His wardens had gone through the three timber floors and the loft, and the corporal had pulled every stair tread to look behind it. They had found goat harness, forty sacks of rock salt wearing the Guild's mark, and a whole wall of pass rope coiled by length on pegs. The pen below he had kept for last. A pen could not be searched fast, and a man who hurried one missed things.

Pass rope was legal to own. That was the trouble with the law. It hanged a man for crossing and let him keep the rope.

The woman who answered for the goat-keeper had followed him from room to room, never closer than six paces, hands folded before her. Her lips moved. He had noticed it on the first floor, and by the second he had stopped pretending he hadn't. She was counting, and not the salt sacks or the wardens. Eleven steps from the stair head to the pen ladder. Nine across the weighing room. Her murmur kept time with his boots, like a woman learning the length of his stride.

Recruits counted when they were afraid. Few counted a stranger.

The pen was warm and low and stank of wet wool. Goats shifted in their stalls, and a man with a hay fork stood among them. In the far corner six wool bales leaned against the wall, corded and pressed. A thin woman sat on the nearest with a red rope across her lap and her eyes on her knuckles. Rohan walked within an arm of her. Behind him the counting stopped, started again and skipped a number.

"Probe," he said.

The corporal drew the iron spike wardens used to find salt in grain and drove it into the first bale to the hilt, then the second, then the third. Each time it came out with greasy wool on the tip and no more.

"Cut one open, sir," the corporal said. "They pack the heart."

"They do." Rohan looked at the fleece. It was the village's winter: every sock, every pattu, every rope between here and the lowlands. "Leave it."

The corporal's face said what he thought of that. Rohan let it. The thin woman did not look up, and neither did the guide, and it was the way she didn't that he remembered.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The whole village stood in the yard when he came up, in a thin white light: shepherds and rope-twisters, women with babies on their hips, an old man whose left hand ended in three fingers. They had the look of people waiting to learn which of them would be taken.

Rohan drew the writ from the leather case at his belt and unfolded it so the back rows could see the brown ink and the crown's red border.

"I am Rohan, captain of the queen's wardens on this side of Shilak. This is a writ of passage in the queen's own hand. It makes my crossing lawful, between first snow and thaw, when no other crossing in Hemkot is." He let them look at the signature. "I go on crown business to the Snow Fair. I must be there in twenty days. That is all I will say of it, and all you need."

It was not all.

Under his collar, in a pocket sewn into the seam at the nape of his coat, lay a lapis stone the length of a thumb joint, cut with a mountain goat. The queen's seal. Regent Pritam meant to stand on the Snow Fair ground at midwinter and be invested lord of Neshar, on the claim that the queen was dying. The queen was ill. She was not dead, and the seal pressed before the assembly would say so in the only voice the hill lords obeyed. Without it, Rohan was a captain with a story, and Pritam had captains of his own.

Inside his coat lay a second paper, its seal broken once and pressed flat since. One line in it he had read until the words went smooth. *Bring in the Salt Fox at the thaw.* For three winters he had followed that crew's tracks, found their crates and never their faces. He did not know why a queen who kept a hanging law wanted the fox delivered and not hanged. He had not asked. A captain who asked why was halfway to deciding not to.

"Two wardens cross," he said aloud. "Myself and my lieutenant. With two guides to tie the rope. The rest of my men return to the post."

"Two guides," said the woman at his shoulder. She had come up without a sound. "Those are the rules, are they? One pair for the Saddle?"

"Two pairs. The mountain's rules, Guide, not mine."

"Then you know them better than most wardens." She said it as if she were entering it in a book.

"One question before we speak of guides," Rohan said, "and I will say what it is for. Two winters ago a warden of mine, Dilan, died on the ice below the Saddle, chasing a smuggler in a storm. He was twenty-three. The report of it runs four lines. It says that where the chase ended there was blood on the ice, and a scrap of skin frozen fast to it, torn from the rim of an ear." He kept his hand away from his coat pocket. "A man whose ear froze to the ice leaves that. Who in this village froze an ear on the pass?"

Nobody spoke. A goat bell clanked in the pen. Rohan had asked a great many yards a great many questions, and he knew the quiet of people with nothing to tell. This was another kind. It was a village looking at its boots.

"Captain," said the guide.

He turned.

"Half this village has lost something to that pass." She did not gesture, and she did not raise her voice. "Hakam there has three fingers. The woman beside him buried her husband in the autumn, and he never came down to be buried. My mother died of the grey cough ten winters ago, because the medicine stood on the wrong side of your law. If you want ears, you'll need a bigger rope."

"The law does not choose who it stops."

"No," she said. "That's what's wrong with it."

"Open the gate and the grain men and the regent's riders use it as well as your sick. A gate has one lock."

"Then make a better lock. Don't leave a village on the wrong side of it."

He had made that argument himself, in a council room, and it had seemed enough. Out here, with a woman's breath smoking before him, it sounded like what it was: a man explaining a noose to the people it hung.

She had not answered his question. She had set a bigger one in front of it, and every person in the yard had let her. Rohan marked that, and put it aside. He had one pass to cross.

"Your name," he said to her.

"You called me Guide in there. You said it as if you'd checked it." One corner of her mouth lifted and went flat again. "I'd keep it. It's the one you can prove."

He could have pressed. A warden had the right. But a name given under a writ was a name entered somewhere. He noted the refusal beside the other things she had not answered.

"Jassi," he said. "You will take my party over the Saddle at first light."

Jassi had stood through the whole exchange with his hay fork upright like a flag. "No," he said.

The corporal's hand moved. Rohan lifted two fingers and it stopped.

"I don't cross without my second," Jassi said. He had gone grey around the mouth. "That's how it's done in this house. Ask anyone."

"Who is your second?"

Jassi's eyes slid sideways, and he did not manage to stop them.

"I am," said the woman.

"No." Rohan said it before he had thought it through, and then he gave the reason, because he had one. "A guide who argues with a warden before her whole village will argue with him on the ice. Or she will lie to him there. I have no use for either."

"I've argued with you in front of everyone," she said, "and I haven't told you one false word. Count them. Then tell me who else walks the Long Saddle in winter. Name him."

He did count. Two hours ago, in the doorway, she had said Jassi had a cold in his chest and was deaf in the left ear. Jassi had just answered him from across a noisy yard, and the left ear had missed nothing. One. He kept the number and said nothing of it.

And he could not name the man she asked for. There was no one. The wardens had hanged the ones they caught and the rest had learned to be somebody else. If he took Jassi and left her, Jassi would stay in the yard. If he took Jassi in chains, a bound pair needed a willing hand on the other end of the rope. A guide whose lies he could count was a rarer thing than a guide who knew the mountain. He had hunted this village three winters and had not met one.

"Cross with a captain who hangs smugglers," he said, "and you may not come back."

"Neither might he." She nodded at Jassi. "He's forty. His knees are as old as your law. He'll walk the Saddle with a stranger over my dead body, Captain, and that's the plain arithmetic."

Jassi muttered something about his knees. She did not look at him.

Rohan studied her. He did not like that she wanted to go. He liked less that he understood why. An old man, a killing pass, and a person who would rather walk beside him than let him walk it alone. He knew that sum, and had never liked the answer.

"Jassi crosses with me," he said. "My lieutenant takes the second. Each pair watches the other's guide. If either guide turns for the lowlands, I still have the other."

"Fair," said the guide. Her mouth moved, a count of something, and then she added, "Captain."

It sounded less like respect than like a name written down so it would not be lost.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Varun was in the mill billet, sitting on an upturned bucket with a rag and a lamp, rubbing at his coat.

"Captain-ji." He looked up with the warm, lopsided smile that had got him through the officers' mess and ten years of Rohan's temper. "You've the look of a man who found rope."

"I found rope."

"They'll hang us with theirs one day." Varun held up the coat. "Look at this. Lost my pewter button on the stair this morning, the good one with the worn crown. The quartermaster's tin held nothing to match, so now I wear brass, in a row of pewter. Cheap, bright, new, wrong. And the cuff is frayed, and the polish they issue is half chalk. I am being asked to defend the realm in a ragged sleeve."

"It offends the regulation," Rohan said.

"I am the regulation's last friend." Varun breathed on the cuff and rubbed at it. "While you were pulling up floors, I walked both approaches to the Saddle. The face has a cornice you could hang a cow from. The gully is longer and ugly, but it sits out of the wind. I've chosen the gully. Safer."

"The guides will have a view."

"The guides will have a view of the face, because it's shorter and they're proud. I walked it in summer, sir. I know what's under that snow."

Rohan thought of the cornice, and of his own twenty days, and found nothing to object to. "The gully," he said. "Tell the guides at first light."

"Captain-ji." Varun went back to his sleeve.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The fine for Puja's house was eighteen silver, and the notice had been nailed to her door since the last sweep.

Rohan had read it that morning, passing. Salt found in a lean-to, a dead husband's stock, taken and sold. The Closing Law allowed the crown to take the goods and the fine, or the house in place of the fine. After nightfall the village was quiet except for coughing behind shutters and a dog complaining about the cold. Smoke from a hundred small fires hung flat over the roofs. He walked down the lane alone, without a lamp, and knocked once.

She was small, grey-haired and sharp-boned, and she opened the door the width of her face. Behind her a child's cough started and was hushed.

"Captain." Her voice did not shake. "The fine is due at the next sweep. I've not forgotten it."

"No." He held out the purse. It was a plain leather one, heavier than he liked, and he had counted it twice at the billet. "Eighteen. Pay it to the corporal's clerk when we have gone. Say it came from a cousin."

She did not take it. Her eyes went from the purse to his face and back, as one reads a price that cannot be right.

"I have no cousin who has eighteen silver."

"Then you have a cousin with a good heart, and you will not tell anyone his name." He put the purse in her hand and closed her fingers over it. They were cold and hard as roots. "Especially not mine. If the village learns wardens pay fines, the next widow will wait for me instead of for the harvest."

"That's not why you're saying it."

"It is a great part of why."

She was quiet. A cough started in the next house and stopped. "My boy says you hang them for the sport," she said.

"Your boy has not seen it done."

He turned up the lane. The cold took his breath as he went. Halfway to the mill, he felt the look before he saw her.

The salt-house door stood open a hand's breadth, with lamplight behind it. The guide stood in the gap with her arms crossed, awake at that hour and watching him. She had seen him leave Puja's step. He could not tell what she had seen him do.

A smuggler's accomplice knew better than to ask a warden questions, and a warden knew better than to answer them. He went by her door at the same pace as before. She let him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It was grey before dawn when he came out into the yard. Frost glazed the cobbles. Jassi was loading the goats by lantern, swearing at each one by name. The thin woman from the pen, the one with the red rope, was going from pack to pack with armfuls of raw fleece, stuffing it under the straps to keep the loads off the animals' backs.

She did it without a word and without looking at him. She kept her face to the work.

Rohan took a pace toward the nearest goat, to check its girth. A tuft of fleece had slipped from under the strap and hung over his wrist, damp and warm from the animal's side. He lifted it, to put it back.

Something dark clung in the grease.

He turned his hand to the lantern. A single grain, smaller than a sesame seed, rust-red against the yellow wool. He rubbed it between thumb and finger. It crushed to powder and left a stain on his skin, and when he raised his fingers to his face, the smell was bitter and resinous, and he knew it at once. Every warden post kept a pot of it for the winter coughs.

Across the yard the ropemaker bent over the next pack with her back to him, humming a knot-count under her breath.

Rohan did not look at the stalls below the salt house, or at the stair, or at the guide, who was just coming through the doorway with a coil of red rope on her shoulder. He folded the tuft of fleece back under the strap, rubbed his fingers once more against his palm, and put what was left of the powder in his pocket.

A warden who let a known load cross was harbouring it, by the letter of the law, and the rope for that was the same rope.

Then he went to find his lieutenant, and said nothing.

# Chapter 3: The Second Slide

The gully was wrong, and Meera had known it since the first step.

She stopped at the lip of the upper bowl and put her glove flat on the snow. It sat hard on top and hollow beneath, like a drum skin stretched over nothing. She pressed down. A hand's depth of crust held, then sank with a soft, sick sigh.

"Wind slab," she said.

"Say it so a warden can follow," said Rohan, a pace behind her.

"Last night's wind blew snow off the face and dumped it here. It's lying on old snow that doesn't want to hold it." She pointed up the bowl at the long, smooth shoulder above them. "Your lieutenant says the gully is out of the wind. It is. That's why the wind put everything it was carrying in here."

Rohan studied the slope as if he were reading it aloud in his head.

"Varun walked it in summer," he said.

"In summer it was rock."

"The face has a cornice."

"The face has a cornice I can see. This has a cornice I can't." She heard her own voice go flat and made it flatter. "We cross singly, thirty paces apart, no stopping, no shouting. Then we're under the rock band and it's the cairn by dusk."

"We cross as you say," said Rohan. "I do not turn back on a guess."

"It's not a guess."

"Then tell me what it is, and I will weigh it."

That was fair, which annoyed her. She had no number for it. She had a feeling in the soles of her feet and ten winters of being right at the wrong moment.

"Odds," she said. "One in four it slides today. If it does, we lose whoever's under it."

"One in four." He nodded slowly, as if she had given him a price. "And the face?"

"One in three. The cornice is real." She hated saying it. "Your lieutenant picked the lesser killer. I just don't like either."

"Then we go the way I can defend," said Rohan, and went to find his lieutenant.

Meera stayed at the lip, flexing her cold hands. Behind her the goats came up in a string, five of them, bells knocking. Jassi walked at their head with his breath smoking and his hood pushed back, because he hated it over his ears.

He dropped back to walk beside her. He did that on a bad slope. He never said why.

"Pull your hood up," she said.

"I can't hear the goats."

"You can hear them in your sleep." She fished in her coat, found the spare mitten liners she had stitched in autumn, and shoved them at his chest. "Wear these under the gloves. Your hands were blue at the last halt, and don't tell me it's your age."

"It's my age." But he put them on, and his face loosened as the wool warmed him. "My sister Leela has apricot trees. Did I tell you?"

"Twice a day for six winters."

"Forty trees. They bloom white in the third week of spring, and the petals get in your tea. Badami's too old for this run, so I left her in the pen. She'll walk that orchard like she owns it, eat the fallen fruit and be sick, and she'll deserve it."

"Badami has your bell?"

"She has her own. This one's from her kid days." He pulled a small, dented brass bell on a thong out of his collar, and it gave a tinny little clunk. "I take it for luck. When I'm done, she goes to the orchard with me, and so does this. Retired. Both of us."

"After this one."

"After this one." He tucked the bell away, slapped her shoulder and went forward, calling to Sheriff in a voice of deep personal grievance.

Meera watched him go. She counted the goats, because she counted everything. Five. Jassi made six on the slope.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They crossed the bowl as she had said, one at a time.

Jassi went first with the goats, a dark string on white. Then Meera. Rohan was to hold thirty paces behind her, and Varun thirty behind him.

At the halfway mark she glanced back, as she always did, to check her spacing. Rohan was not thirty paces off. He was ten, and coming on, his eyes on the slope above and not on his feet. When she looked again, he was eight.

She opened her mouth to snap at him. Then she shut it, because shouting on that slab was stupid, and turned her face to the rock band. Her rule was her rule. He was a warden who had never once been told what to do by a smuggler, and he had decided that on this slope he would stay close.

Behind them, far down the line, Varun's voice carried up, easy and unhurried. "Captain-ji! Strap's gone on my pack. Go on, I'll catch you."

"Quickly," Rohan called back, from only a few paces behind her.

"Quickly, sir."

The rock band was forty paces ahead, where the snow thinned and the slope stopped being a drum. Jassi was nearly there. His bells made a thin bright clatter. Her breath came in short counts. Twenty-nine. Thirty.

Behind her, down the line, iron struck.

Not a probe in snow. A clean, bright ring, metal on rock, once. A hammer's note, or an ice-pick set against stone.

Then, above her, the whole slope answered.

The sound was a crack like a rifle shot, long and flat, running away across the bowl. It travelled under her boots first and hit her knees. The drum skin tore open from one side to the other.

"Down!" she screamed. "Jassi, leave them, come!"

Jassi turned. His face was small and white at thirty paces, and he lifted his hand. Then the shoulder of the mountain slid down onto him and the goats, and there was no bowl and no Jassi, only a roaring wall of white.

A hand fisted in the back of her coat and she left the ground.

He had run the last of the gap. She understood that later, when she could think. Then she only flew. Her shoulder struck rock and she was in a hollow behind a boulder as big as a cart, and something heavy came down across the world.

The first slide poured over the boulder and went on. It was loud. The second was quiet, and worse. The snow came in a long dense roll, like a wet fleece pulled over a face, and over her head rose a sound that was a man taking it across the back.

Then there was no sound at all.

She lay with her cheek against stone, in the small dark of the lee, with a pocket of air no bigger than her two fists around her mouth. She counted. One. Two. She got to forty before her hands moved. Then she dug, clawing up through snow packed like wet clay, until her fingers broke into cold light and she dragged herself out onto the surface.

The bowl was gone. In its place lay a field of broken white blocks, some as big as doors, steaming faintly in the sun. A single goat bell rang somewhere under it, then quit.

Rohan lay half across the boulder's flank, buried to the shoulders, one arm flung out.

She went to him on her knees.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He was breathing. That was the first count she took, and it held her up.

His eyes opened when she cleared his mouth. They found her face, and he said in a thin, careful voice, "Guide. Are you hurt?"

"No."

"Good." He drew in a breath, and his whole face closed on it. "Then I would be grateful if you stopped looking at me and dug for the others."

She did.

Meera dug until her gloves were wet through, and then she dug without them. The wardens' iron probes were in the slide with the goats, so she used her bare hands and a flat piece of stone, and Rohan used his good arm and swore in the cold, formal way of a man who disliked swearing. She called Jassi's name until her voice cracked. After that she called it quietly, at the places where the snow had a certain give.

Rohan worked at the other end of the field. He had found the place where his lieutenant dropped back, a flat patch beside a rock where a pack strap would be mended. He dug there until the light turned yellow.

"There's nothing," he said. "No pack, no print, no cloth. The slide came through here and took the line."

"The line goes down. He would have been taken down the bowl."

"Yes." Rohan stood a while, breathing in shallow sips. "We dug the bowl. We have dug everything below."

"Then he's deeper," Meera said, because there was nothing else to say. She did not look at his face. He had walked beside the man for years.

The sun was down behind the ridge when her stone hit cloth.

She knew it by the colour. A strip of wool, goat-brown, dyed with walnut husk. Bindu's weave.

Her hands did the rest. They uncovered a sleeve, a shoulder, the hood Jassi had hated. She cleared his face, and it was calm. The snow had packed his nose and mouth, and nothing else had touched him. Beside him lay Pomegranate and Auntie, both dead, still harnessed. Nothing had found the other three.

His right hand was shut.

She had to work the fingers open singly. They were stiff and still warm in the middle. In the palm lay a small dented brass bell on a broken thong, its clapper wedged with snow.

Badami's kid bell.

Meera sat back on her heels.

She had thought she would shout. Instead something in her went quiet, like a lamp turned down. Six on the slope. Six, and Bindu singing, and Leela's forty trees in the third week of spring.

"Guide," Rohan said, behind her. He did not touch her. He stood a careful distance off, his left arm held low against him.

"Don't."

"I only—"

"I know what you only." She cleaned the bell with her thumb, set it in her coat against the note from Ranu, and buttoned the flap over both. Her hands shook so that the button took three tries. "He wanted to retire. After this one. He said it every crossing for six winters."

Rohan said nothing, and she was grateful, a little, in a hard, ungenerous way.

She made herself get up. First she put her hands into her pack, under the rope and the sattu, to the bottom, where the oilcloth roll lay as heavy and square as a loaf. She had pulled it from the fleece under the goat straps at the last halt, because she trusted no animal on a bad slope. Now she closed her fingers on the folds. Three rows of four. Twelve packets, dry. She did not look at Rohan while she did it.

The redbark was safe. Jassi was dead. She stood in the blue dusk with her brother's medicine in one hand and an old man's bell against her chest, and she could not make the two numbers come out the same.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Your mountain," said Rohan.

He had lowered himself onto a rock, and the way he did it, one slow inch at a time, told her what the slide had done. Grey showed round his mouth.

"Ribs?" she said.

"Some. I have had worse."

"Breathe in."

He did. It stopped halfway, and he waited, and let it out in small pieces.

"Two cracked at least," she said. "Maybe three. Don't lift your left arm over your shoulder."

"I will not."

"You'll try."

"Yes." The corner of his mouth moved, and it hurt him, so he stopped. "Your mountain, Guide. I am a captain on someone else's ground, and I have lost my lieutenant, and the line behind us is under forty feet of snow." He nodded at the bowl, where the gully they had climbed was now a slope of white blocks with no mark of any path. "There is no way back that I can see. Give the orders. I will follow them."

She looked at him, waiting for the catch, the clause, the warden's hook under it. She found none.

"Leave your spear," she said.

He unslung it and laid it on the snow.

"Leave the tent poles. They're two pounds of nothing."

He did.

"Walk in my steps. Not near them, in them. Where I put a foot, you put a foot, and if I stop you stop and don't ask why."

"Understood."

"And you stay behind me. Not at my heels. Where I told you, this morning."

"Where you told me," he said, "I would have been under the snow with the rest."

She had no answer to that. "No talking until the rock band. Wind carries it and I want to hear the slope."

He nodded once and got to his feet by the long way round, with a small hiss through his teeth. He took each order the way a man takes a wage, quietly and in full, all the way up the last of the gully. She kept glancing back for the catch. It never came.

The rock band came and went. Beyond it the ground flattened, and the wind changed, and at a turn of the ridge, with the stars coming out like salt on slate, the cairn stood waiting.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It was a chest-high ring of flat grey stones, no wider than a threshing floor. A low note ran through it, the hum of the wind in its cracks, the sound the spirits made when they were listening.

Meera stepped over the lowest course and felt the hum fold round her ankles. Rohan stopped at the edge.

"One rope, one pair, until meltwater runs at this cairn," he said slowly, in the voice of a man quoting a text. "Our post archive says as much. It is thin on the rest."

"The rest is short." She unslung the rope. "Behind us the approach is buried. In front of us is the pass, and the Long Saddle in winter is a lie without a line to follow. Nobody crosses alone. A single person loses the path inside an hour, and then the mountain takes them." She let out the red coil in her hands, eight arm-spans of madder-dyed wool, soft as a sleeve. "It takes a pair, with this. Both ends. Both names."

Rohan looked at the rope. He did not look like a man who loved it. He looked like a man who had hanged people for crossing on one.

"If I refuse, I stand here alone."

"You stand here till the cold finishes you, and I lose the line inside the hour. Two dead instead of none." She said it flatly because it was the truth. Not a threat, a count. "I'm not asking you to like it. It's the only road with both of us alive at the end."

"And the medicine in your pack?"

She stopped.

The question was quiet, light as a cough. For a moment she heard only the hum. Then her mind ran the sum fast and cold. He knew. He had said it with her pack on her back and the whole mountain empty around them, and he had not seized it, not in the yard and not on the slope, where he could have taken it from a woman too tired to fight. A warden who knew and did nothing wanted something, or had already chosen. She could not tell which.

Answering would tell him what it was for. Silence told him she had heard. Either way he knew what she carried, and she knew he knew.

Silence was free. She had learned that at six winters old, from a mother who kept the family's every secret in her teeth. She took it. What it cost was the chance to learn his plan. He had laid the question down like a coin on a table and waited, and by saying nothing she had left the coin unturned. She still did not know what he meant to do with what he knew.

"Give me your left wrist," she said.

He held it out, bare, the glove stripped off. The pulse jumped at the thumb. She looped the red wool twice and tied the knot Bindu sang. Two over, one under, the third one tells the truth. The wool was already warm from her hands.

"Now yours," she said.

His fingers were steady, despite the ribs. He tied the knot precisely, slowly, with the look of someone writing a signature. His thumb brushed the inside of her wrist when he pulled it snug, and neither of them moved.

"The name and where you're going," she said. "To the stones. Say it as if you mean it."

She went first, because she had to. She faced the ring and spoke into the hum.

"Meera. Ranu."

Rohan turned toward the stones.

"Rohan," he said. "Garh Neshar."

For a breath nothing happened. Then the low hum rose through the cairn, climbing, until it tightened into a clear singing note. Pale light began to seep from between the stones, thin and blue as ice at the edge of a lake. It lifted off the cairn in threads. It ran out from the ring along the ground, a faint line of cold fire, and where it went the snow showed a path she had only seen in the dark of her own head.

The winter line. The mountain's road. It stretched out ahead of them, bright, and went up.

Rohan stared at it. He had gone very still.

"I have crossed by writ before, with the post's paired guides," he said. "The line showed a lamp's throw at best. I have never seen it run so far ahead, so fast."

"Paid guides tie for the fee," said Meera. "The mountain can tell."

He turned to look at her. In the blue light the lines of his face were hard and tired, and the bruise along his jaw had already started to come up. He was the man who hunted the Salt Fox, and she was the Salt Fox. He would hang her at the thaw if he ever put the two together. She was on a rope with him, and she owed him her life, and the rope would not let her step beyond eight arm-spans from his side.

"Why Ranu?" he said. "You gave the stones that name. I want to know why."

She could have said nothing. It was free. She could have said *I'm a guide, and a guide takes the work that's offered*, and let the cairn listen for a lie and ring.

She held the bell through her coat, a hard small lump over her heart.

"Because my brother is dying there," she said.

The hum dropped half a note, the way a bowl's song falls when a wet finger leaves the rim. The stones had heard a true thing. But a warden could not read cairn-song, and the stones never said who had spoken. The dip might have come from either of them, and he would have to take it on her word. She glanced down at her own fingertips, still pink in the blue light. They were the only proof he could read.

Rohan did not look at her hands. He did not reach for his notebook or his writ or the wrist she had tied. He turned his face up the lit line, where it climbed toward the saddle, and looked at it a long moment.

"Then we go," he said.

He stepped past her onto the path, and set his boot down exactly in her footprint, unasked.

Meera went after him, then stopped, because the line was hers to lead. She took the front and climbed, and behind her his steps came, one in each of hers. Her lips began to move. Twelve. Thirteen. She went on until she reached a number she had no word to put beside, and her lips went still.

# Chapter 4: Watch My Hands

"There are three rules," Meera said. "Count them, because I won't say them twice."

She walked ahead along the winter line with the red slack looped between them. The blue light ran at her heels like a stream, and it lit nothing else. Rohan kept his boots in her prints. Every third step his left side told him what the slide had done to it.

"One is distance," she said. "Eight arm-spans of rope. The rope is the measure, whether it's on your wrist or in my pack. Go past it and the heat leaves you fast. Both of us, at once. Go twice as far and the line goes out, and the mountain keeps whoever is standing there."

"And if the rope is untied?"

"Untie it to sleep, to wash, to climb. The distance still holds. Cut it, burn it, it makes no difference. It lasts until meltwater runs at the cairn where we tied it."

"The thaw."

"The thaw." She did not turn. "Two is lies. Any lie you tell the one on the other end of this costs you heat. A lie is something you believe is false, said so that you'll be believed. The size is how much the truth would change what I do next."

"And the price?"

"Small, your fingertips go white for an hour. Middling, frostnip and a night of shaking. Great, you lose what freezes." She stopped there. He noted where. "Fire won't warm it. Only time does."

"A lie about life or death?"

"Kills the liar. And a promise made on this rope is a contract. Break it while we're bound and it strikes you like a great lie." She said it as flatly as she had said the odds on the gully. "Three is what's free. Silence. Refusing to answer. A joke we both know is a joke. Anything you say to someone who isn't on this rope. And the truth, which is the cheapest thing on the mountain and the one nobody wants to buy."

Rohan walked a while. Under his boots the snow squeaked, dry and fine.

"I would like to see it," he said.

"You would." She stopped and faced him. "You don't trust a rule till you've seen the bill."

She pulled off her right glove with her teeth and held the hand out into the blue light, palm up. It was a worker's hand, scarred across the knuckles and cracked at the thumb. The fingertips were pink.

"Jassi hated those goats," she said.

Rohan had heard the old man in the yard that morning, cursing every animal he loaded. He had no way to know what lay beneath the curses. For the space of a breath he believed it, which was what the sentence was built to do.

Then the white came. It rose from the tips of her fingers like frost on a window and took the nails, bloodless as candle wax. She watched it climb with a face that gave him nothing.

A moment later her face gave him something. Her mouth went crooked, and she looked away up the line.

"He named every one," she said. "Sheriff. Magistrate. Judge. He talked to them like a bench and then complained when they wouldn't mind him."

Rohan, who had sat on benches, said nothing.

"That's a small one," she said, her voice almost level. "An hour. You'll see the white go. Then you'll know I'm not showing you a trick."

"Why that lie?"

"Because you'd believe it. It has to be believed or it isn't one."

"I am sorry for him."

"Don't be sorry. Carry something." She pulled the glove back on over the white. "You've been breathing like a leaking bellows since the rock band. If you're going to be of any use, be of use."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She stopped on a flat of wind-packed snow and unslung the pack. Rohan lowered himself to one knee, a thing his ribs allowed in stages, and took the sattu, the spare liners, and the coil of thin line. Those she let him have. Then she dug to the bottom for the oilcloth roll and her hands slowed.

"Give it here," he said. "My legs are sound, and the right arm is the better one."

"It's mine."

"It is a third of the weight in this pack, Guide. I am not asking whose."

She held it a moment longer and then let it go. It was heavier than it looked, and a corner of the oilcloth gave under his thumb. He did not mean to open it. The fold came away on its own, and the blue light fell across what lay inside.

Three rows of four. Small packets, waxed paper tied with thread, each the size of a man's palm. A rust-red dust had worked through one seam. The smell was bitter and resinous. It was the smell on the grain he had crushed between his fingers in the salt-house yard.

Twelve. He counted without wanting to.

"What is this?" he said, though he knew.

"Medicine." She did not hesitate, and he watched her hands as she said it. The fingertips were already white, and he saw what that meant. For this hour they could not testify to anything. She could say what she liked, and her body would keep no record.

He noted that she had to know it. He noted that she answered anyway.

"Redbark. For the grey cough, in Ranu," she said. "Forty-one sick there. Nine of them under ten. Six already buried. That's from a note I carry, and I've no reason to doubt the man who wrote it." She met his eyes. "They're not for sale, and not for the Guild. And they are not for you. I will walk off this mountain before you take them."

"At eight arm-spans, I would be walking with you."

"I've done the arithmetic."

It was nearly a joke. Perhaps it was one. She did not smile.

"The law is plain," Rohan said. His voice came out as it did in council, which he disliked. "A load carried over a closed pass is seized where it is found. The carrier answers to the rope. At the thaw, if I am to be exact. I will not say otherwise to you because you can hear me say it."

"Then arrest me at the thaw, Captain." Her chin lifted. "Today you carry half."

She was right. That was the part he could not get around. He had a lapis seal in his coat, a deadline, and a broken side, and the only road to the first lay through a woman who wanted the second dead. He could hold the law up in front of her like a lamp. It would not carry him one step along the line.

He had also seen the gully go. He did not intend to forget what it had taken, or how the first order of the night had come from her mouth and the second from his.

"Are you the Salt Fox?" he said.

Her face did not change. The rope lay slack on the snow. She stood in the blue light, perfectly still, and said nothing at all.

It cost her nothing. That was the thing. Silence was free, she had told him so herself, and he had just been given a demonstration of how free.

"Silence is an answer, Guide," Rohan said. "Watch my hands, and I will watch yours."

He meant it to land as a threat, and it did. She had the grace to let him see it hit.

"Mine are white," she said.

"For the hour."

"Then you've an hour's grace." She turned her back on him and shouldered the empty pack. "Count the packets into your coat, Captain. I'll show you where the seam is dry."

Rohan counted six into the inside of his coat, below the collar where the queen's seal lay sewn in its seam, and he did not look at her while he did it. He was a warden holding contraband. That was evidence, in custody, carried in the open by the proper officer, chain unbroken. He built that story with great care and could see through it at once.

The truth stood plainer. The Closing Law gave no word for a man who carried a smuggler's medicine across a closed pass for her. It had one word for a man who sheltered, fed or aided an unlawful crosser, and the rope for that word was the same length as hers.

He had walked into it with his eyes open and a broken side. He did not feel noble about it. He felt the way a man feels at the edge of a frozen river, having decided the ice will hold.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They went on in the blue light. The wind came in long, patient pushes off the Saddle, and the ridge on their left stood against the stars like a row of broken teeth.

"I'll bet you," Meera said, "that you can't keep your mouth shut for an hour."

It was the first thing she had said in some time. Rohan said nothing, which was his answer.

"My mouth is generally shut, you'll say."

He said nothing.

"Name the stake, then. I'll name it. The sattu at the next halt. Winner eats the loser's share."

He nodded once.

"That's a yes. That counts as a yes." She seemed pleased to have caught him at it. "Your hour starts when I say, and I say now."

He walked.

She began with the weather, and the state of his boots, and the vanity of lowland cloth. She counted aloud in fours and fives, and then in a language of her own, a sing-song of knot-numbers that ended on a rising note as if it were a question. She asked what a warden did for supper. She asked whether he'd ever been laughed at. She asked, with some skill, whether it was true that wardens' wives only married men they could outrank in the dark. The snow squeaked, and Rohan gave her nothing.

Three winters he had spent not answering the questions of smugglers' wives and the families of the hanged. He had stood in the doorways of widows. A woman talking at him on a mountain, with the wind for company, was nearly restful.

At the start, the fingers of her right glove had no colour in them. By the middle of the hour she took the hand out and flexed it, and he saw the nails had gone from wax to the pale of a tea-rose. The white was leaving.

She talked on. After a time she stopped asking things and only talked, about the knots Bindu sang, and the way goats looked at weather, and a lowland middleman who sold her bad lamp oil. Her voice had a drive in it. It was not quite a joke, and the jokes had thinned out of it a while ago.

Somewhere past the middle of the hour, he understood that she was not playing to win. She was keeping her mouth full of noise so that the quiet could not get in. He did not stop her. It seemed to him a poor use of his silence to spend it on something she needed more.

When her fingertips were pink again, as plain as the day she was born, he said, "The hour is done."

She stared at him. Then she stared at her own hand, and then at the sky, as if the stars might be called to appeal.

"You could have *twitched*," she said.

"I am not given to it."

"I called you a stone with a hat on."

"You did. It was well put."

"I hate this." She shook the red slack in disgust. "That's three hundred winters of rope-craft and I lose the first wager to a man who'd rather be a post."

"The sattu, Guide."

"Yours is on the way," she said between her teeth, and he saw her struggle with the shape of a smile and lose. He had won a ration and a small, ridiculous victory, and for some reason it did not feel like either.

"I will collect it at the halt," he said, with very great gravity.

She made a sound that might have been laughter if it had not been shaken with something else. She turned her face from him, and he let her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Near the end of the night the line brought them to a waystone.

It stood at the foot of the climb to the Long Saddle, a squat grey pillar as tall as a child, furred with lichen on its north side. At its base lay a shelf of flat stones left by earlier crossers. Beyond it the ground rose, white and long, toward a sky going from black to the colour of iron.

Meera slowed. The rope between them drew, and then slackened as he came up beside her.

She did not speak. She went down on her heels at the foot of the waystone and began to choose stones. She took three off the shelf, each flat as a hand, and set the first on the packed snow in front of her. She set the second across it. She worked as he had seen her work on the ice, exact and without wasted motion, and he realised she was building something that would have to stand.

"Forty winters," she said to the stones. "Ten of them with me on the Saddle. Seven goats in his time, and the last five of them the best." She laid the third stone on the pile. Her voice was nearly steady. "He said he was retiring."

That was all. She did not weep. She took the dented bell out of her coat, looked at it, and put it back.

Rohan stood a pace behind her. He had known the man two days. He had stood in the yard and heard him say *no* to the Crown's captain with a hay fork in his hand, and he had seen him laugh once, over a goat. He had not liked him particularly, and the loss ran through him all the same, the plain cold fact of a man who had been walking in the dark and was not.

Dilan had no cairn. They had brought what the ice gave up to the post and buried it in a wardens' row, and there was no mountain stone over it. There was a rope end in the lining of Rohan's coat, cut clean, that he had never put down.

He peeled the glove from his right hand.

The cold took his fingers at once, the wind getting into every joint. He chose a stone from the shelf, a small grey one worn smooth by someone else's hand, and bent over her shoulder, ribs and all, and set it on the top.

It sat. He did not take his hand away at once.

Meera did not look up. She was watching his fingers where they rested on the stone, bare in the thin light. They were reddening, and they were steady.

He let her.

# Chapter 5: Two Brothers in Arms

The carving was on the waystone's east face, and Meera found it because she was looking for somewhere to put her hand.

Her knees had gone loose after the third stone of Jassi's cairn. She reached out to the pillar to stand, and her glove slid over a groove, then another. She wiped the lichen with her sleeve. The dawn was grey and dull, with no blue left in it.

Two hands. Worn nearly smooth by three hundred winters of wind, but she knew them. One rope went around both wrists in a figure of eight, and the knot sat between them like a third thing.

She had seen the same carving on the inner face of the Shilak Cairn since she was a child. She had never seen it on a waystone.

Under her palm the stone gave off a faint hum, the sound of wind in a hollow. Old cairns did that near a bound pair. It was one more thing the crews knew and nobody had written down. The stones hummed when a pair lay near them, and they hummed higher when one of the pair lied. They never said which one.

"Captain," she said. "Come here and tell me I'm tired."

He came, slowly, a hand at his side. He stood over her shoulder and she heard him breathe in short, careful sips. He was silent so long she looked up.

"'Two brothers in arms,'" said Rohan, in the voice he used for texts. "'They crossed the closed pass bound by one rope, and the pass has stood open to the faithful ever since.' It is in the first chronicle of the Shilak post. Every post child learns it."

"Brothers."

"In arms. A phrase of the time. It means comrades of the sword."

"Then the crews have it wrong." She sat back on her heels. "We tell it as a warden and a smuggler. One of each, tied together because neither of them could do it alone. My grandmother told it that way. Bindu sings it that way."

"A smuggler's song."

"A warden's book." She waved her glove at the carving. "Both of them written by people who weren't there."

"Mine was written at the time."

"By the crown, which wanted two brothers and a sword. Mine was kept by the people who needed it to be true." She heard herself and nearly smiled. "You've no more proof than I have."

Rohan crouched, an inch at a time, and studied the rope in the stone. "The hands are worn too smooth to tell apart," he said. "No rank. No sword." He moved his glove along the lower hand. "This one has a cut across the knuckles. Or the stone does."

"Or the carver did it for a reason."

"Or the weather did."

"Or it's a smuggler's scar," she said, "and a warden's book left it out."

"A fine theory, with no evidence."

"And yours has a chronicle and no hands." She looked at the carving, and then, to her annoyance, at him. "If it were your book, would you want the first crossing to be a crime?"

He did not answer at once. Silence was free, and he had made good use of it all night.

"No," he said at last. "I would not."

"Then we're both telling it the way we need it." She got up. "Neither of us can prove a thing. I'll take that over a lie."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"The pitch," said Rohan, when she was looking for a lee. "Before we sleep I should like to see the first of the climb. If it is scoured, we move at dusk. If it is corniced, we must plan."

"It's scoured. Wind takes this slope first."

"You spoke of the rock band. The pitch above it is the part I cannot judge from here."

She had mentioned the band once, hours ago, in passing. She suspected he had simply listened to everything she said and kept it. "Look from here."

"The shoulder hides it." He was already moving. "Twenty paces. I will be back before you have found a hollow."

They had untied the wrist knots at the stone, as the rope allowed, and coiled the red length between them. He took his end up in his right hand, looping it through his belt, as a warden would. She had her back to him, scraping the shelf for dung cakes. She counted, because she counted everything. Six cakes. Seven.

"Guide."

His voice had changed.

She turned. He had gone up the slope to where the ridge bent, and he stood very straight, as men do when they are working not to fall. The rope ran from his belt out across the snow and lay taut as a bowstring in front of her boots. He had walked it out to the last span, and then one step more, because the shoulder had not shown him what he wanted.

The cold arrived in her chest before she understood it. It went into her like water through a seam. Her teeth ached. Her fingers lost their heat, and the breath left her mouth in one long smoke. She had felt it once before, fourteen winters back, the winter she was fifteen, on her first bound crossing with her mother. She had gone back for a dropped mitten, a few paces past the rope's length, and her mother had screamed.

"Back," she said. "Captain, now."

"I am attempting—"

"You're not attempting anything."

She dropped the dung and took the rope in both hands. She leaned back and hauled, hand over hand, the red wool running wet and heavy through her gloves, and he came down the slope at her pace, not his. He lost his footing once. The rope bit her palms. She hauled him the last three paces like a sack onto a bank, and when he was within a span of her, and then a step, the cold went out of her as if a door had been shut on it.

Bent over with her hands on her knees, she shook.

"I did not know," Rohan said. His face was pale and wet. "It was nowhere near—"

"It was eight spans. I told you eight." She straightened. "You wanted to see for yourself. That's what you do. That's all right on the plain, where nothing minds if you're wrong. Here it'll cost you your feet."

"It cost you too."

"Yes."

"I am sorry."

"Don't be sorry." She wound the slack round her fist. It took three tries, because her hand was not steady. "Listen. You walk where the rope lets you. If you need to see something, you ask me and I'll tell you or I'll go. Your body's not yours to spend on a look, Captain. It's mine. It's mine to keep alive until the thaw, and you're not going to make that harder."

He did not look away. In the grey light his eyes were the colour of wet slate, and they did not drop.

"Yours," he said, "and mine to keep in turn, I suppose."

She had not thought of that. She opened her mouth and nothing came out.

"I will ask," said Rohan.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The shelf at the waystone held more than stones. There was a nest of old dung cakes under the lowest slab, the kind goat-crews left for the next crossers, dry as bread and grey with age. Meera knew it was there. Every guide on the pass did. It was the oldest law of the mountain, older than the crown's, and no one had ever written it down.

She built the fire in the lee of the pillar on a bed of flat stones, with a ring of snow blocks to turn the wind. Seven cakes, a little juniper bark from her pocket, a spark from the flint. The smoke rose thin and sour. The flame took hold, small and orange, and she held her hands to it and nearly cried at how little it was.

Rohan stood a good way off with his arms crossed.

"This is harbouring," he said.

"Mm."

"A fire shared with an unlawful crosser. By the letter of the Closing Law, I am to report you, seize your goods and carry you to the post. The penalty for a warden who fails to do so is the same as the penalty for you."

"Yes."

"I wish it understood that I know." He looked at the flame, not at her. "The packets I could still call evidence in custody. It was a poor story, but it was a story. A shared fire has none. There is nothing I could tell the post."

"Then it's understood." She fed in a cake. "Your wish is noted. You're letting the heat out."

He stood a moment longer, in his torn coat, with the queen's seal sewn in his collar and six packets of redbark in his pocket. Then he sat down at her fire, by the long way round, and held out his right hand to the flame.

He did not say anything else. Neither did she. A man who had hanged people for this should be allowed a minute.

She melted snow in the tin cup. She mixed the sattu in two portions, set one in front of him, then pushed the other across as well.

"What is this?"

"Your wager. Winner eats the loser's share." She nudged the cup closer. "I'm to go hungry. Eat it."

"I would not take a woman's ration."

"It's a bet, Captain. A bet's the only thing on this mountain I don't resent paying."

He looked at her for a long time. Then he took the second cup, and with some gravity, drank half of it and handed the rest back.

"The winning is enough," he said. "The surplus I decline."

She took it before he could change his mind and was ashamed of how good it tasted.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They slept, in turns, wedged against the stone. By late afternoon the light had thickened. The wind fell, and from between the courses of the waystone a thread of blue began to rise, faint as breath on glass. The line would come back with the dark.

Meera woke with her face against the pillar and a stone's rim pressing her cheek. The fire was down to a glow. Across it sat Rohan, awake, with his legs folded under him and his left shoulder held very still.

She watched him breathe.

The right side of his chest moved. The left barely did, taking short, cautious pulls that stopped halfway, as though a hand were pressing down on the top of them. He took one now and his jaw worked before he let it go.

"You're favouring your left," she said.

"I have a stiff side."

"You have cracked ribs."

"You said it yourself last night. Two, perhaps three." He lifted his chin. "I have had worse. There is nothing to be done for ribs but wait."

"You're breathing like a man listening for footsteps."

"I am a warden."

She let that go. It was a dodge, and dodging cost nothing, so she had no right to complain. She fed the last cake into the fire.

"A question for a question," she said. "Since we're stuck with each other. I ask one, you ask one. Anything you won't answer, you say so, and we both go on. No cost."

He considered. "The rope would not charge us for it."

"It's an old trade. The crews do it on long nights. You'd be surprised what comes out when you're allowed to say no."

"Very well." He settled his hand on his knee. "You first."

She'd had the question since the cairn.

"What's at Garh Neshar?"

"I decline."

"That fast?"

"It is not mine to say." His face was steady. "I will tell you that it matters to more than me. I will tell you that I would tell you if I could. Both are true, and you may watch my hands." He turned them palm up toward the glow. They were red from the fire and nothing else. "My turn."

She waited.

"How many people have you carried across?"

She had expected something about the Salt Fox. This was worse, because it was a count, and she kept a count, and her whole body knew the number.

Refusing would have cost her nothing. He had done it himself, not a minute before.

"Two hundred and six," she said. "Not counting salt."

He did not move.

"That's evidence, Captain. You know that."

"I carry no ink," said Rohan. "And the stones heard you, not me. Is the number correct?"

"Last I counted." She stared into the coals. "I wrote it down. Every one."

"You wrote it down."

"I keep ledgers. You'll laugh."

"I am not laughing."

He wasn't. That surprised her more than anything he had said. She had told a warden she kept books on her crimes, and he looked at her as if she had told him something about herself.

"My turn," she said. "Why do you hunt the crew?"

She thought he would refuse again. She thought she saw him weigh it. The fire popped, and a spark rose up and went out against the dark.

"A warden died on this pass two winters ago," said Rohan.

The words were set down plain, with no weight added.

His quarry was her crew. She heard it as plainly as a rope parting, and dread sank low in her stomach and stayed there.

"He was young. He was chasing a smuggler across the ice in a storm. The report said what the report says. I read it, and I signed it, and I put his name in the book." His hand was closed on his knee, and she saw the knuckles. "I hunted your crew one winter because the law said so. I have hunted it two more for him."

"One for the law," Meera said slowly. "Two for him."

"Yes."

She turned the sum over. Three winters of hunting, and a boy dead for only two of them. A man shaping a lie would have rounded it, made it tidy, given himself the grief from the start. Rohan had given her the gap and let it sit there, to his own discredit.

Something cold touched the back of her neck and was gone. It was the wind. There were many things in two winters. It had been a long one, and she had buried a neighbour in it, and Kiran had come in with an ear that would not heal and a story about a bad crossing. She put the thought down, because she did not have the strength to carry it and the redbark both.

"I'm sorry for him," she said. It was true, and she waited for nothing to happen, and nothing did.

"Thank you." He said it as a man says it when he is not used to being offered the thing. "My turn again, or yours?"

"Mine." She looked at his side. She did not decorate it. "Are your ribs hurt, Captain? Plainly. If one's through, we sit here a day and you lose your Fair. If they're only cracked, we climb at dusk and I bind them. I need the true size of it to choose."

His right hand began to turn over on his knee, palm up, the way it had for Garh Neshar. It stopped halfway.

She watched him do her sum. A refusal now would be an answer, with her price laid out in front of it. A lost day. A lost Fair. Declining would tell her everything he wanted kept.

"It's nothing," he said.

The waystone hummed.

It had been a low sound all day, the sound of wind in a hollow. Now it climbed, a note at a time, as if someone were drawing a bow slowly across it. The blue thread between the courses thickened into light and ran down the pillar to the snow, and the whole fire-ring went the colour of ice.

Rohan did not seem to hear. He reached for his glove on the stone beside him, a plain thing, a man getting ready to travel. He worked it onto his right hand, finger by finger, with the patience of someone whose body is already a long way from comfortable.

Under the cairn-light his fingertips went.

They went white from the nails inward, as hers had, a slow pale creeping up like milk drawn along a wick, and the wool of the glove closed over them before he saw. He tugged the cuff down and stood.

"It will be dark soon," he said. "We should go."

Meera did not answer. She sat by the dying fire with the red rope coiled in her lap. She had seen the white pass the first joint of every finger, and it was the gloved hand she could no longer check. So she counted his breaths instead, short and stopped halfway down.

One. Two. She got to six before he turned his back.

# Chapter 6: Taking the Cold

"Are your ribs broken, Captain? Not hurt. Broken."

Meera asked it without turning round, the way she called out a distance. She was three paces up the pitch with the red slack looped between them, and she had stopped to wait for him for the fourth time since midnight.

Rohan had counted the stops. He had also counted the hours of the night they had left, and the days of the twenty, and he had come to a figure he did not like.

The slope above the waystone was scoured to blue ice in places and drifted in others. The blue line had died with the first grey in the east. His left side felt as if a hand were closing slowly on a wet cloth. He had not raised the arm above his shoulder since the slide, and he had gone to some trouble to make that look like choice.

"I saw your fingers last night," she said. "The white. I counted to six and let it go. A small one, I thought, and he's a proud man, and it's no business of mine." She turned then. Her face was raw with wind. "This is my business. If you slow on the Saddle, we lose the day, and Ranu hasn't got days. So I'm asking plainly."

The truth would cost him. She would halt them. She would build a shelter and sit him in it. She would take a day, perhaps two, and he had sixteen left and a regent counting them for him.

He weighed it as he weighed everything. A middling lie would bring frostnip and a night of shaking. He had been cold before. A lost day was a day of the grey cough in Ranu, and that was a debt he could not carry. His hands could pay for the first. Nothing he owned could pay for the second. It was a poor sum, and he knew it, and he did it anyway.

"They are fine," said Rohan.

It was the plainest sentence he had spoken since the cairn, and it was false in every word.

The lie landed. A bite went into his right hand, along the knuckles, as if he had plunged it into a stream in thaw. He looked down. The grey was spreading under the skin, from the second joint toward the wrist, bloodless and patchy. The glove had not hidden it, because the cold was under the glove.

He closed the hand. It did not want to close.

Meera watched him do it. She did not say a word. She pulled off her right glove with her teeth, as she had done for her demonstration, and held out her hand.

"Give me your hand."

"Guide—"

"The right one. Take the glove off."

He did not know what she meant to do. The rules of the rope were hers, and she had recited them, and this had not been one. A man who has just been caught does not argue about terms. He peeled the glove away and put his hand in hers.

Her palm was warm. That was all he understood, for the space of a breath. A rough, dry palm with a callus at the base of the thumb, closing over the back of his hand so that her fingers lay along the grey.

Then the cold left him.

It went out through his skin like a tide drawn off a flat shore. The ache in his knuckles thinned and ran to nothing. Colour came back into the hand in a rush of pins. He saw it go from him into her. It showed as a rime on her fingers, a pallor that crept past her wrist and whitened the nails one by one.

Her face did not change, but her breath did. It went short and flat.

"Middling," she said. "That was a middling lie, Captain."

"What have you done?"

"The loophole." Her teeth had begun to chatter, and she clamped them and spoke through them. "The one who's lied to can take it. Skin to skin. It passes over. The liar keeps their hands, and the other one pays." She let go of him. Her fingers shook so hard that she tucked them into her armpit, and the shaking went on in her shoulders. "I know it was a lie. I know how big. I don't know the truth of it. That's the price of taking it, you see. You can't spare someone and not know they lied."

He stood with his restored hand open in front of him. He had spent three winters hunting smugglers, and two had hanged from the deodar. None of them had ever done him a kindness in return.

"You did not have to," he said.

"No. I could've let you carry it. You'd have had frostnip to the wrist and a night of the shakes, and I'd have had a captain who couldn't hold an axe." She was blowing on her hands, which did nothing. "I'm not being good. I'm being practical."

"You are doing both."

"Don't," said Meera. "Listen. This is how it goes now. We go slow, and you tell me true, or I take it every time. I will take it every time, Rohan. So don't make me."

It was the first time she had used his name. He doubted she had noticed. She was too cold to notice anything.

"Now," she said. "The question again."

He understood that she meant him to answer it properly.

"Two ribs are cracked," he said. He said it to the snow first, and then he said it again to her. "The seventh and the eighth, on the left. The slide did it. I cannot take a full breath, and I cannot raise the left arm above the shoulder. I have been hiding both since the cairn. I have stopped on the pitch more than you have seen, because I stop when you are not looking."

Nothing happened to his hands. He watched them, and so did she. The wind moved in the loose cuff of his coat, and the fingers stayed the colour of fingers.

"There," said Meera. Her eyes shut, briefly. "That's a bill I don't mind not paying."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She bound him in the lee of a boulder where the wind could not get at his chest.
