She had to do it with her hands shaking, and he offered to do it himself, which she declined with a look. From the bottom of the pack she pulled a coil of braided goat hair, grey and soft with use, and for a moment she only held it.

"Jassi's spare," she said. "He tethered the kids with it. He'd never go anywhere without a spare." She shook out a length. "Coat open. Tunic stays on. Lift your arms as far as they go."

He lifted the right. The left went to his shoulder and stopped. She said nothing of it. She slid the cord around his back, under the arm, and wound it round his ribs from below the cracked ones to above, crossing it flat across his spine so that there would be no knot to press on him.

"Breathe out," she said.

He did.

"Out more."

The cord drew in. The ends of the bone, which had ground on each breath since the gully, stopped grinding. A band of steadiness settled round him, snug enough to be felt and not so tight that it hurt. He took a short, shallow pull of air and found the top of it was no longer a knife.

"You have done this before," he said.

"Twice. Both men." She tucked the end under the last turn. "You'll breathe small whichever way. This way the ends don't grate on each other. Tell me if it pinches. Tell me *true*."

"It does not pinch."

"Good."

He did up the coat's buttons one-handed. She was already moving ahead on the slope, the red rope paying out between them, and she had slowed her pace by a third and made nothing of it. She had not looked at him the whole while she wound the cord. Her ears were red, which was not the wind.

He thought of that for a long time afterward.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They made the hollow by midmorning, a wind-cut bowl under the lip of the Saddle where the snow lay as hard as old bread. Meera cut blocks with the axe and laid a half-ring of them as she had at the waystone. Rohan hauled blocks with his right arm until she told him to sit down.

The day went in sleep and small chores. By dusk her hands had come back to a dull pink, but the shaking had not stopped. When the dark thickened and the blue line rose along the edge of the bowl, she was sitting with her knees drawn up and her whole body going like a sail in a squall.

"Frostnip and a night of shaking," she said, when he looked. "I know the rate. I'll live." Her teeth clicked on the last word.

He fed the fire. There were four dung cakes and a handful of juniper bark, and he set them as she had taught him, and they burned bright and honest. He pushed her closer to the coals. She held her palms to them and stared without expression.

"It won't help," she said. "Fire doesn't touch it. I'd tell you if it did."

He knew that. She had told him. He sat anyway, helpless as a man at a bedside, and watched the flames lick at the cold and the cold not give way.

Under his coat, rolled at his belt, was the grey pattu he had carried since the post. It was warden's issue, thick, woven close, and it was the only warm thing in the bowl that was not on fire. He had not touched it when the tents were lost, because he had been raised not to take warmth before the people in his charge had theirs.

He unrolled it and held it out.

"No," said Meera.

"It is wool, Guide. It does not say a great deal."

"You've cracked ribs. You need it." Her chin shook as she said it. "I'm the one who was fool enough to—"

"You took my cold, and you did not have to." He kept his voice level, as he did in council, and it came out less steady than he meant. "It is only a shawl. I am warm enough. Watch my hands, and you will see it is so."

She looked at the pattu. She looked at his hands, which stayed as they were. In the firelight she looked younger than twenty-nine and a good deal more tired. She had buried a man at the foot of this climb the day before, and she had not once asked for anything.

She took it. She dragged it round her shoulders with both hands and bowed her head into the fold, and for a while she only shook.

"It smells of wardens," she said at last, muffled.

"Camphor. It is meant for the moth."

"Terrible."

"I am told it is."

She made a sound that was not quite a laugh, and the shawl moved with it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She slept near midnight, with the red rope slack across the snow between them. He sat up with the fire and did the sum.

It was a habit, and a poor one. He tallied what a thing cost, and who had paid it. He had done it all his life with fines and with men.

The day was gone. Fifteen of the twenty, then, and he had spent one on his own pride. That was the first line of the ledger, and it was the small one.

She had known the moment she held out her hand that she would learn he had lied to her. She had known she would pay for it. She had seen the lie in his face and weighed it as she weighed the odds on the gully.

She could have let him slow. It would have cost her nothing, if cost were measured in cold. She could have let him limp up the Saddle with frostnip and cracked bone, and put the blame where it belonged, and gone on with her sattu and her count. He had given her every reason. He was a warden. He was bound to arrest her at the thaw. He had seized her crew's goods twice, and he could not now remember the names of the families.

She had put her hand in the cold and held it there.

He turned his own hand over in the firelight and looked at it. The knuckles were whole. The fingertips were warm. They were warm because hers were not. He had priced his lie at a night of shaking, and she had paid the price, and the price had never been his to pay.

There was a thing he had never allowed himself to be, in all the years of the badge, and it was in debt. A warden gave and was owed. Rules kept the ledger straight. He did not know what rule covered this, and so he sat and watched the fire and let the figure stand where it was. He owed her, in a currency the Closing Law had no clerk for.

When the fire dropped, the shaking eased. She woke near the grey hour and lay with her eyes open, looking at the lip of the Saddle where the stars were going out.

"You didn't take it back," she said.

"The pattu? I am not cold."

She tested that, he saw. She waited, looking at his hands, and they stayed as they were. She seemed to relax by a degree.

"Rohan," said Meera.

He waited.

"Promise you won't lie to me again."

It came out small and plain, as if she had worn it smooth in her mouth. She was not asking as a guide, or as a smuggler, or as the woman who had bound his ribs. She asked like someone who had looked at the worst of a bill and was wondering whether it would come again.

He knew what a promise was on this rope. He had heard her say it among the rules, in the middle, where she kept the things she meant least to explain. A promise made while bound was a promise to the mountain. If it broke, the breaker was struck as by a great lie, flesh dead in the hands and feet.

He did not know that he could keep it. There would come a night, and he knew it as he knew the Saddle was there, when the true answer would hang her, and silence would cost more than silence could pay. He would not stand in that night with a promise round his neck and call it a vow. He had priced a lie once already, and the bill had gone to her.

"No," he said.

Her face went still.

"I will not promise it." He kept his hands open on his knees, where she could see them. "A promise is a rope, and I would be tying it to you with one end already frayed. I do not know what the mountain will ask of me. I do not know what you will ask. I will not hand you a word I may not be able to hold."

"That's not an answer."

"It is the only true one I have." He held her eyes. "I have not lied to you since the pitch. I'll show you. Watch my hands."

She did. The fire had burned down to a rind of red, and in its light his fingers lay still and steady and warm. She studied them for a long time, with the pattu pulled up to her chin and her own hands hidden in its folds.

She said nothing. It cost her nothing. He had never heard silence sound so much like a question.

# Chapter 7: Other Ropes

"Three hundred and eighteen," said Meera. "Three hundred and nineteen."

The snow squeaked under every boot, the high dry squeak that meant no thaw had touched it since autumn. The Long Saddle lay ahead like a sheet of beaten tin. Glare came up from it and in at the eyes, and by now the ache sat behind her face like a second skull.

"You are counting aloud," said Rohan behind her.

"It keeps the pace. Three hundred and twenty."

"And the number is?"

"Whatever the number is." She did not turn. "Three-twenty-one. Talk if you like. I won't answer till four hundred."

He did not talk. She listened to him instead, the careful sip of his breath inside the goat-hair binding, the long stride he shortened to match hers. At the three-hundred-and-sixtieth step the breathing changed. It went ragged, with a wet catch in it.

She stopped and looked back.

His eyes were streaming. He had them narrowed to cracks, and the lashes were clotted with ice. Tears ran down his face and froze at the jaw. He had not said a word.

"How long?"

"Since the hollow."

"You said nothing."

"I judged it a minor matter." He blinked, and winced. "It has become less minor."

"Snow-blind by noon, if you're lucky. By dark, you're led by the rope like a goat." She slung off her pack. "Writ case. Give it here."

That pulled him up straight. "I beg your pardon?"

"The leather one. On your belt." She held out her hand. "I've seen you touch it forty times. Don't tell me it's empty."

It was not empty. He undid the strap with his right hand and drew out a folded paper, thick and creamy. She saw red wax on the fold before he tucked it inside his coat against the binding. He passed her the case. It was good leather, boiled hard and dark with years of handling, and stamped with a goat.

"This is crown issue," Rohan said.

"It's a strip." She drew her knife. "Hold still."

She cut a band the width of two fingers, doubled it, and pierced two slits with the knife-point, each barely wider than a barley grain. She threaded a length of the red rope's spare wool through the ends and tied it behind his head with her left hand, because her right fingertips still tingled from yesterday. The leather smelled of ink and old oil. His hair was damp under her knuckles. She kept her eyes off his face while she worked.

"Better?"

He swung his head slowly across the white. "The world is a ribbon," he said. "It is a very good ribbon."

"You look like an owl in a bad temper."

"I have been called worse by wardens' wives." He touched the leather. "You have destroyed a writ case."

"I've improved it."

The corner of his mouth moved. It was nearly a smile, and then he put it away as though it might be seized.

She took up her pace again. Three hundred and sixty-two. Three hundred and sixty-three. She looked down the long scoured fall of the Saddle to where it broke into the first folds of the south slope, and there, a very long way below, something moved that was not the wind.

She stopped counting.

"Captain."

He came up beside her with the slits of the leather turned the same way. Down on the white, small as seeds on a sheet, four figures went in two short lines. Between the lines there was a gap of black rope, and between the pairs another.

"Wardens?" he said.

"Not wardens. Look at the ropes. Two pairs means two bindings at the Shilak Cairn. Four people stood in that ring and spoke their true names to the stones." She shaded her eyes with her mitten and counted them, then counted again. "Nobody does that in a closed winter for love. Someone paid them enough to do it."

"You can tell who?"

"Packs low. Covered in ochre canvas. There's one crew on the lower salt road dyes its packs with onion skin so they can find them in the dark." Her mouth went dry. "That's Asha's."

"You know the name."

"Everyone on the pass knows it. Asha runs salt and a little else, and she runs it below Shilak, where the wardens are lazy. She never crosses the Saddle. She says the mountain eats the cheap." Meera lowered her hand. "Unless someone's paying her more than the mountain costs."

"For what?"

"I was about to ask you."

He said nothing. He stood looking at the four small figures with the slits of leather angled down the slope, and it was a long time before he spoke.

"Four," he said. "Two pairs, spaced a rope and a half apart, so that if one pair falls the other is not dragged. No tent poles. No long packs. They carry what a person carries who means to be fast and not to sleep. They keep their tracks tight, in single file, and they stop on a count. Every few hundred paces they watch the ridge behind them."

"Looking for us."

"Looking for something ahead. Then they move up in bounds." He glanced down the slope. "Whoever trained them knew soldiers. That is a soldier's advance, done by people who have been paid to be soldiers for a week."

"They're closing."

"By a third since dawn, by my reckoning. Your slowing for me has cost that."

"It has," she said, "and I'd do it again." She started walking, fast. "Eight hundred paces to the lip. Then the ridge. After that I'll show you what I can do about it."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He waited until four hundred. She had said she would not answer till then, and he seemed to be a man who kept the terms of a bargain even when nobody was holding him to it.

"I carry something," said Rohan.

She kept walking. "I'd gathered."

"I told you at the waystone that what lies at Garh Neshar matters to more than me. It is not only a place I am going. It is a thing I am carrying." The slits of the goggles stayed fixed on the snow. "The regent's people in Neshar would pay a lordship for it. Land and title and a hall with a bell on it. I believe now that someone has already offered, and that those four on the slope have been told where to find me."

She stopped so quickly the rope went slack.

"What is it?"

"I decline."

"A lordship," said Meera. "For what, in your pocket? A map? A ledger?"

"I decline." He stood in the glare with his hands open at his sides. "It is free to say that. I would rather give you the free thing than the false one."

She looked at his fingers. They stayed the colour of fingers. A fresh breeze combed the snow between them and drew a thin white smoke across their boots.

Anger came clean and sharp, and she was surprised to find how little of it belonged to him. A man had said he could be bought for a hall and a bell, and he was not selling. He had told her he was hunted and by whom, and what the hunters would pay, and had held back only the one thing she could do nothing with.

"Fine," she said. "Keep your lordship."

"I do not want one."

"I know." She set off again. "That's what's so annoying."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The ridge came up out of the Saddle like a spine, a long fin of black rock the wind had stripped to the bone. Meera led him onto it with the snow squeaking behind them, and at the first bare stretch she stopped.

"Here the tracks end," she said. "There's no snow on this rock. They'll lose us for a quarter mile unless they've a tracker. And they'll have a tracker."

"Then they do not lose us."

"They lose us for long enough. Listen." She dug her axe into the crust and leaned on it. "Past the ridge there's a shelf on the south face. A crew shelf. Bindu's mother cut it forty winters ago, and every guide who ever needed to sit out a storm unfound has used it. From the Saddle, it's invisible. From the slope below, it's a dark line in the cornice that looks like a shadow. A bound pair can lie there while an army passes."

Rohan's head came round. "A crew shelf."

"Aye."

"A hiding place for smugglers."

"For anybody who's hiding."

"Guide." He said it slowly. "I would ask you something. I would ask you very plainly not to show me anything I would be required to report."

She had been waiting for him to ask it. She had not expected it in that voice, quiet and unhurried, with nothing officious in it.

"The shelf is a crime scene," Rohan said. "The pegs, the cut, the cache, if there is one: each is evidence of a continuing offence under the Closing Law. If I see it, I know it. If I know it, I am sworn to carry it to the post. I would not wish to be obliged to do that to you." He lifted the leather slits to her face. "You have lost a man already on my account. I do not wish you to lose a crew."

The pegs he named were iron, hammered into the rock by Bindu's mother with a stone hammer. The cache was three days of food and two dry blankets. Every guide in the crew had slept on that shelf one night or another, and a good half of them would hang if the shelf were counted. It was her crew's ledge, and she was the Salt Fox. If he ever set the one beside the other, the shelf was the noose with her name on it.

Her hand stopped on the axe haft. Then she looked at his, open at his sides, the fingers pink and whole, and she pulled the axe out of the crust.

The wind rattled in the crust below. Four small figures were climbing the slope.

"I keep asking who knew my road," Rohan said, low, as if to the wind. "It was chosen for me, and only a handful ever saw it. Yet there they are."

"Ask it later. How many hours do you think I've got?"

"Three. Perhaps four."

"If I take the high route to lose them, it's six. I'd leave you out in the open, snow-blind by dark, and the first time they gained the ridge they'd have us in sight." She had pulled her right mitten off to work the axe free, and she kept her bare hand wrapped on the haft. "If I use the shelf, I save the time, and they don't see us go in. That's all it is. Time. I don't care a fig for the shelf."

The cold took her at once. It bit the pads of her fingers where they lay on the wood, and they went white from the tips inward, a small lie's worth, plain as chalk.

"That is not true," said Rohan.

He was not looking at her face. The leather slits were tipped down at her hand, and he had read it as plainly as a page.

She shoved the mitten back on. "Fine. I care," she said. "I'm doing it anyway. Shut your eyes if you want. I'll walk you like a goat. But I'm not leaving a man to die so a peg stays secret."

"Meera."

"Eyes closed, Captain, or eyes open. You pick."

He was quiet. She watched him think about it. Then he did a thing she would remember for a long time afterward: he lifted the strip of leather off his face, shut his eyes, and put it back, so that there was nothing in the slits to see.

"I choose the first," said Rohan. "I shall see nothing. You will tell me where to put my feet, and I shall put them there."

"And when you're asked?"

"I shall say that I was blindfolded by a snow-glare, and that this is true." The corner of his mouth moved again. "It is a fact I can make good on."

For a second she could not say anything at all.

"Hand on the rope," she said finally. "Walk where I walk."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She read the snow as she went, because there was nothing else to read.

She told him the ridge was cold-hardened on the windward side and rotten on the lee. She told him the cornice beyond it was old and sound, and the wind slab on the south face would hold a man's weight if he was careful and not hold a goat's. She said it all in small, flat sentences, and he repeated each one in his head, she could tell, because he put his feet where she told him and never once asked why.

Halfway down the ridge he stopped.

"They have reached the foot of the Saddle," he said, without opening his eyes.

"How do you know?"

"The sound. A pair lifts its feet on a count. I heard two counts, then two, and the gap between them is the length of a rope." He tilted his head slightly. "They are at a speed they cannot hold for long. They will halt at the ridge to read the ground."

"They'll halt at the first bare rock. They'll look for tracks and find none."

"Then they will send one pair across the ridge and keep the other back. That is how I would do it." He listened a moment. "That will cost them half an hour."

"Half an hour's good."

"It is better than good, if we use it."

"We're using it." She looked down the south face, at the long white fall of the slope and the dark seam in the cornice, so thin she would not have seen it if she had not known it by heart. "Shelf's there. You see?"

"I see nothing, as I swore."

"Good man." She took a breath. "There's a gully before it. A snow bridge, eight paces across. It's good. I crossed it six winters running."

"How good?"

"Nine in ten."

"And the tenth?"

"The tenth is the mountain's," said Meera. "It's cold. The snow's dry. The bridge has had a freezing night to set." She set her teeth. "I'll go first. I'll plant the axe on the far side and take your weight on the rope. You come across when I say, in my steps, not beside them. Don't look. Don't think. Walk."

"And if it gives?"

"It won't." She said it to him, and then to the snow, and a third time to herself, with her eyes on her mitten. The white tips would show under the wool for an hour yet, but they could not get worse. "I believe it won't."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The gully was a black slot in the white, twelve feet down at its lip and deeper beyond. Across it lay the snow bridge, a smooth blue-white arch no wider than a door, with the shelf's dark mouth at its far end.

Meera went over it like a woman walking on eggs. She did not hurry. She set each boot, tested, shifted her weight, and counted. One. Two. The snow gave a small dry sigh underfoot and held. At the far side she stepped onto rock, sank her axe to the head in the crust beside the shelf's lip, and wrapped a turn of the red rope around the shaft.

"Come," she called.

He came the way he had promised. He put his feet in her steps, eyes shut behind the leather, and his right hand ran along the rope as if it were a banister in a house he had known all his life. She watched his boots. Each time one went down she held her breath, and each time the snow held.

Four paces. Five.

"You're at the middle," she said. "Slow. Slow."

"I am aware of it."

"Don't be aware. Walk."

Six. The wind lifted a thin sheet of powder off the bridge and spread it across his coat. Seven. She could see the shelf's dark mouth over her shoulder, and the cache, and somewhere on the ridge behind them a pair of strangers coming up on a count.

On the eighth step the bridge breathed.

It was a sound she knew, the low, flat sigh of snow that has stopped being a floor. She saw his boot go down and the white around it dissolve, not in a crack but in a quiet, slow pouring, as if the bridge had turned to sugar. She saw him throw himself forward. His hand came off the rope and closed on air.

Then the whole span went, and Rohan went with it.

The rope hit its length with a bang she felt in her ribs. It snapped taut across the lip of the gully and tore at the axe's shaft, and the weight on the other end dragged her one stride toward the edge before she threw herself back. She came down on her knees on the rock with both fists around the axe. The blade bit. The wool shrieked round the shaft and held.

It held, and every ounce of him hung off it.

"Don't you dare," Meera said.

She had not meant to speak. It came out of her through clenched teeth, to the rope, to the gully, to the long black drop and the man at the bottom of it. The rope sang under her hands, thin and high. Somewhere below, a boot scraped on ice, and a sliver of snow ran down the wall, and there was no other sound.

"Rohan," she said. "Answer me."

He did not answer.

# Chapter 8: The Rope-Hut

"Rohan," Meera said again. "Count your fingers."

A scrape answered her, then a cough, then a voice from the bottom of the gully, flat with effort. "Ten. I have been through them twice."

Her knees nearly gave. She did not let them. The rope was still singing against the axe shaft, and every pound of him hung off her two fists.

"Can you see the wall?"

"I see a very blue wall."

"Is there ice you can kick?"

"There is ice I can kick."

"Then kick it when I say, and not before." She looked at the axe. One turn of wool around the shaft would hold a hanging man. It would not lift him. At fourteen, Bindu's mother, a woman with no patience, had taught her what to do about that.

She worked fast. She dug the axe in to the head and stamped the crust around it. She ran the rope back through a loop knotted from its own spare end, tied a sliding grip in the line, and threw her weight on the grip instead of the rope. The tail ran through the loop and doubled her pull. Bindu called it the lazy woman's lift. It was the only way one pair of arms could do the work of two.

"Right hand on the rope," she called. "Left stays down. I'll count, you climb."

"On what count?"

"One is my pull. Two is your boot. Don't think about three."

She hauled. The line came in a hand's width and stuck. "One!"

Below, a boot struck ice. "Two," said Rohan, through his teeth.

"One! Two! One!"

It went like that, a hand's width at a time. The binding of goat hair creaked around his ribs. Each breath he took came out as a sound he would have refused to make in front of the post. He did not stop. He climbed on her numbers, because she was the only voice in the world with a plan for him.

"Twenty-six," she said. "Twenty-seven. You're past halfway. Twenty-eight."

His hand came over the lip. She let go of the grip and seized his wrist with both of hers, and he came up over the edge on his belly, goggles knocked askew, snow in his hair and his face the colour of a candle.

He lay with his cheek in the crust. She knelt beside him, shaking in a way that had nothing to do with lie-cold.

"Ribs," she said.

"Present. Complaining." He did not move to prove it.

She put her palm flat on the binding over his left side anyway and felt him breathe under it, shallow but even, nothing grinding. Jassi's cord had held. She took her hand away.

"You're a fool," said Meera. "You're a bloody, sworn, rule-bound fool. I said slow."

"You said walk." He rolled onto his back and looked at the sky through the slits. "I was walking."

"You shut your eyes!"

"At your instruction."

The laugh got out of her before she could stop it. It was a poor, cracked thing, half sob. Rohan made a sound that might have been a cough and then wasn't, and held his bound ribs with one arm while he laughed too, badly, wheezing, his head going back against the snow. It hurt him. She could see that it hurt him. It went on anyway, and so did hers, until she had to wipe her face on her sleeve and turn it into something else.

"Up," she said. "They'll be on the ridge."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They lay in the crew ledge until the light began to go.

The shelf was as she remembered it: a low cut in the cornice, dry as a cupboard, iron pegs in the back wall. Rohan sat against the rock with the leather slits turned to the floor and told her, with great correctness, that he was not looking at anything. She sat at the mouth and watched four ochre packs move along the bare ridge above, cast about like hounds that have lost a scent, and turn east along the Saddle's lip to look for tracks that were not there.

"That buys the night," she said.

"It buys a part of it."

"Part's enough."

Dusk came blue. The cairn-light woke along the south slope in thin threads, and she took the line down it with the red rope tight between them and the snow squeaking under her. Rohan walked in her steps with his hand on the wool and his jaw set. She did not call it out. She slowed by a third and said nothing.

The Rope-Hut came up out of the dark an hour later, a hump of dry-laid stone, roofed with slate and sod, with its door facing downhill out of the wind. Smoke stains blackened the lintel. A sprig of juniper had been nailed above it by someone dead.

She put her shoulder to the door. It gave on a room barely long enough for a man to lie in, and she stepped in and looked at what was hers.

One chulha, a clay hearth the height of her knee, its mouth black. One sleeping shelf, a plank slab of deodar worn to a dip by forty winters of crossers. And against the north wall, tied in a bundle with hide, the juniper pile.

She counted it with her finger. It took a while, because her finger was clumsy, and she did it again.

"Forty-one sticks," she said.

"Is that a good number?"

"It's a number. One night's fire is five, if we're mean. Eight nights, and one stick over." She dropped her pack against the wall. "The next pile is two days' walk past the door. We can't burn this one to the last stick and hope."

Rohan had come in behind her and stopped under the lintel. He took the leather off his face in the dark and stood holding it. His gaze went to the hearth, and the shelf, and the stacked stones of the walls, and the two of them, and at last to the sprig above the door.

"Guide," he said. "I should say this aloud."

"Go on."

"A lawful warden may not enter this house. A lawful warden may not sleep in it, nor eat in it, nor warm his hands at its fire." His voice was perfectly level. "What we are about to do is harbouring. It carries the rope for me as it carries it for you. I would be hanged beside you, and rightly."

She lit the first stick with her flint. It took on the third try, a blue lick of juniper resin, and the room filled with its sharp clean smoke, the smell of every night she had ever been warm on this road.

"Then hang yourself warm," said Meera.

For a moment he said nothing at all. Then he crossed the floor, folded himself down on the dirt before the hearth with his ribs held stiff, and put his hands out to it.

"It seems a sound policy," said Rohan.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


While the fire caught, Meera did the redbark.

It was her habit on every night of every crossing. She opened the oilcloth roll, took back the six packets Rohan had carried in his coat, turned the twelve small bundles over one by one, and pressed each between thumb and finger to feel for damp. Damp meant clumping, and clumping meant the grey cough would win. She had done it on the pass with her mother's old patience, and now she did it in front of a warden.

He watched her hands. Eventually he said, "May I?"

She weighed it. "One. If it comes to powder, I want it back."

He took a packet in his right hand. A seam had worked loose in the avalanche, and a pinch of dust had come out of the wax. He rubbed it between his fingers and bent to the fire to see it.

His face changed. It was slight. She would have missed it a month ago.

"This is crown-grade," Rohan said.

"It's redbark."

"It is ground fine. Finer than any hill apothecary has the sieve for." He rolled the grains on his thumb. "The crown storehouses sift through a silk sieve with a ring in the weave, and the mark of that ring is pressed into the wax of the wrapper." He tilted the packet. In the firelight, along the fold, she saw it: a faint round print, like the ghost of a coin. "This came out of a crown storehouse. I have seen this mark on every medicine chest the queen has sent north."

"Which is not the same as stolen."

"No. It is not." He looked at her. He didn't accuse. He asked. "Where did you have it?"

Her hands were bare and the fire was near. She kept them where he could see them. "A middleman in the lowlands, who sells through the lower-lane runners. I've never met him. I send silver down the road and a crate comes up." She held out her palm for the packet. "I saw a stamp scraped off the crates. I didn't ask whose. He never told me, and I never wanted to know."

Brown and warm in the light, her fingers stayed still. He studied them, and she let him. That was the worst of it. The truth did not cost her a thing, and it was not clean. Then he gave the packet back.

"I believe you," he said. It was a plain statement, and not his to make. The cold would do the believing. His belief was a courtesy.

"It means a crown man is selling medicine out the back door," she said.

"It means that at least." He did not say the rest. She saw him decide not to, and the decision sat heavy in the room while the juniper snapped.

Meera wrapped the packet again and put it in its row. Then she took the count from inside her coat, the waxed book that never left her ribs, and found the middleman's page. Ten winters of crates, in her own small hand. Beside his entry she drew a round mark, the size of a coin, and wrote nothing under it.

Rohan watched her do it. He did not ask what it meant, and she did not say.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He cooked.

Her hand was already on the sattu bag, with the pot beside it and the water melted, when he said, "I gave back half my winnings at the waystone, Guide. Let me cook, and call it the rest of the wager."

"You didn't win. You cheated. You hummed."

"I did not hum. I breathed in tune."

"Fine." She sat down. "Make it, then."

He made it badly. He poured the flour in before the water had warmed, so that it floated in grey islands. He stirred it with his knife, which was the wrong end of the wrong tool. He tasted it and gave a small clerk's frown, and added salt, and then added a great deal more, from which she deduced that the warden's mess served its sattu without any. When it was done it was a paste the colour of wet slate and nearly as hard, and he held out the bowl to her with the same grave face he used for oaths.

She ate every mouthful. It was dreadful. It was hot.

"Well?" he asked.

"It's the worst thing I've eaten since I was fifteen."

"That is true?"

"Look at my hands."

He looked. He was smiling, with only one side of his mouth, the side he never offered, and then he took his own bowl and ate it as if it were a duty.

The shelf was the next trouble. It was one slab, three feet wide, with a hollow in it, and he stood looking at it with the half-formal air of a man at a council.

"You will take the shelf," he said.

"You've cracked ribs."

"I shall be lying on a floor which is dry and sound. You have been shaking for two days. You will take it."

"You'll take it. I'm the guide."

"You are the guide. That makes you the one thing on this mountain I cannot replace." He said it quite evenly. "I am a warden. There are forty more at the post. The mountain has one of you."

She opened her mouth and found she had nothing in it. She tried again.

"The rope's eight spans. Floor or shelf, you don't go farther than that."

"Then I shall be on the floor within the length." He had already spread the pattu on the dirt beside the chulha, and now he lifted it and held it out to her, folded. "Camphor and all. The shelf will be cold."

"It's your pattu."

"It has done more for you than it ever did for me."

She took it, and she took the shelf. She lay in the hollow the crossers had worn, with a warden's shawl over her and her red rope running across the floor to his wrist, and she did not know where to look. Rohan lay down by the fire with his coat for a pillow. The ribs bound with Jassi's cord made him lie very straight, like a stone figure on a tomb.

Then she listened. She had not meant to.

His breath was slow at first, and careful at the top, and then, when he thought she slept, it dropped. It went long and low and even. The juniper ticked and the wind shifted in the slates. He turned in his sleep, toward the fire, and the rope drew taut across the floor and tugged at his bound wrist. His breath caught on the left side, a small hitch, and stopped.

She was off the shelf before she had decided to be. She crouched, took up the slack of the red rope in both hands and eased it back across the dirt until it lay loose again. His wrist settled. The breath came out of him long and easy.

Longer than the rope needed, she stayed there. Then she went back up to the shelf, lay on her back, and counted the cracks in the slate. At sixty she gave up and counted his breaths.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Near midnight the fire sank. She rose to feed it, a single stick from the forty-one, and stepped to the door to listen to the cold.

The hut door stuck. She eased it open a hand's width.

Down the mountain, far below the cairn-light and the black fold of the south slope, a road ran into Neshar. She had come up it a dozen times, with her pack and her goats. It was a pale thread under the stars, and on the thread there was a row of fire.

Torches, climbing. She counted them, because she could not stop. Nine, then fourteen, then more behind. They went up the road in a line too slow for a march and too tight for a caravan, and between them, thin and carried on the wind, came a horn: two notes, falling, the same pair the regent's hunters blew when they ran a man to ground.

They were not Asha's. Asha's packs did not carry fire.

She stood with her hand on the cold wood of the door. Behind her Rohan slept on, slow and unaware, with the thing in his coat that the regent would pay a lordship for, and that she still could not name.

Nobody on the Neshar side should have known there was a man to hunt. Nobody should have known which pass.

She turned and looked at him. One shake of his shoulder and he would be awake, and angry, and planning. It was what he would want. It was also the first whole night's sleep he had had since the avalanche, on ribs that would not mend without it, and the torches were half a night's climb from the third bend at the best of their pace.

Weighing one against the other, she chose. Dawn, then. She would let him have the dark, and at dawn she would tell him, and he would be right to be furious about the hours. She would answer for them.

She shut the door on the torches, laid the stick on the coals, and did not sleep.

# Chapter 9: Clean-Cut

She told him about the torches before he had his boots on.

"Fourteen that I counted," said Meera. "More behind. They were still climbing when I lost them."

She crouched at the chulha with her back to him, feeding it one stick. Grey light leaked through the gap she had left in the door. Rohan sat on the dirt with the leather strip tied over his eyes and his ribs aching like a bad tooth, and he went through what she had said once, the way he went through a report.

"Regent's hunters."

"A two-note horn, falling. I know it."

"How?"

"From being on the wrong end of it." She did not turn round. "Free to say. Not free to say more."

He let that stand. It was an answer of a kind, and he put it where he put such things.

"Then we have a choice," he said. "Go down on the line this morning, or wait."

"We."

"You are the guide. I am the man asking what the guide would advise."

That turned her head. He could not see her face through the leather, but he heard the pause. He had spent three winters giving orders on this mountain, and one winter of that being wrong about it. He found it was not hard to say. It was only unfamiliar, like a left-handed knife.

"The line comes out on the Neshar road at the third bend," she said. "Half a day down. Fourteen torches are climbing that road. We'd walk into them at noon."

"And if we wait?"

"They can't cross the Saddle in a column. Nobody can. They'll make camp under the lip and send out pairs, as Asha's done, and pairs take days to bind and cross." She poked the fire. "So we wait. We keep the fire low by day and watch the road from the door. We go down when they've gone up past us, or split."

"You do not like waiting."

"I like it better than being found."

He weighed it as a warden would, and found that it held. Going down meant fourteen men with a lordship in view. Staying meant a hut with one door, smoke from one chimney, and four ochre packs somewhere above them on the Saddle's lip. Both were bad. One was slower.

"We wait," said Rohan. "Your judgement, Guide."

"Don't thank me for it."

"I was not going to."

"Good." She sat back on her heels. "You were about to look pleased with yourself."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


By dusk the road below had gone quiet and the ridge above had shown nothing. The hunters' fires burned in a long red string beneath the lip, as small as sparks from a forge. Nobody had come to the hut. Rohan had slept most of the day in the dark, and his eyes had stopped streaming.

Meera knelt at the woodpile and counted the bundle with her finger. Her lips moved.

"Thirty-six," she said. "Five a night is seven nights, and one stick over. We can't spend more than that."

"Then we will not."

"We'll be spending something. Sit down. I've got a rule."

He sat. She dropped onto her heels across the chulha from him, so that the fire stood between them like a third party at a hearing, and held up one finger.

"A question each, every night. Any question. You can answer it or you can refuse." She tapped the juniper bundle. "A refusal costs a stick. It goes on the fire as it is, over the five. You feel it in the wood, not in the hands."

"The mountain does not require this."

"The mountain says silence is free. I'm saying it isn't, in here." Her mouth went crooked. "You keep your secret and I keep mine. But there's seven nights of wood, and whoever won't talk burns the rest of us colder."

He saw the design at once, and admired it more than he liked. A silence on the rope could be a lie in a better coat. Her rule made it a thing with a price that both of them could count. It was a tariff on evasion, and she had set it at the exact weight of what they could afford.

"It is a sound rule," said Rohan.

"Is that a yes?"

"It is an acceptance. I wish to say that I dislike how well it works."

"Good. I'll go first."

She sat quite still, the firelight in the lines beside her mouth. He thought she was going to ask what he carried. His refusal was ready and already priced: one stick, and a look from her that he did not want to see twice.

"Who died?" said Meera.

It was not what he had braced for. He opened his mouth for the refusal and shut it.

"You told me you hunted my crew two winters more for a dead man's sake," she said. "I'd like the man. Not the reason. The man."

The fire ticked. The wind moved in the slates.

"His name was Dilan," said Rohan. "He was twenty-three. He was the youngest warden at the Shilak post, and the worst at knots, and he could not carry a tune in a bucket. He sang anyway, every watch, a hymn he thought was a love song." He looked at his hands. "He died on this pass two winters ago, in a storm, chasing a Kothi smuggler."

He had not said it aloud to anyone since the report. It came out in good order, in the voice of a man reading a report, and that was the only way he could have said it.

Meera did not move. Her hands lay open on her knees. Her eyes were on the fire, and they stayed there.

"He's why," she said at last.

"He is why. I would have hunted your crew for the law alone, and I did, for a winter. After Dilan I hunted it for him." He stopped. "I should not have said that last."

"You said it. I'd rather have it." Her voice had gone low and level. "I'm sorry, Captain."

He looked at her fingers, as brown and warm as the fire made them. She spoke no lie, and for a moment he could not think why he had checked.

"It is my turn," he said.

He had a question ready, and he looked at it plainly. What are you to the Salt Fox? It sat in his mouth like a coin. He could ask it. She would refuse it, and burn a stick, and say nothing, and he would have the shape of her silence to turn over in the dark. It would be evidence, and he knew by now what evidence did to him.

"Who taught you the line?" said Rohan.

Her head came up. He watched her take it in, and take in what he had left out.

"My mother," she said. "I was fifteen. She took me over bound, my first winter crossing, and made me walk it twice with my eyes shut." A small breath. "She died ten winters back. Fever, in a closed winter, with the medicine on the wrong side of a notice on a gatepost. I was nineteen."

"Guide."

"That's the answer. It's all of it." Her hands stayed warm. "Don't say sorry. You've said it once tonight, and I made you."

"I was not going to say it."

"What were you going to say?"

"That it is a very good line," said Rohan. "The one she taught you."

She laughed, one short breath through her nose, and looked away. Her eyes were bright. Then she picked up a stick, turned it over, and set it on the coals, where it caught blue.

"Five," she said. "That's the night's. Go to sleep, Captain."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She slept, or did the next best thing, curled in the hollow of the shelf under his pattu with the red rope slack across the floor between them. He listened to her breathing change, shorten, deepen. Then he lay quite still for a long time with the dark pressing on his eyelids.

At last he put his right hand into his coat.

It lay where it always lay, against the lining over his sound ribs. A forearm's length of grey hand-spun wool, rolled and tied with a scrap of its own cord. He drew it out and held it in the last orange light of the coals.

One end was whipped with black thread, tight and neat. Dilan had done that himself. It was the only knot Dilan had ever tied well, and he had tied it on everything. The other end was where Rohan's thumb always went.

He rolled it over, and found the cut.

In the loose fibres he tied an overhand and pulled it tight, then picked it out again with his thumbnail. He did it slowly, so that the wool made no sound. Tie, pick. Tie, pick. The fibres lay together, every one, severed at the same line, as flat as bread under a good knife.

A mountain did not do that. A mountain frayed. Rock sawed a rope, ice chewed it, and the wool came apart in a beard of broken ends. He had seen a dozen ropes that had parted on the pass, and all of them ended in a ragged mess. This one ended like a cut loaf.

Somebody had held a knife.

He had told himself so on the day he found it, in the cold room at the post with the body on the board and the report in front of him. That evening he had stood in the warden hall, before eleven men and a bare table, with this rope in his right hand, and sworn on it to find the one who had stood at its far end. He had meant every word. He had said the oath, put the rope in his coat, and never taken it out in company since.

And now he was lying in a smuggler's hut with the nearest thing to a smuggler he had ever met three paces from his boots, who had made him sattu-bread jokes, and who had pulled him out of a gully on a lazy woman's lift. He could not have said what he wanted. He turned the cut over and over.

The shelf creaked.

He did not move. The rope lay in his open hand, with the neat black thread on one end and the flat white ruin on the other, and he was perfectly aware of it.

She had half-woken. He could tell by the quality of the silence from the shelf, which had changed from the silence of sleep to the silence of someone deciding. Her eyes were on his hand. He felt it as he might have felt a lamp.

He closed his fingers. He drew the rope back into his coat, slowly, and laid his palm over the place it lay.

She did not ask.

He waited for it. It would have been a fair question, a free one if she wished. He could have answered it or refused it, and the price of either would have been small. She had the right to put to him anything she liked. She looked at him a moment longer, and then she turned her face to the wall, drew the pattu over her shoulder, and settled.

She had chosen silence. He understood that it was a choice. No rule of the mountain gave it to him, and no tariff of hers. It cost her nothing the cold would count, and it was the kindest thing anyone had done for him in a year.

He lay with his hand over his coat, and what settled in his chest was not gratitude. It was something sharper, and he took a long minute to name it.

I will have to use it, he thought.

Not tonight. But there was a ledger in him that never closed. It had already written the evening in a clerk's hand: *Her mother crossed. A line taught at fifteen, over a closed pass, in winter. Her mother died of fever in a closed winter, ten winters ago. Motive. Knowledge of the hunters' horn. A reluctance when the dead man was named.* He had set each truth down as she gave it, in its column, as he had done with every truth since the waystone. He had done it as he breathed.

She had given him her mother, as plainly as she would give a man a cup of water. And he had been counting it.

Then he turned his face to the dark and was, for the length of one long breath, as angry with himself as he had ever been with a smuggler.

It was not fair, he thought. The law was not unfair. It was merely exact, and he had made himself exact in its service until there was nothing in him that could hear *my mother died* and not hear *a motive.* A year ago he would have called this his virtue. He would have said it in a hall.

The coals sank. Somewhere above the hut the wind moved over the lip of the Saddle and found the stacked stones of the nearest cairn, and he heard it hum, low and level, the sound of a true word.

Frayed, he thought, he could have forgiven the mountain. He had been trying to do it for two winters. But the rope was cut clean. Somebody had held the knife, and the somebody was still out in the snow, and he lay awake under the juniper smoke with his hand on the place where the rope lay, not asking himself whose hand it had been.

# Chapter 10: Parley

"Shilak!"

The voice came down the rock like a dropped bucket. Meera had the axe in her fist before she knew she was awake.

"Guide of Shilak! I'm coming no closer than I like, so keep your hands where they are and we'll both stay pleasant!"

She stood in the hut door, heart going, the red rope running slack behind her across the floor to Rohan's wrist. Grey morning. Cloud lay on the Saddle like a lid. Fifteen paces up the slope, a black rib of rock stood out of the snow, and on its crest a woman sat with her boots dangling, as at the edge of a well.

Four ochre packs lay in the snow behind her. Asha had come up in the night and camped over the hut's head, and none of the watching had caught it.

The wind had been blowing across the slope when the shout began. Now it swung and ran downhill, straight at the hut door, and it carried every word.

"Stay down," Meera said, without turning.

"That is Asha's voice?" Rohan had pushed up on one elbow on the dirt. His face looked wrong in the half dark, too bright across the cheekbones. "I should hear what she offers."

"You should stay on the floor with your ribs. If you stand in this door, you're a target the size of a barn." She looked at him over her shoulder. "I'm the guide, Captain."

A pause. Then, to her surprise, he lay back. "Eight spans," he said. "Do not go past them."

"I know my own rope."

She stepped out and paid the line off until it drew, then walked its length, up and across the slope, so that the hut sat below her and the rock rib stood above. Wind pushed at her coat. At the top of the rib the woman grinned and swung one boot.

Asha was twenty-eight, wide in the shoulder, with a gap in her front teeth you could post a coin through. She wore a felt hat with a salt-stained brim and a rope at her waist that ran back along the rock to a thin young man hunched in the lee, a spyglass on a cord at his neck. A second pair squatted by the packs, a long way off, doing something to a stove.

"There she is," said Asha. "Meera of Shilak. I've priced you for three winters and never once seen you. You're shorter than your reputation."

"You're louder."

"It's a loud trade." She tapped her own rope. "This is Lalit. My eyes. And I like the high ground. That's a good axe, by the way. Eleven silver, new."

"It's not for sale."

"Everything's for sale. That's what makes it a market."

Meera looked at the gap between them. One span of steep snow, smooth and glassy, lay between the end of her rope and the foot of the rib. She could see every ripple in it. She could not cross it. Asha's rope tied her to Lalit and nothing else. She could have strolled down to the hut door. She sat where she sat on purpose.

"Eight spans, isn't it?" said Asha pleasantly. "Red wool, spun fine. I'm sitting nine. So you can stop measuring, Guide. You'll get to the end of that rope and no further, and I'll be here, enjoying the view."

Meera kept her face still. Asha knew how far a bound guide could come, to the span. She had done her sums before she ever climbed the rock.

"I'll be quick, because it's cold and I like my toes," Asha went on. "Whatever's under that warden's coat, I want it. His coat, and whatever's sewn in it. I don't ask what. I'm a trader. I don't ask what's in the bale."

Sewn in. Meera thought of the hand that had flattened over his chest in the firelight, and the grey wool that went back under it.

And she thought, plainly: *Asha knows what I am.* The Salt Fox stood on this slope with a warden's rope on her wrist. Eight spans down, Rohan lay with his ear to the door. One shouted word from that rock and he would have the name he had hunted. Her mouth had gone dry.

"Who's paying?"

"The regent." Asha said the word as if tasting it. "A lordship's purse, in Neshar silver, counted out before witnesses. And a crown pardon, with the queen's name on it, for every soul in my book."

"The regent can't pardon. Nobody can but the queen."

"He says he'll have her hand to hand by midwinter." Asha shrugged. "I don't ask about that either."

"Why would I sell you a man I walked up a mountain with?"

"Because he's a warden, and you're what you are, and the rope won't last past the thaw." Her voice dropped into the reasonable register of a woman reading figures off a slate. "And because I can add names. The regent's clerk wants a list for the pardon. Every Shilak name you give me goes on it. Your crew. Your brother. Whoever's sitting in the salt house this minute wondering whether the wardens come back."

It was exactly the right place to push. Meera felt it go in under the ribs and stay.

"How'd you find us?" she said, to give her mouth something to do.

"Oh, we didn't find you." Lalit's voice cracked in the wind, pleased with itself, a boy's voice that had not learned to be quiet. "We were told where to look. The gully approach, three wardens and two goat-folk, and they'd go in on the second day. We watched the whole slope slide from the ridge. Only one warden came out of it, and he came out roped to—"

"Lalit," said Asha, not loudly.

He stopped. The wind took the end of it.

Meera did not move. She set the words in a row. *The gully approach. Three wardens and two goat-folk.*

Two wardens had gone into that gully, and two guides. She had counted them on the slope herself. Rohan had named two in the Kothi yard, before the whole village. She had never heard anyone say three. A watcher on the ridge would have counted two. A gossip in Kothi would have said two.

So the number had not come from eyes on the slope. It had come off a page, from a plan someone wrote out before any of them set foot on the snow.

"Tell your boy to mind his tongue," Meera said evenly. "He'll lose it."

"He loses it daily. It grows back." But Asha's eyes had narrowed a hair. She had seen something cross Meera's face and was trying to price it.

"I'll make you an offer of my own," said Meera. "The salt road is yours at the Kothi toll. Free of the levy, for a winter. My crew's word on it, and a guide at your disposal when it's snowed in."

Asha laughed. It rang off the rock, big and honest and unembarrassed.

"Free of the levy! You're offering me a toll you don't own. Your crew's word is worth, what, a decent goat? A goat with all its teeth." She wiped her eye. "No. I'll tell you what you are. You're two hundred in silver for the skin, and a hundred more if you come in quiet. I'm fond of you, in a distant way. By my mother's last good tooth, I'll bring you down alive, whatever you decide."

"That's your oath?"

"It's my best. I keep it in a drawer." Asha stood, balancing on the rock with the ease of long practice. "I've the whole of today, and tomorrow till the light goes. After that I come down with four, and the hunters under the lip are a good deal less polite than I am. Think about your brother. Think about the names."

She turned her back, which was a kind of insult, and Lalit scrambled to follow her.

Meera stood with the wind in her face until they were gone. Then she looked down at her hand and made herself open it. The fingers were red from the cold, and shaking.

Asha's threat wasn't what did it. The other thing did: a pardon for Kiran, waiting at the end of one word.

She walked back down the rope to the hut, and every name on the list walked down with her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He was awake when she came in. He had propped himself against the wall under the shelf with his right hand flat on the dirt, and the light from the door showed her how bright his eyes were.

"She wants my coat," Rohan said.

"You heard."

"Not the words. The tune of it." A cough took him, dry and short, and he held his ribs through it with his teeth set. "What does she offer?"

She looked at his fingertips on the dirt. Pink, and warm with fever, with no trace of white in them. And the wind had turned downslope right after Asha's first hail; she had felt it herself. Anything that carried to the door would have carried inside. He had caught the tune and not the words, and it was no lie.

"A lordship's purse for the coat, and a pardon for her crew." She sat down across from him, since there was nowhere else. "She gives us today, and tomorrow till the light goes. After that she comes down with four."

"And how she found us," said Rohan. "You went quiet at the end. I heard that, too."

"Her boy said they were told where to look. The gully approach, and three ward—" She stopped. The word sat in her mouth. Three was a number off a page, and the page would have been his post's. If she gave him the number she gave him the shape of the hole. She could not yet rule him out. If the leak came from his own door, it was leverage, and she might need it to buy Kiran's name out of that book. "And a deadline," she said. "That's all of it. The coat is worth a lordship to her. That's the price on you."

He studied her. She watched him see the stop. He had asked precise questions all his life, and she saw him weigh this one and set it down.

"Silence is free," said Rohan.

"It is."

"Sewn in," he said, after a while.

"Her word."

"It is a fair word." He closed his eyes briefly. "I wish to say that I do not know how she learned it."

His hands lay open on his knees, steady. Out on the rise above the hut the cairn hummed through the roof stones, low and level. It told her no one under that roof had lied, and nothing about whether his post had.

"What is it, Captain?" she said. "The regent's after the queen's hand. What's that?"

He did not answer. She waited, and he went on not answering.

She let it ride through the afternoon. She melted snow. She made a thin soup out of barley and a dried apricot she rationed with a knife. From the door she watched the road, where the hunters' fires pricked the dusk under the lip. She counted them, marking the tally on the doorpost with her thumbnail, and each time it came out the same. Then she took the count from her coat and wrote, in her small square hand: *Three wardens, two goat-folk. Said by Asha's boy. We were two and two.* She looked at it a while, then closed the book. Twice she glanced at the man by the wall and found him with his head against the stone and his lips slightly apart, breathing as though the air were something he had to bargain for.

At dusk she took the night's five from the bundle, thirty-one down to twenty-six, and laid the first on the coals.

"My question," she said.

"You have one."

"There's a woman on the rock who'll pay a lordship for it. I'd like to know what for. What's in your coat?"

He looked at her. The fire made his face all hollows.

"Guide, I cannot."

"That's a refusal."

"It is." He sounded tired and entirely sure. "Burn the stick."

She did, because she'd made the rule and she'd hold to it herself. She took one more from the bundle, twenty-six down to twenty-five, and put it beside the five. It caught blue.

"I'll guess, then," she said. "You can stay quiet. It's free."

"You are very pleased with that rule."

"It's a good rule. First guess: coin. A pay chest sewn in the lining."

"Wardens do not bribe, Guide."

"Wardens don't do a lot of things. Second guess: names. A list of the regent's men, handed to you by an informer." She leaned in. "Third: a pardon. A real one, with a seal on it, made out blank."

"That is a charming idea."

"That's not an answer."

"It is not." He coughed again, harder, and the rattle at the bottom of it made her hands go still. "You are close to something and well away from something. I will not say which."

She turned a stick over in her fingers. She had meant to stop there. But the hunters' fires had nagged at her all afternoon.

"You haven't asked yours tonight. I want a smaller question." She waited for his nod. "Does the regent want you dead, or only the coat?"

The fire ticked. She had thought it a kindness, a thing he could answer with half a breath. *Only the coat.* If it were true, it cost him nothing, and it would have let her sleep.

He said nothing. He looked at her, and the nothing went on, and she heard the wind go over the roof stones twice.

"That's easy to answer," she said, "if it's only the coat."

"I know."

"Then say it."

"I cannot." His voice was very gentle. "Burn the stick, Guide."

She did it herself. Twenty-four. Her hands were quite steady as she laid it down, and that was the worst of it. The man across the fire could not tell her the one thing a man in his place would tell her if he could.

"Meera," said Rohan.

He had never said it on purpose. She heard that. The stick she had been lifting slipped from her fingers and rolled against the hearthstone, and she picked it up again with both hands, as if it were heavier than it was.

"Asha is not the only danger," he said. "I must be careful, and I am not being careful with you. If I say the whole of it, I put a weight on you that may cost your whole crew. You would be carrying it."

"I'm already carrying it. It's on a rock above my head."

"Yes." He looked at his hands. They were trembling very slightly. She did not think he knew. "Then you should have part. I did not want to give it."

The wind slid over the roof.

"It is the queen's will," said Rohan.

She watched his hands. Warm, pink, no white at the tips. The red rope lay slack and warm at her wrist, and stayed that way.

"The queen's will," she said. "Her wish? Or—"

"I will not be drawn further."

She turned it over and could not make it lie flat. A dying queen's testament, naming her heir. A paper the regent would pay a lordship to burn. It fit too neatly, the way a key fits a lock it was filed for. And a will could be sewn in a coat. It could not be, she thought, the whole reason a warden captain climbed a closed pass in the thirtieth winter of the reign with the goats dead behind him.

But it was a door. He had held it open the width of a hand. A man who had refused her twice and burned two sticks for it had still reached across the fire.

"Thank you," she said. It came out small.

"You are not to thank me." He drew a short breath that stopped halfway. "The hunters are the greater trouble. I should tell you how they will come, because I know the road. I walked it as a boy with my father." His voice had sped up, slurred at the edges. "They will hold the third bend. But the herdsmen's cut above the bend, behind the stone fold, they will want that as well, because it is the only way round. Two men there, I think. If we take the line down and cut left at the shrine, before the—"

He stopped.

She looked up from the fire.

He sat very straight, mouth a little open, as if the next word had not arrived. His eyes had gone to some distance. A sheen stood on his forehead, though the hut was cold, and the breath that went in and out of him made a small wet sound, like a boot in a thawing marsh.

"Rohan?"

"Before the," he said. "The shrine." His right hand lifted off his knee, searching for something, and fell.

She was across the floor before he tipped. The rope snapped taut between their wrists and she took his weight on her chest, one arm behind his shoulders, the other flat on the dirt to stop them both going over. His head dropped against her collarbone. His hair was damp. He was heavy and slack, and the breath dragged in and out of him with that dreadful rattle.

And he was burning.

She put the back of her bare hand against his neck and snatched it away, then put it back. Heat came off him in a slow rush, like the side of a bread oven. It had been hiding under the snow and the shivering and the camphor and the quiet good manners of a man who would not say he was ill. It had been there all day. She had sat across from it and counted sticks.

"Captain," said Meera. "Stay. Say my name. Say anything."

His head moved against her. His lips shaped something she couldn't catch.

Outside, above the hut, a woman had given her a day and a night. Below, the hunters' fires waited on the road, one spark for every scratch on her doorpost. Four hundred paces of ridge and a pocket of herdsmen's trail lay between her and anywhere.

She held a burning man in her arms, and the coat over his heart was warm against her wrist.

# Chapter 11: The Lapis Goat

The coat came off him in three pulls, and he never knew it.

Meera dragged the left sleeve over the bound ribs as gently as she could manage, which was not very. Rohan made a sound in his throat and rolled his head against the wall. The heat of him came through his shirt like a stove through a door. She laid the coat on the shelf, unwrapped Jassi's goat-hair cord from his chest, and looked at what lay under it.

Bruise, yellow-green and black, wide as her two hands. And over it, all down the left side, the skin glistened.

"Right," she said. "Counting."

She said it aloud because the hut was too quiet. The breath dragged in and out of him with that wet sound. She fetched the pot, packed it with snow, and set it on the chulha. Then she took the juniper sprig from the nail above the door, the one the crew hung for luck, and stripped it into the water. The needles hissed. The smell came up sharp and clean, like a cold room with the window open.

Four sticks to bring it to a boil. She fed the fire and did not look at the bundle.

She tore a strip from the hem of her own shirt, soaked it in the steaming water, wrung it until her palms stung, and laid it across his ribs. He flinched, then sighed. She did it again. Steep, wring, lay. Steep, wring, lay. The cloth cooled in a minute against the fire in him, so she kept three going and rotated them like a woman minding bread.

It was not enough. She knew that while her hands were still moving.

Juniper opened a chest. It did not break a fever. For that she had one thing, and it was not hers.

She pulled her pack across the dirt and unrolled the oilcloth at the bottom. Twelve packets lay in three rows of four, palm-sized, wrapped in waxed paper and tied with thread. She had counted them at the salt house and on the Saddle and every night since. She touched the first with one finger and stopped.

Nimmi measured redbark by the pinch: a pinch a dose, morning and night, three days to a course, child or grown man alike. Forty-one sick in the note. She had done that sum on the pass a hundred times, and it had never once come out right.

"Half," she said.

She slit the corner of the first packet with her knife. The powder was the colour of dried blood and smelled of wet bark and iron. She tipped half a pinch from the knife tip into a cup of the hot juniper water and stirred it with a twig until it clouded. Then she raised his head onto her arm.

"Drink. Captain. Drink it."

He swallowed because she pinched his jaw and made him. Most of it ran down his chin. She wiped it and tried again, and the second cup went down better. It was less than a man his size should have. She did not tell herself otherwise.

She folded the packet's corner over and tied it with its own thread. Next she took the waxed notebook out of her coat, found the page, and wrote with the stub of lead.

*Redbark, half a pinch. One captain, lung fever. Ranu is one dose poorer.*

The line was in the same flat hand as *two hundred and six people* and *eighteen silver*. Nothing in it showed what it cost to put down.

She shut the book.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


By the time the pot had gone black on the bottom, the shirt strips were steaming on his ribs and the sweat had come out on him in earnest. That was either good or very bad. She sat back on her heels and watched his chest move, one breath, one more, and counted them.

His coat was steaming too, where it lay on the shelf by the fire, soaked with a day of fever. She picked it up to turn it, and her fingers closed on something that was not wool.

Hard. Small. Under the collar, in the seam.

She went still.

Then she turned the coat inside out on her knee and ran her thumb along the lining. A stitched pocket, no wider than her palm, in the seam at the nape, where no hand would go in a search. The thread was a warden's grey. The stitches were close and even. Someone had done this carefully, and not at the post.

*Sewn in*, Asha had said.

Her knife was out before she decided anything. Six stitches came away, each as neat as a seamstress's, and two fingers went into the pocket. They found a cord first, then the heft at the end of it.

She drew it out into the firelight.

It was a stone no longer than her thumb joint. Lapis, deep as water under ice, flecked with gold dust like a frozen sky. On its face, cut so finely she had to tilt it to see, stood a mountain goat. Horns swept back. Forefoot lifted. One eye, a single pinprick, looking out.

Every child in Hemkot knew that goat from the coins. It had never been on a coin like this.

The queen's seal.

Someone had threaded the cord through a hole in the stone's shoulder and tied it off with a double hitch: two turns round the standing part, a tuck, and a locking half-turn. Every warden tied that knot. It held the notice on the Kothi gatepost, the lanterns on the post wall, and the noose peg on the deodar. She had seen it ten thousand times and never once held one in her hand.

Her mouth had gone dry.

"Meera."

She jerked. He had said it soft, the way a man says a name to keep hold of it. But his eyes were closed and his head rolled on the wall, and the next word was for somebody else.

"No," said Rohan, quite reasonably. "No, not the grain. The bell. He will have the bell rung for it."

She sat very still, the stone cold in her fist. He had named her in his fever, and she did not know what to do with that, so she did nothing.

"Twenty days." The words came in a rush and a drag, as if he were wading. "Twenty days from the post. Fifteen, now. Sixteen. I have not—it must be pressed before the assembly, before they—" A cough broke him in two. He rode it out with his eyes still shut. "Pritam. He will say she is dying. He will have the Snow Fair say it. They will look at the regent and see a man who feeds them, and—"

*Pritam.* The regent, in that voice. She'd heard the name said with contempt all her life, but never with the ache of a man who'd carried it a long way.

"Dilan," said Rohan. His hand lifted a little off the dirt. "Mind the thread. Dilan, the thread." And then, very low, "No."

She put the seal down on her knee, picked up a wet strip, and laid it on his ribs. She kept her hand there until he quieted.

Then she sat and put it together, line by line, as she would a ledger.

A regent who wanted Neshar. A queen too sick to ride to a midwinter fair. A fair where the whole assembly gathered below Garh Neshar to hear a man say the queen was dying, and to see him invested before they thought about it. And a warden captain who climbed a closed pass in the dead of winter, against his own law, with a stone in his collar that said otherwise.

*Queen's will.* He had said it, and his hands had stayed warm. Not a testament. The queen's hand, in the shape of a goat. A seal pressed in front of the assembly would undo whatever Pritam had built.

And that was why Asha wanted the coat. Never the man. Never the redbark. Only this: a stone the length of a thumb joint, and the clerk who would pay a lordship for it, and a pardon, and the end of Pritam's trouble.

She turned it over on her palm. The goat watched her out of one gold eye.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She held it a long time.

The fire sank and she did not feed it. Outside, the wind worked at the slates. Rohan's breath rasped on.

*Two hundred in silver for the skin, and a hundred more for quiet.* That was her own price. The other price was the list, and it was longer. Bindu and her songs. Roop and Chand somewhere on the road. Every name in the salt house, written into a clerk's ledger as pardoned. No more nailed notices. No more pegs. Not one more of her people hanged on the deodar.

And Kiran, with a space beside his name in the regent's book.

And her. She made herself say it, since there was no one awake to hear. The third peg on the deodar was empty, and the name the wardens meant to hammer under it was the Salt Fox. The Salt Fox was Meera of Shilak. Asha's pardon would lift the rope off her own neck first.

There would be silver in it too. Enough for the road to Ranu to stay open, for more redbark than twelve packets, for a lowland physician to climb to the walnut slope and tell Nimmi her count was wrong. The queen's seal was not just a stone. It was the one thing in Hemkot that could buy what she'd have sold her right hand for since she was nineteen.

It was in her hand now. He was lying at her feet with a fever she could let run.

She did not have to cut his throat. That was the clean part of it. She only had to keep the redbark in the oilcloth, and let the juniper go cold, and in the morning walk up the slope with the stone and tell Asha to name her price.

She looked at the man on the dirt.

*He hanged two.* She knew it as plainly as the heft of the stone. Two lowland men in three winters, and her loads twice, and he had told her himself, without cushioning it, that he hunted the Salt Fox crew for Dilan's sake and meant to bring it in. He did not know whose hands were on his ribs. The Salt Fox was standing over him with his life in her pack.

She could not make that stand for nothing. That was the trouble with a rope that took lies out of your mouth. It put a man's words into your count whether you wanted them or not.

And what would Pritam do with a winter? She knew what he did with grain. He sold it at famine prices to villages like Ranu. A regent invested at the Snow Fair, with no seal to stop him, would own every valley behind that pass. Nimmi's count would be the least of it.

That was a reason, and a good one. But she made herself look at it, because she knew her own mind and did not trust a good reason that arrived so conveniently. It was not why her hands would not close on the stone.

Her hands would not close because of last night. He had lain awake with Dilan's rope end in his fist, thumb working at the cut end, tying and picking the same small knot over and over. She had seen it, and turned her face to the wall, and asked him nothing. Earlier that night he could have asked her about the Salt Fox, and he had asked who taught her the line instead.

He had known what her silence was. She'd felt him know it. And he had not asked for thanks.

"Right," said Meera, to nobody.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She did it by the dying fire, slowly, because slow was the only way she would get it right.

The cord had slipped half out when she unpicked it, and she had to find the way it ran. She threaded it back through the hole in the stone, slid it to the shoulder, drew it snug, and put the lapis goat-side down in its pocket, deep in the seam. Her needle was in her coat. Six stitches of the grey thread went in, small and close, to match the six she'd cut. A knot, and she bit the thread.

The cord still hung loose out of the pocket's mouth, a hand's length of it. It needed tying off.

Her fingers made the knot before she'd had a thought. A flat reef with a half hitch, the crew's knot, the one Bindu had taught her at five.

She looked at it.

It was a Kothi Shilak knot, and it sat in the seam of a warden's coat like a signature. If he woke and put his hand to his collar, he would know it in a breath. A man who could read a pass by its pegs could read a knot.

Her thumbnail picked it out.

Then she thought back. The old tie had been plain in the firelight, with the stone lying across her knee. Two turns round the standing part. A tuck. The half-turn that locked it, drawn down tight against the hole. She laid the cord over her fingers and did it as she would copy a line from a ledger. Two turns. The tuck. The locking half-turn. Her fingers wanted to go the other way, the way they had gone for twenty years, and she made them go against it.

It looked right. It looked exactly right. A small, hard tug set it.

She looked up.

His eyes were open.

They were slits, bright and sunk, glazed with fever, and they were on her hands. How long, she could not tell. One thumb still pressed the knot flat, and the coat lay across her knees with the open collar in the light.

He looked at the knot. Then he looked at her face.

A dozen things were there for the saying. *You were burning. I used half a pinch. I found it.* She said none of them. The silence sat between them across the chulha and the cold pot, the third party at the hearing.

His lips moved. No sound came. His gaze went back to the cord, and rested there. Then his eyelids sank slowly, like a man putting down something heavy, and his head rolled gently against the wall.

His breath evened out. Not much, but it evened.

Her hand ached by the time she moved it. She folded the coat over his legs, lifted the strip from his ribs, wet it, and laid it down again.

The notebook came out. The fire gave enough to see by. She found the page where she'd written *Ranu is one dose poorer* and held the stub of lead over the line beneath.

She thought about two hundred in silver. She thought about a space beside Kiran's name, and a peg with her own.

She wrote:

*One captain. Price: none.*

The stones in the wall hummed their low note and held it. She looked up from the page. Rohan's right hand had crept off the dirt in his sleep, and it lay flat over the seam at his collar, fingers spread, as if to keep something warm.

# Chapter 12: Grandmother's Knot

"Lift your hips," Meera said.

"I am able to walk."

"You were able to walk to the wall and back, and the wall won. Lift."

Rohan lay on the hut floor in his blankets with his jaw set. The fever had broken some time before dawn. It had left him pale, hollow at the temples, and polite in the way of a man who has been very ill and wants it understood that he has not been *changed*. The second half-pinch of redbark had gone into him at first light. She had written that down too.

The trouble was the stick count. Twenty-four dry sticks of juniper, and a fever that had eaten four. She needed the day fire built from something else. Outside, ten paces down the slope, a wind-felled juniper lay with its roots in the air and its limbs half buried in snow. Deadfall. It would be wet and smoky and it would sulk, but it would burn, and it would leave the dry sticks for the nights.

Ten paces. Her rope ran eight arm-spans.

She had measured it twice with her arms flung wide, once from the door and once from the sill, and it came out the same both times.
