From the sill, with the rope paid out to its end, she could just reach the tree's root-ball. The limbs lay a pace beyond that. A pace was a price she could pay, if she kept it short.

So the man would have to go to the door.

"I'll drag you," she said.

"I would rather not be dragged."

"Then crawl."

He looked at her. She looked at him. After a moment he shut his eyes and lifted his hips.

She got the grey pattu under him, and the hut blanket over that, and gathered the corners at his shoulders into one fist. She leaned back and pulled. The whole bundle moved six inches, and Rohan, rolled in it like a dumpling, said something short.

"I didn't hear that."

"I said," said Rohan, with great dignity, "that the floor is uneven."

"It's packed dirt. It's the flattest thing in the hut."

"It has found every rib I own."

She pulled again. The rope between their wrists tangled in his arm, and she had to stop and untwist it, and by then she was laughing in a way she did not like. He heard it. His mouth went thin.

"Guide."

"Sorry. Sorry." She wiped her eye with the back of her glove. "It's the voice. You've a voice for a tribunal and you're being hauled along like a goat on a feast day."

"I have commanded forty men," said Rohan to the roof beam. "I have written to the queen. I am being moved like a sack, by a woman who comments."

"Sacks don't argue."

"This one has begun. I wish it noted that I offered to crawl."

"You didn't. I did."

"Then I wish it noted that I thought of it."

That time the bundle slid a full arm's length, and she got another, and another, and the dirt hissed under the wool. At the door the sill had a lip of old snow. He stuck against it like a cork. She set her boot against the post and gave one great pull that tipped him over the lip and out into the cold, where he landed on his back and said a word she had not known he knew.

It was a good word. It had two syllables and a great deal of feeling in it.

"Captain," said Meera, "I'm shocked."

"You are the one who is shocked." He lay and breathed. A little colour had come up under the pallor, from the effort or the shame. "I believe I will sit now."

She propped him against the doorpost with the pattu round his shoulders. His hair was stuck flat. He looked like a man fished out of a river, holding on to what remained of his manners.

"Stay," she said.

"I am not a dog."

"You're an anchor. Same thing, for the next hour."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The morning came up hard and bright, with no cloud on the Saddle. From the doorway the whole slope lay open, a long fall of snow to the black line of the Neshar road, and on the right a rock rib where Asha's camp had sat.

Asha's camp was moving.

Meera stood with the axe in her hand and watched. Four ochre packs were going onto four backs. The second pair doused their stove with snow. Lalit came last, thin as a heron, his spyglass swinging on its cord. At the lip of the rock he turned and looked up at the hut.

She lifted the axe. It was not a wave.

He turned away. They went down in single file, two ropes and four packs, and the lip of the Saddle took them one by one.

"Gone?" said Rohan behind her.

"Down. Not far. She has until the light goes still owing to me, and she's leaving it." She frowned at the empty rock. "Repositioning, or the hunters have called her in. I'll know when I know."

She paid the red rope out to its end and walked.

At eight spans the line drew tight, trembling behind her like a plucked string. The root-ball stood at her elbow. The first limb hung a pace past it. She took that pace.

The leak began with the step: a thin draining of warmth from her ears, then her cheeks, as if someone had opened a door in her. She hooked the axe haft round the limb and hauled. A branch as thick as her arm snapped off at the knuckle. She stepped back inside the line and the warmth rushed home, stinging.

Three trips, three paces past the line, three small thefts of heat. On the fourth she reached for the topmost limb, and something that was not a branch swung against her glove.

A bundle.

It hung in the crook of the tree at head height, wrapped in oilcloth and tied off with a cord, the same ochre as the packs. It had not been there yesterday. Anyone passing could have hung it. Anyone, she thought, who knew exactly where she'd have to come for wood.

"What is it?" Rohan called.

"A present." She untied nothing yet. She stood and looked at the knot.

It was no ordinary knot. She had tied ten thousand and seen more, and she knew a hitch from a bend at a glance, as she knew a face. This started as a loop, drew its tail through the loop's own throat, and came back round to do the same twice more, so that three small loops sat nested in one another. The final tail did not finish. It slid under the first two, like a snake tucking its head beneath its own coils, and left the whole thing tight enough to take a dead lift.

Her thumb went to the tuck and traced it. Once. Twice.

She knew that shape, and not from rope. She had seen it in stone every winter of her life, on the inner face of the Shilak Cairn, where the old carvers had cut two hands bound by a single cord. Where the cord crossed itself between the wrists, it made this figure. She had run her thumb over it as a child, and nobody had told her it was a pattern a person could tie. Once more she had seen it, worn smooth, on the east face of the waystone below the Saddle.

Somebody had tied it onto a bundle of food and left it in a tree.

She cut the oilcloth, not the cord, and worked the knot loose with her fingertips, slowly, to learn how it went. Inside lay a skin bag of sattu, a handful of dried apricots, a twist of salt, and a strip of smoked goat as long as her forearm. A trader's courtesy, or a price tag. She could not tell which.

She carried it back with the last of the wood. The fire took the branches and gave off a sullen white smoke that smelled of resin and wet pennies. She sat down in the doorway beside him with the bundle on her knees. He looked at the apricots, and then at her.

"You will not eat that."

"I'll eat it when I'm hungry enough to count the risk. It isn't poison. A woman who priced me honestly doesn't poison. It's the other thing she's doing."

"Which is?"

"I don't know yet."

She took the waxed notebook out of her coat and found a clean leaf. The lead stub had gone blunt. She laid the cord over her left hand, loop by loop, and drew it as she saw it. Beside it, small, she wrote *Asha's bundle, deadfall, at the rope's end*, and under that, because the thing would not leave her alone: *Same as cairn.*

Rohan was watching the lead move. He read things, as a habit. She could feel him doing it without turning her head.

"May I ask a question?" he said. "Not on the night's account. It has been in my mouth since yesterday."

"It's daytime. Ask. I'll decide whether to answer."

"The post keeps a file on the guides of Shilak. A factor's receipt was seized at Taravan last spring. It shows a crossing paid at twice the Kothi rate." He looked straight ahead. "The same week, a widow crossed with a sick child, by the gatekeeper's tally, and no payment is set against her anywhere. I have read that file eleven times. The sums do not agree, unless the rich pay for the poor." He paused. "You charge the rich double, I believe."

She did not stop drawing. She had carried two hundred and six people, and he had found the place where the ledger leaked.

There was no reason to lie, and a lie would have cost her a finger-end. She found she did not want to.

"Yes."

"Why?"

"So the poor cross free."

The chimney drew the white smoke up and away. Down the slope the wind pushed snow across the road in a long, low sheet. Rohan was silent, and not as he went silent over the seal. He sat the way a man does when he has lifted the lid on a pot and found a different dish.

"All of them?"

"All who ask, and can't pay. A widow with a sick child. A shepherd whose flock's on the far side. Taravan factors pay what they're charged and complain about the weather. It balances, if you're careful."

"You are careful."

"I count."

"That is not in the file," said Rohan, slowly. "The gap is there. The reason never was." He rubbed his thumb along his knuckle. "I had a picture of you, Guide. A clear one. I supposed it accurate."

"What was it?"

"A woman who makes money out of a closed road."

"I do make money out of a closed road," said Meera. "I just don't keep it all."

He turned his head and looked at her then, a straight, grave, unsmiling look, and she could not hold it, so she bent over the notebook and shaded the third loop darker than it needed.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She ate a strip of Asha's goat, in the end, because her stomach had made up its mind. She watched him watch her do it and did not offer him any. By noon the fire had settled to a low, dirty orange, and in the doorway the sun lay a hand's breadth across the floor and warmed their boots.

She had answered. It was her turn. The day had no stick rule for turns, but she kept the rule by habit.

"My question," she said.

"You are owed one."

"Not the seal." That was the one she'd bought with silence. "Something plain. Something the file would have. Have you hanged anyone, Captain?"

He did not flinch. She had half hoped he would.

"Two," said Rohan.

"I knew that."

"I know you did. You wished to hear it in my voice." He looked down at his open hands, lying on his knees in the sun. "The first was a lowland runner with a debt to a factor. Thirty-one, I think. He had carried powder for the regent's men over the Saddle two winters running, and I did not weigh whether the regent's men were the worse for him or he for them. He cursed me till the peg, and I thought it fair."

She waited.

"The second was a boy."

The word went in softly and stayed.

"Ketan," said Rohan. "Seventeen. A lowland boy from the Taravan road. His mother had married into Neshar, and she had a cough and no salt for the broth. He was taking her forty pounds of it over the pass. The post sent a patrol. I was the officer. He was alone on a closed pass in the first snow, with no writ, and the law is plain on that." His voice never rose. "He did not curse me. He asked if I would carry the salt to her. I said I could not. A warden who delivers a smuggler's load has harboured it."

Meera watched his hands. The knuckles were pink, the nails clean. They stayed the colour of hands. Not a thread of white anywhere, not a tremor. Only his thumb pressed once, hard, into his own palm, and lay still.

He had said it plainly and every word was true.

"Did you do anything?" she said.

"I paid his mother's fine, when the post fined her for the salt that never reached her." He looked at her. "It was too late for the boy and not for his mother. I do not offer it in defence."

"Good."

"I did not think you would find it so."

"I don't," said Meera.

She waited for the anger. It had been with her for ten winters and she had a place ready for it, with a door and a bolt. It did not come as she'd braced for. She saw a boy of seventeen with forty pounds of salt on his back, and a man in a warden's coat who had said *I cannot* and meant it, and gone on meaning it until the peg.

Forgiveness was not on offer. She wanted that clear to herself. Forgiveness was a thing you gave, and she had nothing to give a man who stood on the right side of a rope and let a boy go through. It was a debt, and it stood on the page of his account, and she did not close the page.

She lifted the lead stub and wrote, below the knot, in the same flat hand as the rest:

*Ketan. 17. Salt to his mother. Hanged. The captain's count.*

She did not write *two*. She wrote the name. It was the first time she had put a warden's act into the book in his own words.

The sun moved a finger's width across the floor.

"The double price," said Rohan, after a while, "is in no record."

He was not talking to her exactly. He had his head against the post and his gaze on the road. He turned his thumb against his knuckle, over and over, and did not seem to know it.

She was the Salt Fox, and the file had hunted her for three winters without once learning why she was worth keeping.

"No," Meera said. "Nobody writes down what we do for free."

She got up to see to the fire. Behind her the rope ran slack between them across the sill, and neither of them touched it.

From far down the slope, a horn sounded. Two notes, the second falling.

The rope drew taut on her wrist. She had not moved.

"That is the hunters' call," said Rohan quietly. He was already looking at the knot drawing in her notebook. "And it came from where Asha's crew went down."

# Chapter 13: Hunters' Fires

Rohan got to his feet on the second try and did not look at her while he did it.

The doorpost took most of his weight. The pattu slid off one shoulder, and his ribs closed round the breath like a fist round a coin. But he stood. The hut floor stayed where it belonged, and the rope between his wrist and hers hung in a slack curve and did not pull.

"Walk," said Meera.

"Where?"

"To the fold in the rock and back. Twenty paces. I'll count."

He walked. The snow outside was crusted and took his boots with a squeak. He kept his left arm pinned against his side and set each foot as if it might be asked to testify. Behind him she counted aloud in the flat voice she used for goats and packets, and he hated, with great precision, every number.

Yesterday she had hauled him across the floor like a sack. He had commanded forty men. He had stood before the queen's chancellor and read out a post's accounts without a stumble. And he had lain in a blanket while this woman laughed so hard she had to stop and untwist the rope.

"Twenty," said Meera. "Turn."

He turned. It cost him. "I apologise for the delay."

"No."

"I beg your pardon?"

"You've said sorry six times since the door. For the delay, for the weight, for the floor. I'm not charging for any of it." She came up beside him, close enough that the rope tightened and eased. "You can say it again if you like, but I've work for your hands and I'd rather they didn't waste breath."

"What work?"

"Rope."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She set him on the sill with the red rope across his knees and made him learn it.

It was his own trade turned inside out. A warden used rope to take things: a prisoner's wrists, a load off a mule, a body off a ledge. She used it to keep two people alive. She taught him to pay it out hand over hand when she stepped off the line, so the pull never came as a jerk on a bound wrist. Next came taking in slack against a slide, sitting back with his heel dug in, and reading the tug of her weight by its rhythm. Three short pulls meant halt. One long one meant come.

"You'll do all of it with your right," she said. "The left stays in your coat."

"The left is perfectly—"

"The left lifts nothing above the shoulder. You told me so yourself on the pitch, when you thought you were lying."

He had not thought he was lying. He had simply not said. He closed his mouth and gave her his right hand.

The first bowline came out wrong. The second came out as something that would have slipped under a wet goat. "I am sorry," he said, "I have not tied that one-handed since—"

"Seven."

"You are counting apologies."

"I count everything. A bowline round a post, Captain. The rabbit comes up out of the hole."

The third held. The fourth held. By the ninth he had stopped talking. His fingers knew the work even where his pride did not, and she sat on her heels and watched them as she might watch a horse she was thinking of buying. When he drew the loop tight on the post, she tugged it hard enough to rock him, and it did not move.

"Good," said Meera.

That was all. No grin, no more. But the heat that had been sitting in his face since the doorway eased off, and he found himself looking at his own hand with something close to interest.

Under the collar of his coat, the seam at his nape lay flat and warm against his neck. He knew by touch the knot at the end of the cord. Two turns, a tuck, a locking half-turn. A warden's knot, tied by hands that had spent twenty years tying another. He had said nothing about it. Neither had she. It sat between them like a line in a ledger that neither would sign.

"Again," she said, and he tied it again.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The afternoon went to walking. Twenty paces became sixty, then a hundred, to the shoulder of the rock rib where the slope fell away and the whole Neshar side lay open under a white sky. He needed the rest by then. He leaned on the stone with his breath sawing and did what he did best, which was read ground.

The Saddle's lip. The black thread of the Neshar road, switchbacking down. The third bend, half a day off, where a stone fold sat in the lee of a spur and a herdsmen's cut climbed behind it, steep and narrow, to a shrine no one had tended in his lifetime.

"They're lighting early," Meera said.

Smoke. A thread of it at the third bend, then another higher up. By dusk the threads had become fires. He counted them from the rock, as he had once counted torches along a ridge at night, because counting was a way of not feeling. Three at the bend. Two by the stone fold. Four more above it, strung out on the slope, and one small orange point right at the shrine.

Eleven. All of them climbing.

He frowned. It was wrong, and for a moment he could not say how.

Then he could. The road lay a mile to their left, broad and packed, an easy march for any column that wanted to reach the Saddle. A hunting party with fourteen torches and a horn would take it. They would take it because it was easier, and because they had no reason to be anywhere else.

The fires were not on the road. They were on the cut.

His mouth had gone dry.

The cut was a fallback. It was no part of the pass or of any map the post held. Varun had drawn it at Rohan's direction, at a table in lamplight two nights before the crossing, in case the gully failed. Rohan had held the ruler and spoken the way aloud: from the winter line at the third bend up behind the fold, past the shrine, and round the spur to the Saddle's lip, where a pair on foot could come up unseen by anyone on the road. Varun had drawn it, in his neat hand, on the only copy.

Varun was under the gully. He had been under it for seven days. Rohan had stood on the slide in the dark and dug until his nails split.

So someone had been told. Someone had read the line off a sheet in the post room, or off a wall, or out of a man's head, and carried it down the mountain and sold it. And the cleanest answer, the one that sat there plain as a peg, was—

No.

He did not follow it. He put his hand flat on the stone and made himself look at the eleven fires as if they were eleven fires, and not a sum with only one possible answer. A dead man could not sell a road. Meera? He had named the cut to her himself, on the night of the parley. He counted her, and he struck her out in the same breath: she had been tied to him every hour since, and a woman could not sell what she could not walk away to sell. A living clerk might have copied a sheet. A guard might have talked. It was a long list of other things.

Beside him Meera said nothing. She had put on the snow goggles she had cut from his writ case, and she was watching him through the slit.

"What?" she said.

"Not yet."

His hands stayed warm. It was not a lie, exactly. It was a refusal to say what he thought, which cost nothing, and she knew it as well as he did.

"That's your road," she said quietly.

"It is not a road."

"It's the one you named. The stone fold and the shrine. You said it the night you fell."

"Yes."

"Then I'd say you've a bigger list of people who know it than you thought."

He looked at the shrine's small fire. She was on the list, and he had struck her off, and the list was still too long. "I would say the same," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dusk came down early and blue. In the hut the chulha ticked as it warmed, and Meera knelt at the shelf by the wall with the juniper bundle between her knees. The sticks were pale and dry and about the length of a forearm. She laid them on the shelf in a row and touched each one, moving her lips.

"Nineteen," she said. "The deadfall carried last night on one dry stick, and smoked us for it. Five for the night. That leaves fourteen."

"And eleven days to the Fair," said Rohan. "By my count."

"Mine too." She tied the bundle back. "Fourteen's under three nights. It's less if either of us has to ask something we don't want answering."

"We cannot stay here."

"No. We can't." She sat back. "Two days. The hunters are on your cut. By the day after tomorrow they're on the Saddle's lip, and we're standing in the one place they'll check. We go within two days, whatever the sky says."

"The sky is clear."

"It's clear now."

He did not argue. There was nothing to argue. He had a ruined lung, a left arm good for nothing, and a guide who had measured every risk since the gully and given him the odds out loud. One in four, one in three. He had learned to trust the figures because she had never once rounded them in her own favour.

She fed five sticks to the fire. The room warmed and filled with the clean bitter smell of juniper. He ate sattu from a wooden cup, and she ate it too, and neither of them touched the rest of Asha's bundle.

"My question," he said.

"Go on."

"The Salt Fox." He watched her face and kept his voice level. "You have crossed Shilak for ten winters. You know the crews. Do you know who leads it?"

It was the same question he had asked on the winter line, the night they were bound. He knew that, and so did she.

She did not answer. She sat with the cup in her lap, perfectly still, and the firelight lay along her cheek. Then she reached past him to the shelf, took a sixth stick, and laid it on the fire.

It caught with a crack.

"That's one," said Meera.

Thirteen. He did the sum without wanting to. A refusal cost one stick, and a stick was warmth, and warmth was the hours between them and the Saddle's lip. She would rather burn it than say a word.

There were two reasons a person did that. One was that the answer was no, and she did not care to be questioned. The other he did not follow, because he had refused one such thought already today and had no room for another. Instead he looked at the stick burning down, and at her bare hands round the cup, pink and steady in the heat. She had paid, and not for herself alone.

"I will not ask it again tonight," he said.

"You can ask whatever you like. It's on the account."

"I know." He set down the cup. "I would rather you were warm."

She glanced at him. Her thumb stopped on the rim of the cup and stayed there. She said, "My question, then."

"Yes."

"Why 'Guide'?"

He had expected it, some day. He had not expected it to feel like a hand on the back of the neck.

"You've called me that since the salt house," she said. "In the yard, in the gully, now. I've been called worse and I've been called better. A warden's got a dozen words for a woman on this road. Smuggler. Runner. Whatever's on the notice. You don't use any of them. I want to know why."

He was quiet a while. The truth was a plain thing. The difficulty lay in how much of it was his.

"Every other word for your trade is a word I write on a warrant," Rohan said. "Smuggler. Runner. The crew's name, the name the post gave it. They are names I have put on men before I hanged them. I do not mean to say them to you."

She did not move.

"It is not courtesy," he said. "I want that understood. I will do what the writ says at the thaw, and I will not pretend otherwise. But there is a name for what I may have to do to you, and a name for what you are on this mountain. 'Guide' is the second. You took a stranger with ribs like kindling across the Saddle, and you did not leave him. I will not call you by the other word while you are doing that."

The fire ticked.

He looked down at his own hands on his knees, and they stayed the colour of hands, and he understood that he had put them there to be looked at.

"It is true," he said, unnecessarily.

"I know," said Meera. "I watched."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The stove burned down to a red eye. Outside the wind had dropped to nothing, and the quiet had the ringing edge of deep cold.

He had not meant to move. It was the rope that did it. It lay slack between them across the packed dirt, the red wool dark in the dim light, and somewhere in the last hour his right hand had gone down and found it, as a man's hand will find a rail. It lay on the cord a hand's width from her wrist, where the knot sat, the one she had tied and he had tied back.

He knew where it was. He did not take it away.

She had seen it. Her breathing changed, a small, even catch, and she looked at the hand and then at the fire. Her own wrist lay on her knee, bare below the cuff of the glove. She did not draw it back.

Neither of them spoke. There was nothing to say that would have been free.

The cold at the door pressed in. The wool of the rope was dry and a little rough under his thumb, and the heat of her wrist reached him across that hand's width, faint, if he held still and noticed it, which he did. He thought: *this is the first thing she has allowed me.* He did not think anything after that.

Then she said, very low, "Listen."

He had heard it a moment before she spoke and had told himself it was the wind. But there was no wind. The sound came up from the dark slope below the hut, thin and carrying, the way sound carries in cold: a man's voice, young and hoarse with shouting.

"Left of the fold! Keep the cut on your right hand! There's a drop at the shrine, mind the drop!"

Meera had gone rigid. The hand beside the rope turned, very slowly, into a fist.

"Lalit," she said.

Rohan listened. Below the voice came the sound of boots on crust and the clink of iron, a whole line of men feeling their way upward in the dark. Then the calling again, patient and steady, picking the path as a man does who has walked it before.

*Keep the cut on your right hand.*

Asha's crew was not tracking the hunters. It was guiding them. And the road it was guiding them up was the one drawn on a single sheet of paper, in a neat hand, by a table in lamplight.

He did not move his hand from the rope. He found, to his lasting surprise, that she had not moved hers either.

# Chapter 14: Died on the Pass

Lalit's voice stopped, and the silence after it was worse.

Meera stood in the doorway with her fist still shut and listened to eleven fires climb the cut. Below the hut the cairn-light lay faint on the slope, a blue thread laid over the dark. Far down, a rope of orange points crept along the herdsmen's way. They were not hunting. They were being led.

"She said the light," she said. "She gave me the day and the next until the light went, and she hasn't waited a day past it."

"She is not bound to you," said Rohan behind her. "Her word cost her nothing."

"I know what her word cost." She turned back into the hut. "Lalit's calling the road like a man who's walked it. Somebody gave her your fallback, and she sold it on. That's all I've got, and I'll thank you not to ask me what I think of it."

"I did not intend to."

"Good. Come to the fire. I need your eyes and your opinion, in that order."

She knelt at the chulha, tore a clean leaf from the back of the count, and drew by the red light. The hut at the rock rib. The Saddle's lip. The cut, with its fires. The road, broad and obvious and, by morning, full.

"They come up the cut tonight and tomorrow," she said. "By the day after, they hold the lip and the road both. We can't go by the road. We can't go by the cut. That leaves the Teeth."

"The Teeth."

She drew a row of small jagged marks along the south-east shoulder, where the Saddle broke away into stone. "A spine of rock, steep as a roof. It drops two thousand feet to the Neshar side and comes out below the third bend, past the hunters' backs. Crews call it the Goat's Teeth because a goat could do it and nothing else could. Jassi named it. He hated it more than any place on earth."

The last word caught. She laid her palm flat on her coat, over the small dented bell buttoned there beside Kiran's note, and went on.

"It's three ledges, two ropes' worth of down-climbing, and a chimney with ice in it. A pair who knew it could do it in a day and a half. A pair with one good arm will want two."

"A day lost."

"A day lost, or the hunters. I'll take the day."

He studied the drawing for a long time. He did not argue. He asked about the ledges, how wide and how long, and where she would stand to belay him. She told him. He asked what she would do if he came off.

"Sit down hard and swear," said Meera. "You've been practising the sitting."

His mouth moved, barely. It was a smaller thing than a smile.

Something had bothered her since noon, and she got up and went to the door to look. The stars had been thick and cold an hour ago. Now they were going out in the west, not in clouds but in a slow greasy smear, as if someone had breathed on a window. Over the Long Saddle the sky had gone the colour of old tallow.

"What?" said Rohan.

"The sky's turning." She counted the missing stars under her breath. Eleven. Twelve. "I've seen that smear before a storm. It might hold off a day. It might be on us by morning."

"Then we go before it."

"We go before it, and we pray the Teeth are dry."

"I do not pray."

"No," said Meera. "I don't suppose the post allows it."

She shut the door and set the bar, then sat across the chulha from him with the plan between them. Tomorrow they would be two people on a rope, and the night belonged to them still.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She banked the coals rather than feed them. Then she reached for the branch of wet juniper deadfall she had dragged in at dusk to dry on the stones. It was meant for tomorrow's kettle, and it was the only wood in the hut that wasn't counted.

"Another round," she said.

"We have both asked tonight. I the matter of the name, you the matter of 'Guide'."

"This buys one for each of us." She laid the damp branch across the coals. It hissed, then sulked, then gave out a thin sour smoke and almost no heat. "That's the price. A night colder, and no kettle at first light. I'm tired of rules that cost me sleep, so I'll pay for the one I want." She leaned her back against the shelf. "Why keep the law, Captain?"

He did not answer at once. He had the pattu round his shoulders and the red rope across his knee, and his right hand had found the knot in it again.

"You have an answer to that," he said. "Everyone does."

"I've got mine. Mine's ten winters old and has a grave in it. I want yours."

"Then you shall have it, though you will not like it." He looked at the fire. "My grandfather died on the pass."

She said nothing.

"It was sixty winters ago, the winter before the Closing Law. He was a warden of the Shilak post, before the post had a hall. I never knew him. My father told it as a short story, the way men tell what hurts. There was a storm, and he went out at night to check the cairns for a lost party, and a whiteout took him. They found him at the thaw. The law was written the next spring." He turned the rope a quarter turn. "My father said it was for him. So that no one else would go out on that mountain in a winter storm to bring in a fool who had no business there. I believe it was an accident. I think it very likely that it was a stupid one. He was a good man doing a thing he need not have done."

"So no one dies that way," she said.

"No one on my watch. Not a warden. Not a fool. Not—" He stopped, and she saw him decide to finish. "Not a boy like Dilan, chasing a runner onto the ice in the dark, because a runner was on the ice at all."

"The runner was on the ice because a sick village was on the other side."

"I know that."

"Do you?"

"I know it now," said Rohan. "I did not know it as I know it this week. I said the law keeps the strong from the passes. It does. Pritam would have the Shilak open to his grain wagons by midwinter if it did not. I said it keeps my wardens off the mountain. It does, a little. I have buried seven. I know the tally of both."

"My mother died of the grey cough in a closed winter," Meera said. "Ten winters ago. The medicine was in a lowland shop three days off, and the law said no one crossed, and no one did. That's my tally. One."

"One is enough."

"For me. It's not enough for you, though, is it? You'd weigh her against seven wardens and the grain wagons." She heard her own voice go flat, which meant it was close to shaking. "You'd do the sum, and she'd come out light."

"I would not do the sum with her in it."

"You'd have to."

"Yes," said Rohan. He looked up and met her eyes, and he did not look away. "That is what I am telling you. I would have to. And I have done it, and signed it, and hanged a boy by it. I do not say the sum is kind. I say it is the only sum I have, and that I begin to think the sum is wrong, and I do not know yet what to put in its place."

The banked fire settled. The wind had come up outside, and the roof beam creaked under it.

She had come to the question with a place ready to put his answer. A cruel man would have fit it. A stupid one would have fit it. It did not fit a man who had stood on a mountain with a dead man's rope in his coat and said *begin to think*.

The law's hand and the law's victim sat in one ledger, and she had no column for a man like this.

"Your turn," she said, and her voice came out rough. "Ask."

He thought about it. She watched him pick one question up and put it down, and pick up another.

"If the law were gone," said Rohan. "Tomorrow. Struck off the roll. No peg, no notice, no warden on the Saddle. What would you do?"

She had thought about that more than once in ten winters and never said the answer. She said it now.

"Sleep."

He blinked.

"That's it?"

"That's the whole thing." She laughed, short and surprised, and it hurt in her chest. "I'd carry the medicine over in daylight, on the road, with a mule. I'd get home before dark. I wouldn't count anyone's fingers. I wouldn't hide a packet in a wool bale or go to bed with a boot on in case the salt house door got kicked. I'd put my head down and sleep through a night." She rubbed her eyes with the heel of her glove. "Ten winters. I can't remember it."

"Sleep," said Rohan, as if testing a word in another language.

"Don't."

"I am not laughing."

"You are. Your mouth is."

"It is a very small laugh." He ducked his head, and for the first time she saw the line of his throat move without a cough behind it. It was not an official face. She did not think anyone had seen it in a long while.

She put it in the count in her head, where the other things went.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She let the quiet sit for a while. Then she asked the thing she had carried since the yard.

"There's a widow in Kothi Shilak," she said. "Puja. Her husband had a lean-to behind the house, and the patrol found salt in it the autumn he died. They fined her eighteen silver, and she hadn't eighteen coppers. The night before we crossed, she had it, and she wouldn't say from where." She kept her eyes on the fire. "She told me in the yard before dawn that a cousin brought it. She has no cousin with eighteen silver. And I watched you leave her step."

He did not move.

"And there was Ketan's mother. You said it yourself. And I'd lay money there are others." She waited. "Do you pay the fines of the poor families, Captain?"

That made two questions from her in one round, and she knew it. The bought round was spent. He owed her nothing, and a refusal would not have been charged.

Rohan looked at the rope across his knee, as a man looks at a thing he has been asked to carry in front of a crowd.

He did not answer.

She knew what he would do; she had learned it over nine days. He leaned and took a stick from the shelf. It was one of the pale, dry ones, and he held it a moment, then laid it on the coals without a word. It caught and burned.

"That's twelve," said Meera.

"Yes."

His hands lay in the light, easy and warm, steady, the nails pink as hers. There was no lie in them, because he had not told one. He had spent a stick of juniper he need not have spent rather than say a thing aloud.

She had hated him for three winters on the strength of a notice nailed to a gatepost and two pale pegs on a tree. She had no wish to stop. It would have been easier. But the hatred had a hole in it now, the exact size of a man who paid fines in the dark, and every honest thing she had learned this week fit into it.

"You don't have to say," she said quietly. "I'm not asking you to."

"I know."

"I know too, then."

He did look at her at that. She had not meant it as an answer, and he took it as one, and his shoulders eased as if a rope had been paid out an inch.

"Thank you," said Rohan, "for not saying it for me."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The fire had burned low and the room had shrunk around it. At some point she had moved. Or he had. She was sitting on her heels at the edge of the hearth stones and he was beside her, and the rope between their wrists had gone slack and curled on the dirt like a sleeping thing. She could see the pulse in his neck. She could smell camphor from the pattu and juniper smoke and, underneath, the clean tired smell of a man who had been ill and was not now.

His hands had caught her eye in the gully, and again on the sill, when he took the bowline for the ninth time. An hour ago he had laid one on the cord a hand's width from hers and had not taken it away, and she had told herself it was nothing.

Now he was looking at her mouth.

He did not lean. He sat still as he had sat for the Saddle's wind, giving her the whole of the choice. There was a breath's width between them, and he was not going to cross it.

She lifted her hand and laid it along his jaw. The stubble rasped against her glove. His eyes closed, briefly, as if he had braced for a blow and found only a touch.

"Guide," he said, very low.

She was close enough to feel it. She could have done it. Her heart was going in her throat like a goat on a ledge, and there was a hunger in it she had not given room to in years, because it was a luxury and she did not keep luxuries.

*Say something true,* she thought. *It'll be free.*

The truest thing she owned was not the hunger. She was the Salt Fox. She had known it since the third empty peg on the hanging tree in Kothi Shilak. And an hour ago, with his hands warm in the firelight, he had said, *I will do what the writ says at the thaw.* He had not yet joined that name to her face. He read files eleven times over; he would.

She took her hand away.

"No," said Meera.

He opened his eyes. He did not look hurt. He looked, if anything, as if he had expected it and wanted to be told the reason.

"I'm not saying it's hate," she said, and made herself hold his gaze, because anything less would be a small lie in its own right. "It was, a week ago. It isn't now. I'd like you to know that, since you like things written down." She drew a breath. "But you said the writ comes due at the thaw. You said it with your own mouth and I watched your hands stay warm. So at the thaw you deliver me. I'll walk beside you down this mountain, and I'll hold your rope, and I'll tell you the truth when I can afford it. But I don't kiss my noose."

The fire cracked.

Rohan's eyes went still on the word *me*. It lasted half a breath. Then they moved on, to the fire, and his face gave her nothing at all.

She had said it plain, and on purpose. Tomorrow he would be hanging on her rope above two thousand feet of air. He could choose his next step knowing that she knew what he carried.

"That is a better reason than the last one," he said at last.

"I didn't give you a last one."

"You gave me a week of them. They were all about what I am." He lowered his head. "This is about what I will do. It is a fair reason, and I cannot answer it, because it is true."

"You could promise," she said. It slipped out. "You could say you won't."

"I could. And the mountain would hold me to it." His voice was steady, and gentle, and it was the gentleness that made her throat tight. "I will not promise what I do not know I can keep, Meera. Not to you. Not when it would cost you my hands."

It was her name, plain, with no *Guide* in front. He did not seem to notice. She noticed for both of them.

"Sleep," she said, a little hoarsely. "We go before light."

"Yes."

"I'll take the shelf. You take the floor."

"That is the second time you have given me the floor."

"It's a good floor. It's very flat."

"It has found every rib I own," said Rohan, and she nearly laughed, and had to turn her back to lie down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She woke in the dark with the rope tugging at her wrist, one long pull, *come*, and he was at the door with the bar already lifted.

He did not speak. He only stood aside.

The wind had gone. That was the first wrong thing. After a night of rising wind, the Saddle lay in a silence so complete that the snow seemed to be holding its breath. The cairn-light on the slope had thinned to a single blue thread.

The second wrong thing was the west.

It stood over the Long Saddle like a wall, black at the base and bruise-yellow along the crest, and it did not look like cloud. It looked like a cliff that had decided to walk. The top of it caught a colourless light from somewhere, and below that the whole range had vanished, stripped out, as if a hand had wiped a slate. A single low sound came out of it, the groan of a very large thing shifting its weight.

Far down the slope, thin and small, a two-note horn answered it. Falling, falling.

"Twelve sticks," Meera said, because her mouth needed something to count. "Two days down the Teeth, with your arm, if the ledges hold."

"And the wall?"

"Hours." She took the pack from beside the door and slung it on, and the bell shifted against her ribs and rang once, soft and dented. "Maybe less. Rope up, Captain. We're going down into it."

# Chapter 15: The Goat's Teeth

Iron rang on stone somewhere below the second ledge. Three clean strikes, a pause, three more.

Rohan went flat against the rock and raised his right fist. Above him, Meera stopped paying out the rope at once. They had come onto the Goat's Teeth at first light, and for two hours she had fed him down the spine as if he were a bucket in a well. He had stopped noticing her hands on the line. It was the best thing a man could say about a belay.

"Picks," she said, low, coming down the last pitch to the ledge. "Two pairs, by the sound. They're in the chimney."

"They were told the Teeth."

"Or they guessed. Every crew on the salt road knows what a goat can climb." She kept her eyes west, where the black wall stood on the edge of the sky, no nearer than at dawn and no farther. "Four hours before that breaks. Three, if it's in a hurry. I'd like to be off this rock before it decides."

"Then I had better be quick."

"You'd better be tidy. There's a plate of ice under your left heel."

He shifted his weight before he had finished hearing it.

The ledge was two strides long and a yard wide, with a rock horn at the south end and the chimney's mouth at the north. A man coming up the chimney had to come over the lip alone. Rohan put the horn at his back and pinned his left arm against his side. A warden took the weapon first, the wrist second and the life never, unless the writ named it. He had taught that to forty men. He had not taught it with two cracked ribs.

The first hand came over the lip. Then a hood, and a broad face under it. The man saw Rohan and did not look surprised.

"Captain," he said, almost pleasantly. "There's a lordship on your head."

"So I am told. Come up slowly."

The man did not. A hand-axe came up on a wrist loop in a short, hard arc. Rohan took the haft across his right forearm, turned in under the swing and rolled the wrist outward until the fingers had to open. The axe dropped into his palm. He threw it over the edge and heard nothing land. The man's other fist caught him on the cheekbone. Rohan let it. Dodging would have cost him a rib.

The second hunter came over the lip with a short sword already drawn. The tarred climbing line between the two men, three arm-spans of it, snagged on the first man's boot, and the sword came in crooked across his partner's back.

"Left!" Meera shouted from above. "He's low, Captain, *left*!"

He was already going. He trapped the sword arm against the horn, laid the heel of his right hand on the wrist and bore down over the stone edge. The bone gave with a small, dry sound. The sword rang on the ledge. The man screamed once and bit it off.

Rohan stepped on the line between them and held it there.

"Yield," he said. His breath sawed. "Both of you. Now."

They yielded. The first man sat down with his empty hand in his lap, and the second folded round his wrist and rocked. Neither looked like a man who had been promised a lordship. They looked cold, and young under their beards.

"The ice was real," Rohan said, up the rope. He did not know why he said it.

"I know," said Meera. "I read it."

That was all, and it was enough. For a moment he had stood on a yard of stone with a sword at his back, and a woman he did not trust with his name had told him where to put his feet. He had put them there without arguing.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He searched the men for knives and orders. The second carried a flint and a clasp-knife and nothing else. The first, the broad one, wore a leather purse at his belt, and in it were three silver pieces, a pinch of salt in a rag, and a twist of waxed paper no longer than a thumb, tied off with thread.

He knew it before he had it open. Powder, rust-red, and a smell like old tea and bitter bark. Redbark.

He turned the twist to the light. Along the fold, pressed into the wax, was a faint round mark, a ring within a ring. It was the print of a silk sieve in the guild stores in Taravan, where crown medicine was sifted and weighed. He had seen that same ring on a wrapper in Meera's oilcloth roll. He had told himself then that it meant nothing, that crown-grade bark could be had by anyone with enough silver.

A regent's hunter did not buy medicine for a sore chest. This was a crown packet, and it was not in a crown store.

He closed his fist on it. Meera was looking down the chimney, not at him. He put the twist inside his coat, against the shirt, and said nothing, because he did not yet know what the saying would cost.

"Captain." Her voice had changed. "Come and look at this, and tell me I'm wrong."

Below the throat of the chimney the second pair had stopped. Two hoods, forty feet down, their picks driven into the ice. One of them had a hand up to his eyes, looking at the ledge.

"They've seen the others," said Rohan.

"They've seen the others went quiet." She pointed across the chimney with her axe. "Look up there."

A tongue of snow hung off the shoulder on the far side, grey-white and smooth as a plastered wall. Along its top lip ran a thin black crack. "That's slab. The wind built it in the last storm and it's been sitting on a skin of nothing ever since. Hear that?" She struck it with the haft from the end of the ledge, and it answered with a flat, hollow note, like a drum with no skin.

He understood. "You can bring it down."

"I can cut it. I can't tell you how far it runs." She counted it out as she would count goats. "Five in six it dumps itself in the throat and stops. One in six it takes the chimney and the ledge and us. And it's what's between us and a fight I'd rather not have."

He thought of the other slide, and of Jassi. Meera was thinking of it too. She laid her palm on her coat, over the small dented bell, and the hand was not quite steady.

"You were the one who read the gully," Rohan said. "I would be a fool to argue the slope."

"You are a fool. A tidy one." But the corner of her mouth moved. "Take my rope, and keep it short. If I shout, sit down hard."

"And swear."

"I'll do the swearing."

She went out along the north end of the ledge, as far as eight arm-spans would let her, and he paid the line out with his heel dug into the stone. She did not look back. She set the adze of her axe in the crack, leaned on it, and the crack ran. Then she stepped back, once, and kicked.

The slab slid off the shoulder with a long sigh. It was not the roar he remembered. It slid, bowed and broke, and poured into the chimney's throat in a grey rush, packing as it fell. Spindrift blew across the ledge and stung his face. Below, a voice cried out, and a pick rang, and then there was only the settling hiss, and a wall of tight white snow where the chimney had been.

No blue light had stirred. It was snow, and her knowledge of it, and the stone it hung on. He had not thought to be glad of that.

She came back along the ledge with her face grey. "They'll dig," she said. "It'll take them till dark. I didn't want them under it." She took a breath. "I didn't."

"I saw," said Rohan.

"There's a price in it." She nodded at the white plug. "That chimney was our road down. I've shut it along with theirs. We climb back to the hut and sit out the storm, and look for another line when it clears."

"Then we climb," said Rohan.

The two hunters on the ledge had watched all of it without a word. He knelt and splinted the broken wrist with the man's own scabbard and a strip of his cloak, because it was what a warden did. Then he stood, and they looked up at him.

"The storm will be here within the hour," he said. "Your friends will dig through by evening. Get into the lee of that rock, put on both cloaks between you, and keep each other awake." He had no better counsel. It was not enough, and he knew it, and they knew it.

"Captain," said the broad one, as Meera drew the rope tight. "You'll not make the Neshar side."

"Nor you," said Rohan. "Not this week."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wall reached them on the climb back.

First came the wind, a clean cold shove that nearly took Rohan off the second pitch. Then the light went. It went the way a lamp does when a hand is laid over it, and the Saddle above them turned the colour of tin and then of nothing. Snow came sideways, fine as flour. He could not see the rock he gripped. He could not see his own hand.

He could see the rope. A short curve of red wool went up into the white, and at its end was a woman's wrist.

"Cairn-light!" Meera shouted. He barely heard her. "Watch for the blue, Captain! It'll come!"

It came. Faint at first, a thread laid over the nothing, and then thicker, a line of cold sapphire running up the spine of the Teeth and across the Saddle's shoulder. He had never seen it so near. He set each boot where the blue touched the rock. Every breath caught on the seventh and eighth ribs like a hook in a sleeve, and his left shoulder burned from the socket down. The thread did not waver, and the mountain, which had been trying to kill them all morning, closed its hand at last round the path and let them walk it.

How long it took he could not have said. Hours, perhaps. When the door of the Rope-Hut came out of the whiteness it was dark already, and the bare nail above it was furred with ice.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Meera barred the door with both hands and leaned her back on it.

"Light," Rohan said. He was down on one knee by the chulha, unable to rise. "I will do it. You have the flint."

"I've the flint." She held it up between finger and thumb, and it dropped. She looked at it on the dirt. She tried to pick it up, and her fingers would not close.

He saw her hands then. She had stripped her gloves for the axe work, for the slab and the knots in the chimney, and she had not put them back on in the wind. The skin was swollen and a hard, shining red, split across both knuckles. A strip of the haft's varnish was ground into the palm. She held them in front of her like things belonging to another woman.

"Give me your hands," said Rohan.

"It's ordinary cold. Not the other kind." Her voice was thin. "I'd know."

"I know. That is why I am asking."

She did not move. He took off the pattu, opened his coat, and then the shirt beneath it, on the right side where the ribs were sound. The air struck his skin like a slap. He held out his own hands, palms up, and waited. It was the first thing in ten days he had asked of her without a writ behind it.

She put her hands in his.

They were as cold as the stones of the cairn. He drew them in against his bare side and held them there, her palms flat on his ribs below the arm, and he did not flinch, though it was a near thing.

He braced anyway. It was an old, absurd habit, the way a man braces for a cold plate. If there had been lie-cold in her hands, he would have taken it through the skin and known at once that she had lied to him, and how greatly, and nothing else. He had held them anyway. He found he could bear that. There was only the ordinary cold, slow and honest, draining out of her into him.

"That hurts," said Meera.

"It will for a while."

"I wasn't complaining."

"You are doing it very well."

She made a sound that might have been a laugh. The blood was coming back into her fingers, and he felt it as a faint pulse against his side and then a tremor. She hissed through her teeth and did not pull away. Her forehead came down, briefly, against his shoulder, the width of a breath, and lifted again.

"This isn't—" she said.

"No," said Rohan. "I do not think it is."

She did not say what it was. She sat with her palms flat against him till the red had turned to pink, and the pink to the colour of hands. Then she took the flint from the dirt, struck it, and lit the chulha with a single stick of the juniper. The fire caught and held. The warmth reached her face.

She looked at her fingers in the glow, and flexed them. "Fire warms this kind," she said. "And so does a hand."

"Both work," said Rohan.

"Don't make a speech."

"I had not planned one."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She counted the sticks by firelight. She laid them in a row on the dirt against the wall beside the door, the one stretch of floor the roof drip never reached, because wet juniper smoked and she would not burn smoke. She touched each one with a finger that finally obeyed her.

"Eleven," said Meera. "One to light. Eleven left, and a night of storm."

He did not answer. He was thinking of the twist inside his coat, and of the ring pressed into its wax. He had held it back on the ledge for no better reason than that he did not know how to hold it. Now there was a fire, and her warm hands, and he had no excuse left. He took it out and laid it on the hearth stone between them.

"I took this from the broad man's purse," he said. "Before the slide."

She looked at it. For a time she did not touch it. Then she bent close, picked it up, and turned the fold to the fire with her thumb along the wax.

"Redbark," she said. She held very still. "It's the guild's silk. That's the sieve ring, there, same as—"

She stopped. She looked up at him, and then, quickly, away, to the oilcloth roll beside her pack.

"The redbark in my pack and that purse came from the same—"

The door burst inward.

The bar did not break so much as leave, its old frost-split giving at last. The whole slab of oak went over on its hinges with a groan like a deodar splitting in a hard frost, and the night came in on top of it, white and howling. The chulha flared and went flat. Ash and sparks flew across the floor. Rohan saw the row of sticks by the wall lift and scatter, rolling and skidding, and the wind took them as a hand takes a fistful of straws and swept them across the sill, out into the dark.

"The wood!" Meera was already moving.

He was moving too. The rope snapped taut between them. She had dived for the door, and he was a pace behind, and the first stick had gone past the sill and was sliding downhill toward the felled juniper, a pale line against the black. Behind it went another, and another, and the snow rose to meet them.

Eleven sticks. And past eight arm-spans of rope, the mountain would not let either of them go to fetch them.

# Chapter 16: What the Storm Kept

The third stick skidded over the sill, and the wind took it down the slope like a thrown knife.

"The wood!" Rohan shouted.

"Together!" Meera was already through the door. "Same pace, rope short! Stay on my left!"

They went out shoulder to shoulder. Snow came flat across the slope and filled her mouth. The cairn-light was out there, a blue thread under the white, and by its glow she saw the pale sticks skidding toward the wind-felled juniper. She went down on her knees and swept her arm through the drift. One. Two. Rohan crouched beside her, working one-handed, his left arm clamped to his ribs. He dragged a stick from the snow and shoved it inside his coat, then reached for the next.

"Four," she yelled. "Five!"

The sixth was wedged in the juniper's root plate. The seventh lay half buried under her own knee. After that there was nothing. She felt along the trunk until the wind drove her off, and then she watched the rest go. Four pale lines slid over the lip of the slope into the dark, and the dark took them.

"Seven," said Rohan in her ear. His breath rattled, but it was only the ribs. "Meera. Seven is what there is."

"I can count."

"Then count inside."

Eight spans of rope ran between the two of them, not out from the sill, and that was the only reason the mountain had let them go so far from the door. They went in. It took both of them to haul the oak slab back onto its hinges. She wedged the split bar against it, lashed the halves with Jassi's goat-hair cord, and jammed her pack against the foot. The wind still found the gaps. It whistled through the seam like a kettle.

She sat down on the hearth stones and found she was shaking, and not from cold.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She laid the sticks across her knees where the snow couldn't get at them. Seven pale lengths of juniper, each as long as her forearm.

"Five for the night," she said. "That's what a storm night costs when the fire's honest and the door's bad. Two left for morning. After that, the deadfall. Wet wood, and no way to keep it lit."

"And if it blows on past dawn?"

"Then we sit." She turned a stick over. "That's the wood. There's another sum, and it's the one that matters."

Rohan had set himself against the wall across from her with the pattu round his shoulders. The fire put red along his cheek and none in his eyes.

"Say it."

"Fire doesn't warm lie-cold." Her voice went level, the way it did at a cornice. "So a lie tonight means a night of shaking beside a fire that can't help. We can't pay for that on top of the storm. No lies, Captain. Not a small one. Not a joke that isn't quite a joke. Nothing but the true thing, and we spend what we have to."

"That is a harder rule than the firewood."

"It's the same rule. We just never said so."

He looked at the fire. He looked at her hands, lying open and ordinary in her lap. "Then I spend my question for the night," he said slowly, "and I will not waste it. Who is the Salt Fox?"

The wind leaned on the door.

A refusal would burn a sixth stick, and a sixth was tomorrow's. She had set that rule herself, days back, to make evasion cost something. It had never cost this much.

She had rehearsed the answer for ten days. In the rehearsals it came out as a speech, with the crew's reasons ranked in order and a dying brother at the end for weight. None of that came now. What came was the deodar outside the Kothi gate, and its pegs, one for every hanging, two of them pale from last winter. There was a third hole, the empty one. She had counted it every morning for three winters.

Truth was warm. That was the trap in it. She could not buy her way out of this with wood.

"I am the Salt Fox," said Meera.

The fire ticked. She watched her own fingers, because that was where a lie would show, and they stayed pink and warm and bent a little at the split knuckle. Outside, the storm howled and nothing in the hut answered it.

"It isn't a crew," she said. "Not the way your post files it. It's me. I run it. I've run it ten winters. The packets in my pack are mine and the debt's mine, and every salt-house cellar you've seized was one of my doors." She did not stop. "That's the true thing. You can do what you want with it."

Rohan did not move. Silence was free, and he spent it. It ran on until a log cracked in it. She studied the knot of the rope at her wrist, because she could not look at his face.

"I suspected," he said at last.

"You didn't know."

"I did not. I will be exact, since exactness costs nothing here. You said *you deliver me* in this room, and I have been trying very carefully not to hear it since. I suspected and I chose not to know. That is not the same as ignorance, but it is not far from it."

"Why?"

"Because if I knew, I would have to write it."

She breathed out slowly. It was not what she had braced for. She had braced for the warden, the iron voice. She had not braced for a man who had spent a week declining to open a letter.

"My question," she said. "What does your writ say?"

"Yes." He sat forward, wincing. "It orders me to deliver the Salt Fox to the warden-judge at Taravan at the thaw. Whoever it is. Alive, if the mountain permits."

His fingers stayed pink in the firelight. Nothing in them changed.

"Then that's both of us," said Meera. Her mouth was dry. "The two things that make us enemies, said aloud, and neither of us froze."

"No." He turned his hands over on his knees, palms up, and let her look. "Still warm."

"Say what you are, then. All of it."

"I am the man who must hand you to the rope. You are the woman I must hand over. And I am sitting here warm."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The twist of redbark lay where she had dropped it, by the hearthstone, one corner scorched by flying ash. She picked it up. Her hands were steady now, which she resented. She set a packet from her own roll beside it, wax fold turned up, so the two sieve rings lay together in the red light.

"Same ring," she said. "Same guild wrapper. And my crates came to me with the crown stamp scraped off the lids." She heard how it sounded. "I buy from a lowland middleman I have never met. His carter leaves me a brass button at each drop. The next courier carries its twin, or no crate moves. I thought it was a good arrangement. I never once asked why it was so good."

Rohan's face changed. "A brass button. Small, domed, plain."

"Why?"

"Varun has a new one on his coat. Brass, among pewter. He took it from the quartermaster's tin of odds, he said, after he lost a pewter one on the stair." He looked at the fire. "I remember because he grumbled all that week about the chalky polish, and never once about the button."

The storm hit the door and went on.

She dug past the packets and brought out the waxed notebook. The count. She found the page by feel and held it to the fire. "Asha's tracker called your party across the ridge. I wrote it down because numbers are what I do. *Three wardens, two goat-folk.*"

Rohan read it over the edge of the page. His mouth went thin. "I struck the third name before we rode. I named two in the yard. The only paper that ever said three was Varun's sheet."

"I heard iron before the first crack," said Meera. "One clean ring of it, on stone. I thought it was the mountain. It was a pick."

"And Varun picked the gully over your odds." Rohan's voice had gone quiet. "He dropped back for a strap. Just before. I thought he trusted me to go on, and took it for kindness."

"The hunters on the Teeth had picks. The same ring." She said it like a count, flat and fast. "He set the slide, Captain. And he's my middleman."

"Yes," said Rohan. "He is."

He touched his collar. "And the Teeth. The pairs came up the one chimney that lets out behind the stone fold, on the herdsmen's cut. That is my fallback. Varun drew it, in his own hand, on a single sheet, with my ruler. Only the two of us ever saw it. And what they would pay a lordship for is the queen's seal. You have held it. The cord was tied my way when I woke, but snugged harder than I snug it, and I have wondered who taught you the hitch. I told Varun I carried it. I told the queen. No one else alive."

Neither of them spoke after that. Meera turned the count back to its first page, the first drop, ten winters gone, and ran her thumb down the column of dates. One scraper's hand on every lid. The fire settled in the grate.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Ten winters I've paid that button," said Meera. She hadn't meant to speak. "Every season, silver for the drop. Honest silver, most of it from the rich merchants who pay double, so the poor cross free. It all went to the middleman. To Varun. And Varun belongs to the regent."

"Meera."

"My lifeline was your enemy's purse." The words came out in her own flat counting voice. "The medicine keeping Ranu alive, and Kiran, was bought with money that went into Pritam's coat. I've been paying for the coup you're riding to stop. Every winter. For the price of a village's cough."

"You did not know."

"Jassi didn't know either. And he's dead." She pressed the heel of her hand against one eye. "That's the sum, Captain. Don't tell me it's different."

"It is not different. Hear mine."

She nodded.

"I have known Varun ten years. I stood at his wedding. He was the first man at my shoulder when Dilan died, and the last to leave the hall after I swore my oath over that rope." He turned back the front of his coat, and the clean-cut end of Dilan's rope showed in the lining, grey and neat. "I gave him my route. I gave him the seal. I told him the one fact that would hang me. I trusted the man who killed Jassi, and tonight I would have called him dead and grieved."

"You didn't know either."

"I am not asking pardon." He touched the cut end of the rope and left his fingers there. "We fed the same hand. Your silver. My faith."

She laughed once, a cracked sound. It did not feel like a joke. It felt like a latch letting go.

"Four crates in the salt-house top room," she said. "All four lids scraped clean. I thought a clerk had been careless. Somebody scraped them so no warden would read a crown stamp. Somebody who knew which wardens read stamps."

"He chose the gully," said Rohan. "I thanked him for it." He nodded at the red rope on the dirt between them. "And the mountain has been telling me since the cairn that you were honest. I kept filing it under something else."

Sparks lifted from the fire. The wind eased for a moment and rose again.

She had two choices, then. She could go on sitting on her side of the hearth with the whole truth lying between them. Or she could stop.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She got up and crossed the hearth.

She did it on purpose, one step and then the next, so that neither of them could call it a stumble. She knelt on the stones a hand's breadth from him, and he did not pretend the distance was an accident either.

"I told you once I don't kiss my noose," she said. "I meant it, and you didn't even know what you were asking. This time I'm choosing it. I know exactly who you are, and what you carry for me."

He said nothing. His breathing caught at the ribs.

"Tell me why I shouldn't," she said.

"You have the reasons," said Rohan. "I have given you every one. At the thaw I must take you to Taravan. You would be kissing the man who carries your rope." His voice dropped. "I would be kissing the Salt Fox, and I would be a warden who knew it."

"That's all of it?"

"That is all of it, and it is enough to stop me. I will not move. You will tell me which."

She put her hand against his jaw and did not take it away.

He kissed like a man reading a document he had waited a long time to sign. Slow, and careful, and entirely there. His right hand came up and settled at the back of her head, the way he might have taken a wrist to feel for a pulse. She tasted smoke and the thin bitterness of redbark on his breath. Her fingers dug into the pattu at his collar, and her split knuckles stung against the wool, and she let them. The rope lay warm across both their wrists, and it pulled gently, as if the mountain were leaning in to listen.

When they parted, neither of them went far. Her forehead rested against his. His breath was steady and not quite even.

"That," said Meera, "was free."

"I know," he said. "I watched your hands."

She laughed against his mouth. Then the laughter stopped, because he had gone quiet, and his eyes had left hers for the rope.

"Meera. Listen."

"I'm listening."

"I will not let you fall on this mountain." He put both hands on his knees where she could see them. "Not the Teeth, nor the Saddle, nor any ledge between here and Ranu. If the ground goes, I will be under you."

The rope hummed.

It came first through her wrist, a low, level note, rising out of the red wool as if the whole length of it had been struck. The stones in the wall kept silent. The sound ran up her arm and into her teeth, and it did not climb or crack. It stayed even and held, like a hand laid flat on a table.

Rohan's fingers were warm. They had stayed warm all the way through it.

"You shouldn't have," she said. Her voice shook. "A promise made while bound is a statement of intent. If you break it—"

"I know the rule."

"Then you know what you've done."

"I have made a promise I mean to keep," said Rohan. "I would not have made it otherwise."

She sat back on her heels and looked at him by the light of the dying fire. She read mountains for a living, counting what a slope cost and what it gave back. She read him that way now, and the answer came without any help from her.

A broken promise struck like a great lie, and a great lie took flesh. Until the thaw, his hands, his body, were tied to her footing.

And the thaw was the day he had to hand her to the rope.

She did not say so. There was nothing left that was free to say. She put her hand over his, and under the wind and the humming wool, she counted: five sticks for the night, two for morning, and a man who had just promised her the one thing he was ordered to take away.

# Chapter 17: Below the Snowline

Rohan had her pack on his right shoulder and one boot laced before she opened her eyes.

The storm had gone out in the dark. He had felt it go, the way a man feels a fever break, and had lain on the sleeping shelf listening to the quiet until grey light came through the door seam. Now the hut smelled of cold ash and wet wool, and Meera lay with the red rope across her chest, one hand curled by her face.

She woke all at once, as she always did. Her eyes went to the pack, then to him.

"Put it down."

"It is forty pounds, and you carried it through the Teeth. My right arm is sound." He kept his voice reasonable. "Let me take the door as well. Let me kick the drift clear and test the sill before you put a foot on it."

"Captain."

"A cornice can sit against a door. I would rather learn that with my boot."

"Rohan." She sat up. It was the first time she had used the name when she meant to be heard, and he stopped with the strap in his fist. "Put it down. Your promise doesn't make you my guide."

"I did not say it did."

"You're doing it with a pack on your shoulder." She pulled her boots toward her. "I heard you last night. The rope heard you. You'll be under me if I fall. That's a promise about falling. It isn't about where I put my feet."

He set the pack on the shelf. It cost him something, and he was surprised by how much. For ten days he had reached for the downhill place on the rope and for the first step through every door, and each time she had stopped him, with a look and nothing more. It was not vanity. It was the oldest rule of his trade, and it was what *safe* meant to him.

"If you fall because I let you lead—" he began.

"Then I fell on my own line, and you caught me. That's the whole bargain." She laced up with fingers that obeyed her again. "Two sticks for the morning. I'm spending one on the sattu and keeping one. Eat."

They ate. Then Meera drew the door and stepped through first, and he let her. Outside, the world had been scoured. Every slope lay clean and blue-white, and the Saddle's lip stood against a sky so hard it looked glazed. She stood on the sill with her eyes narrowed to slits behind the strip of boiled leather she had cut from his writ case for goggles.

"The chimney's plugged," she said. "So we take the winter line itself. Down the south slope to the Neshar road, third bend. Half a day, if I read the snow right, and I will."

"And the hunters?"

"Digging. They'll come out onto the Teeth, find nobody, and cast east. I'd cast east." She tested the rope between them with a flick of her wrist. "Behind me, Captain. Uphill of me, always. Where you can sit down on the line."

Behind. Uphill. He saw why she wanted it. A man below her could only fall with her. A man above her could be an anchor. His reach for the downhill side had been an annoyance to her yesterday. With his promise humming in the wool, it was a danger.

He walked behind.

For an hour it was easy, and he hated it. She picked her way down the long slope in short, flat steps, stopping to probe with the axe haft. He wanted to call out every hollow she was already stepping around. He bit it back and watched the back of her neck, the stray black hair freezing against her collar.

Where the slope rolled over into a shallow bowl, a clean, smooth stretch opened to the right, an easy shortcut, and his body began to take it. One stride off her line, and he had a good view of the way. The rope went slack in his favour.

Three short pulls.

He stopped with his weight forward. Meera did not turn her head.

"Look at your left boot," she said.

He looked. The snow under it was hard and pale, and it seemed sound. She came back up the rope, knelt, and tapped the surface once with her axe. A square of crust the size of a door sank away with a soft, sighing sound, and under it was a black hollow where wind had undercut the slope. The hollow went down further than the haft was long.

"You'd have gone through to the hip," said Meera. "And I'd have gone after you, because that's how a rope works." She said it without heat. "That's not a lesson. It's a cost. You're paying part of it now by standing still when you want to be ahead."

"I am paying," said Rohan. His voice was not quite steady. "I had not understood the price was this."

"What did you think it was?"

"That I would be the one at risk. In your place." He stepped back onto her line, in her footprints, exactly. "I see now that keeping it means doing what you tell me. I had not thought of it as obedience."

"It's not obedience." She stood and brushed snow off her knees. "It's being on the same rope. I'd do the same for you if you were the one who knew the ground."

"Would you?"

"Ask me again when you do."

She went on. He followed, and it was not as hard the second time.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They stopped at noon in the lee of a boulder, where the sun had melted a hand's breadth of rock bare. Meera sat with her back to it and chewed sattu without tasting it. He could tell by how she held her jaw. When she spoke, she kept her eyes off him.

"I want to ask you something I already know the answer to."

"Ask."

"You hold the writ." She pulled up a knee. "It names the Salt Fox. If you put me down in your report as dead on the mountain, or never found, or not me, can you do that? Or tear the name off the paper?"

It was the most dangerous question she had asked him, and she had asked it softly, with her bare hands on her knee where he could see them. He did not take long to answer, because the answer was not in doubt. He took long because he did not want it to be true.

"No," said Rohan.

Her fingers did not whiten. She had known he would say it.

"A warden cannot pardon," he said. "Not a captain, not the judge at Taravan, not the Salt Guild. I can arrest you and hold you. I can delay, and I can argue. I cannot unwrite a name once the crown has it. Only the queen can, with her own hand."

"Then you could tell the judge I was never the Salt Fox."

"I could. It would be a lie, and I would have to say it to a man who has never taken my word on less." He spoke slowly. "And it would not hold. Your crew know you. The paper would only say that I was a liar."

"So it's the queen."

"It is the queen. I would carry you to her myself, if she were nearer." He heard what he had said, and did not take it back. "I cannot make the law bend for you, Meera. I can only walk you to the one person who can."

She was quiet. The wind moved a skin of snow across the rock.

"That's the worst true thing you've said," she said at last. "And you said it while I could see your hands."

"Yes."

"I think I liked it better when you were just the man with the noose." She huffed out a breath that was nearly a laugh. "At least then I knew where to stand."

"You still do." He held his hands out, palms up, and they were warm. "Here. Where I can see you."

She looked at the hands for a long moment. Then she turned her face toward the valley, and he knew the conversation was finished and that it had moved something.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It was the stone fold that undid him.

They had come down to where the Saddle's snow broke up into scree and stunted thorn. A herders' track crossed below them, and beside it a low wall of piled stone stood like an animal fold. Rohan knew it. He had walked this trail at twelve years old with his father's hand on his collar. Two nights before the crossing he had stood over the post table with the ruler held flat on a sheet of the post's good paper, and watched Varun's neat hand draw this very wall along its edge. *Captain-ji, you've forgotten the shrine,* Varun had said, and added it.

Rohan had not forgotten the shrine. Varun had only wanted it on the sheet.

He walked past the fold, and the thing came up through his chest without warning, so fast he could not shut it. Ten years. Varun had held his horse at the Taravan gate when Rohan was newly captain and sick with nerves. Varun had stood at Dilan's grave in the wardens' row with a skin of wine and said the wrong funny thing, and made the whole hall laugh when nobody could breathe. Varun had *sat with him*. He had sat in the dark of the post room on the night Rohan first read Dilan's report, saying nothing, and Rohan had thought: *this is what a friend is.*

And the pick had struck stone, and Jassi, forty winters old, was dead.

Rohan stopped. His fist went into the nearest slab of the fold before he knew he'd closed it. It split his knuckles. He hit it again, and the rope jerked at Meera's wrist.

"Rohan." She did not shout.

"He asked me about my sister." His voice was strange. "At the table, before we left. Whether Ila was safe in Neshar through the winter. He had a face like an honest man worried for her. He was already counting what I'd sell for."

"I know."

"You do not. You never trusted him." He pulled the hand back, bloody to the second joint, and found he did not care. "I stood at his *wedding*. I would have put my own neck in his hands. And I hope he is under that snow. I hope he is still under it, and that he woke, and knew."

It sat between them in the cold, ugly and entire. He waited for her to say something merciful.

She came back up the line and took his bleeding hand in both of hers, and turned it to the light. "That's a true thing," she said. "Don't take it back."

"I should not feel it."
