"Why not? It's free." She pressed snow into the split knuckles, hard, and he hissed. "Jassi named every goat he ever owned. Auntie was the one who kicked. I'd like Varun to know that, before he's done." Her eyes were dry and fierce. "Be angry, Captain. I'll hold the rope."

He breathed. His chest heaved, and his ribs ached like a stitched wound. Under the anger there was grief for the friend he had had, and he let that show too, since there was no one to see it but her.

When he was done, she let go of his hand and walked on. He fell in behind her, exactly in her steps.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The birches began at the third bend, thin white trunks standing in snow to their knees. Below them the trees changed. The bark grew rough and dark and the branches thickened into broad, heavy arms, bare now, and the slope turned to long terraces of walnut, stepping down toward a hollow in the hills.

He smelled it first. A green, bitter steam, thick as tea, hung in the cold air from a hundred chimneys at once.

"Walnut leaf," said Meera. Her voice had gone odd. "They boil it for the lungs. Every house, every day." Her stride lengthened, and the rope drew him after her. "That's Ranu."

Then he heard the coughing.

It came through timber walls. It was not one cough, but many, a low rasp behind every door and shutter, wet and dry, near and far, a child's high bark answered by an old man's deep one. The sound never stopped. It simply moved from house to house, like a bell passed hand to hand.

The track dropped out of the trees onto the open shoulder above the village. Timber roofs showed black with damp, and pale threads of smoke rose between them. And on the slope beside the track, in frozen ground, lay the mounds.

He stopped. He had not meant to.

They lay in rows along the contour of the hill, each one a hump of earth the colour of rust where the spades had cut through the crust. Stones were piled on some. A bit of rag was knotted to a stick on another. The newest had no marker at all, only a scatter of dark clods across the snow.

Rohan had buried seven wardens. He knew how to count a grave. He started at the top corner and walked the first row slowly, and Meera did not stop him. Eight. Nine. A long mound, and a long one, and then one that was no longer than his own forearm and a half. Her breath caught. The next was the same length, and the one after, and he understood what the little ones were, and kept counting, because stopping would have been a way of looking away.

She was counting too. He heard it under her breath, the way she counted goats. Her hand had gone to her coat, over the bell.

The second row. The third. The ground was so hard here that the diggers had left stones at the edge of every cut, heaped up in small cairns of their own. Some mounds had fresh pine boughs laid across. Some had a toy: a carved goat, a spindle, a crooked wooden horse.

"Twenty-six," he said, and his voice came out thin and strange. "Twenty-seven."

He finished the row. He turned and came back along the lowest terrace, and the last of them were barely a hand's width apart, as if the diggers had given up the idea of space.

Thirty-one.

There was a shovel in the ground at the end of the row. A woman stood behind it, thick in the shoulder, wrapped in a shapeless brown pattu with its hem stiff with frozen earth. She was fifty perhaps, with iron-grey hair scraped back under a cloth and a mouth set hard as a ledge. She had been digging. Her breath hung in front of her in short, hard clouds, and beside her feet the raw earth was opened to a depth of barely a forearm. It was the right length for a child.

She looked at Meera's rope, the wool still red under its crust of ice. She looked at the grey pattu on Rohan's shoulder and at the warden's knot at his collar.

"Nimmi," said Meera. Her hand had gone to the pack, flat over the oilcloth roll. "Kiran, is he—I have it, twelve packets, near enough, it's here—"

"I know who you are." Nimmi's voice was flat as a plank. She ignored the pack, and Meera. Her eyes stayed on Rohan. "Breathing. That keeps." She drove the shovel in again and left it standing. "Blockade dead. Thirty-one this winter, and it's not yet midwinter. I've buried most of them myself. You're the captain who signs it?"

He could have said that the post signed it. He could have said the warden-judge, or the queen, or the Closing Law itself, written sixty winters ago, long before he was born. He could have said that he had only held the line. Every one of those was true. The rope on his wrist lay quiet. Nothing in his hands would have moved.

The smell of boiled leaf sat in his throat. The coughing went on behind him, and thirty-one counted graves lay at his back, belonging to people whose names he did not know. He said the one thing that was entirely his.

"Yes."

His hands stayed bare at his sides. He did not hide them.

# Chapter 18: Twelve Packets

"Didi," said Kiran. "You brought a warden?"

He lay on a pallet by Nimmi's hearth with a quilt up to his chin, and he was the colour of old ash. His lips were split and dry. A wool cap was pulled down over both ears, hiding the frostbitten one, and under it his face had lost its flesh. One fist was closed under the quilt, gripping something, and it did not let go.

His eyes had gone to Rohan's face first. Meera saw it. Something in Kiran flinched, a small hard tightening, as if he had been struck. Then his gaze dropped to the grey pattu on Rohan's shoulder, and the joke came up over the top of it.

"Don't talk," said Meera.

"Ten days gone, and you bring me a warden. I'd have settled for apricots." The laugh turned into a cough that bent him double. She was on her knees with her arm under his shoulders before it finished. His ribs moved under her hand like a bird's.

Any smuggler flinched at that coat. She put it down to that.

"Don't," she said again, and heard that she was begging.

"He's tied to you." Kiran's eyes had found the red rope. His voice broke, then steadied. "Of course he is. Of course you did it the hard way." He turned his face into her sleeve. "Didi. I kept them going. Nimmi will tell you."

"I know. Hush."

Behind her, Rohan stood in the doorway, close enough that the rope hung slack. He said nothing. It was the one silence she had ever been glad of.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Nimmi did the counting at her own table, with a kettle going and her sleeves pushed back.

"Forty-one sick. Nine under ten. When your brother wrote his note it was forty-one, and twenty-five have been buried since, and twenty-five more took to their beds. The number doesn't move. The people do." She ticked each case off on a thumb, like a woman reading out damages. "Fever, all of them. Cough, wet in most, dry in the old. Hira the weaver has the blue lip. Bholu's mother lost her voice on the third day, which is bad, and her daughter has the rash, which is worse. Kamla's two have been sleeping since morning, and I don't like sleep. The Dev boy won't eat. The Dev boy's father won't stop eating. Your brother is the only one who jokes, and it's wearing him thin."

"Nimmi."

"Open the pack."

Meera unrolled the oilcloth on the table. Three rows of four, palm-sized bundles of waxed paper tied with thread. She had counted them at every halt since Kothi Shilak. She had opened only one, for the captain's lungs. Now she saw the wax on the end bundles, and grey paste in the oilcloth beneath.

"The Teeth," she said. "I had the pack against the rock all that day."

Nimmi turned the ruined bundles over with a fingertip. Two had split along the fold. The third had taken the damp and set like clay. She scraped what she could into a saucer, weighed it in her palm, and shook her head.

"Not enough to dose a cat." She pushed them aside. "Nine sound. A course is a pinch in hot walnut tea, morning and night, three days. A packet is two courses. Eight whole ones, sixteen. The one you opened is short two half-pinches, so call it a course." She eyed the twist Rohan had set on the table, thumb-long, the silk-sieve ring pressed in its wax. He had taken it from a hunter's purse. "And this. Crown-grade, from the men who wanted you dead. Another course. Eighteen."

"Could you thin it? Two pinches to a cup, a smaller—"

"Thin it and it turns nobody. I tried it three winters ago. I buried the proof." Nimmi folded her arms. "Eighteen courses, forty-one sick. You can do the sum. I did it on the way here, every night, and it came out the same."

Eighteen. The number lodged in Meera's chest like a stone she had swallowed whole. She had carried twelve packets over the Long Saddle with the regent's hunters behind her and Jassi dead in the gully. She had counted them until the count was a prayer. She had never once thought of the other number.

"Then we choose," said Nimmi, "and you write it down. You're the one with the book."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They did it in the back room, where the loom stood under a sheet. Nimmi sat on the weaver's bench. Meera took an upturned crate, with the count open on her knee and the pen she kept for names.

At the door she had told Rohan to wait in the front room.

"Eight arm-spans," said Rohan. "I am aware of the length. I will be at the edge of it."

"At the edge of it with your back turned."

He gave her the long, level look he gave a map he did not like, and she held it. "Meera, the choosing—"

"Is mine." It came out harder than she meant. "Nimmi's and mine. It isn't a warden's business."

"I have no wish to make it my business." His fingertips stayed brown on the doorframe. "I will turn my back."

He turned. But the back room was windowless and the lamp had burned to a stub, and when Nimmi cursed and fumbled with the flint, an arm came through the doorway holding out a fresh wick, already lit. Rohan had not turned around. His hand was simply there, steady, holding the light at her shoulder.

"I said—"

"I heard you. I am not looking." He faced the front room, and the lamp stayed where it was. "You cannot write by a stub. If I put it down, you must get up and light another, and then you will be away from the rope."

She did not answer. She dipped the pen.

"Hira," said Nimmi. "Blue lip. Sixty if he's a day. A course won't turn him."

"Then he doesn't get one."

It was the first name, and she wrote it without a pause, in the left column. She made her hand form every letter. *Hira, weaver, no course.* The lamp did not shake.

"Kamla's two. Both sleeping. Girl of five, boy of three."

"Since dawn?"

"Since dawn."

"Gone too deep," said Meera. "Both. No."

"Meera—"

"I know. I'll write it. It's the same rule." She wrote *Kamla's boy, Kamla's girl*, and the pen scratched. The rule was plain: the course had to catch someone still on the near side of the worst, a fever that could be turned, a mouth that could still swallow the tea.

They went down the list. Bholu's mother, yes. Her daughter with the rash, yes, because Nimmi said the rash meant days left and not hours. Old Lalli, who'd had the cough three winters running and would likely outlast them all, no. The Dev boy, yes. His father, no, and Nimmi laughed once, short, and didn't look up from the paper.

Sixteen. Seventeen. When the pen reached Sunni, Meera's hand stopped.

She had seen Sunni already. The girl was seven, with plaits as thin as pencils and a fever that painted her cheeks. She had been watching Meera's coat. When Meera crouched to put a cup in her hands, she had asked, in a voice like dry reeds, whether the bell in her pocket was for a goat.

"For an old one," Meera had said. "She's called Badami. She's retired."

"Can I ring it?"

Meera had taken out the small, dented bell and put it in the girl's hand. Sunni had shaken it once and laughed, and the laugh had turned into the cough. She'd laid the bell on her chest and kept her fist over it.

"Sunni," said Nimmi now. "Still talking. Still eating, a little. Her breath's wet but clear at the bottom."

"So she's stronger than the others."

"Stronger than Bholu's mother. Which means she can wait for the third water." Nimmi rubbed her eyes. "I can brew six cups at a time on this hearth. That's three waves. Sunni could take till dusk."

"You're sure."

"No." Nimmi dropped her hands. "I'm sure of nothing. That's what this is."

Meera read the page. Eighteen with a course, Sunni the last of them. Twenty-three without. She wrote it. *Sunni, seven. Third water, dusk.* The ink sat there, black and wet and exact.

Behind her the lamp had not moved. At the corner of her eye she saw the hem of his coat, and the hand at the end of the light, bare and still, split across the knuckles from the stone fold. He did not touch her shoulder. He did not tell her she had chosen well.

She set the pen down and found she was glad he was there.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The water began as Rohan's idea, and ended as a thing he did with his whole body, as if there was nothing in him but arms.

There were four wells in Ranu. The nearest to Nimmi's house was a hundred paces down a frozen path. At noon he took two buckets from the porch before anyone had asked, got to the end of the porch, and felt the rope go tight.

"Eight arm-spans," said Meera. "I'm coming."

"The tea—"

"Will wait a quarter hour." She pulled on her gloves. "It won't like it."

They went down together, a warden of the queen in a pattu with a goat on the collar and a smuggler with a kettle-burned sleeve, and the whole village watched from doorways and shutters. They saw the red rope between the wrists. They saw him slow at every step to keep inside its length. He filled the buckets. She took one so he need not carry both on his ribs, and they climbed back.

A hundred paces down, a hundred up. She counted it, because she counted everything. Every trip was a quarter hour, and every quarter hour came off the time between houses. The tea went late to the Dev house. It went late to Bholu's mother. Nimmi said nothing, but her eyes went to the sun.

Rohan followed her to Hira's and stood at the edge of the room with the bucket while she crouched by the weaver's pallet. He followed her to the Dev house and filled the kettle, and when the Dev boy's father, still eating and weeping into his bread, took hold of his sleeve and said *please, please*, he stood and let the man hold it. He did not tell him the law. He did not tell him he was sorry.

"You don't have to haul every bucket," she said at the fifth door. "Get a boy to do it. You'd be a quarter hour quicker, and I'd stop walking to the well."

"Which boy?"

She opened her mouth, and turned, and looked down the lane. In every doorway stood a woman with a cloth over her arm, or a grandfather holding a basin. Every pair of hands in Ranu was already full. The boy who had hauled for Nimmi till yesterday lay on a pallet in the third house, burning.

"Everyone who can walk is nursing," said Rohan, quietly. "I asked at the second door. Nobody has hands to spare." He lifted the bucket. "Without the rope you would be quicker. With it, you are not. I can do nothing about the rope. I can carry what it allows."

She had counted the trip. She had not counted why it had to be them.

At the sixth house a woman stood in the doorway with her arms crossed and her face shut. Her gaze went to the goat on Rohan's collar, and she spat on the boards at his feet. It was a careful spit, not at him but beside him. Meera opened her mouth.

"It is a fair price," said Rohan. He picked up the bucket again. "Please tell her I am carrying water, and that I would carry it if she spat on me."

The woman's mouth twisted. She stood aside.

By late afternoon the second water had gone out, and Meera's boots were soaked to the ankle. She stood in the lane between the Dev house and the next, with her cold hands pressed to her mouth.

"One hundred and forty steps from the well to this door," said Rohan, behind her. "I counted. You always count."

"Don't make me laugh."

"I was not trying to." He set down the buckets. "I was giving you something to count that was not the other thing."

She laughed anyway, or something near it, a hard cracked sound. When she turned, his face was grey with the ache of his ribs and his hands were raw, and he was looking at her as if she were an ordinary person to whom ordinary things might be said.

"Third water," she said. "It's nearly dusk."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Sunni took the tea in both hands, and Meera held the cup beneath her chin so the steam rose into her face.

The girl was hotter than at noon. Her breath came short, and her plaits had come loose. But she smiled, thin and wobbling, when she saw the bell was still in her fist.

"Is Badami in the barn?"

"In the barn. Eating the roof, probably."

"Mama says goats can't eat roofs."

"That's because she hasn't met Badami." Meera tipped the cup. The girl swallowed, and coughed, and swallowed. The pinch of redbark had turned the tea bitter and brown, and she wrinkled her nose. "It's horrible, isn't it?"

"It's *so* horrible," said Sunni.

"Then it's working. Drink the rest."

She drank the rest. She slept, with the bell under her chin and her mother's hand in her hair, and Meera stayed for the length of a hundred slow breaths. She counted them. Then Rohan's shadow shifted at the door, and she got up and went out into the dark.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kiran's cough was lower now, the tea in him. He was awake, on his side, watching the fire with half-lidded eyes.

"You're writing it down," he said.

"What?"

"The count. Your book's out." He tried to grin. "Who did you pick?"

"Eighteen."

"And you hate it."

"I hate it." She put the notebook away. It was an effort, and her hand shook, and she saw him see it. "Turn over. You're lying on your lung."

She went to ease the pillow from under his head. Something was beneath it, a small hard weight wrapped in wool. When she lifted the corner it slid out onto the quilt.

A badge. Brass gone brown, a stamped disc the width of her palm, with a green bloom in the lettering. She knew it at once, having seen a hundred like it on a hundred coats: a goat's head, the queen's mark. A warden's badge, with a tiny dent in its rim.

She sat very still.

"Kiran."

His hand came out of the quilt and closed over it, faster than a sick man's should. He held it against his chest. The same fist, she saw. It had been gripping this all day.

"It's nothing."

"It's a warden's badge. Under your pillow."

"It's mine."

"It isn't yours." She kept her voice low. In the doorway the edge of Rohan's shoulder showed at the margin of the light, turned away, watching the lane. Eight arm-spans. He could see the room if he turned his head. "Whose is it?"

"Didi."

"Whose?"

"I'll tell you." His eyes were very bright, and the fever was in them, but under it was something that was not fever. It was fear, plain and old, and she remembered how it had flickered across his face when he first saw the coat in the doorway. "I swear. Not now. Ask me when I can breathe."

"Kiran, if a warden—"

"Not *now*." His voice broke, and the cough took him. When it was done he was crying a little, the way the sick do, without sound. "Please. I'm so tired."

Her eyes stayed on the badge in his white fist. Then they went to the back of the man she was tied to, who had hunted a smuggler for a dead boy's sake for two winters.

Silence was free. It was the one gift the mountain gave you, and she had never wanted it less.

She tucked the quilt over Kiran's hand and the brass in it, and moved so that her body stood between the bed and the door.

"Sleep," she said.

"You're not going to tell him."

"I'm going to sit here." She sat. "That's all I'm doing."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The fourth water went round at full dark, and then there was nothing left to do but wait.

Rohan slept sitting against the wall, the lamp turned low and his hands open on his knees. Meera watched them for a time out of habit. They were warm. They were only tired.

At the grey edge of dawn, Sunni's mother began to make a noise.

Meera was on her feet before she knew it. The girl lay as she had been, one hand curled over the bell, her plaits damp and her face turned to the wall. There was no cough. The room had become much quieter.

"She was asleep," said her mother. "I put my hand on her chest and it was going, and I closed my eyes. I only closed my eyes."

Nimmi put two fingers under the little jaw and held them, and held them, and took them away. She said nothing.

Meera knelt where she had knelt the evening before. The cup was still on the floor, the dregs gone cold and brown. She had given the redbark at dusk. Too late by a day, or six hours, or the quarter hours spent walking to the well. It did not matter which. Sunni was seven.

The pen was in her hand. She did not remember taking it out. She opened the count to the last name on the right and drew a line through it, plain and horizontal, straight as a rule. Then she wrote the date beside it and the hour, in the same hand that had written every other name.

Grey light came through the shutter. A palm came down, not onto her shoulder but onto the floor beside her, flat on the boards, an arm's length off and no nearer. He had only put it where she could see it.

She did not look up.

"I wrote her down eighteenth," she said. "It was my sum."

"Yes," said Rohan. "And it was my water. Eleven trips to the well. I counted them, as you would." He did not move his hand. "I am not saying it to lessen yours. I am saying it is not yours alone."

She shut her eyes. Nothing in his voice asked her to feel better.

He stayed where he was, on one knee in the cold, a hand's breadth from the small curled shape and the bell, and let her do the work.

# Chapter 19: Nimmi's Count

They buried Sunni at first light, and Meera dug the last foot herself.

The ground fought her. Nimmi kept one cut open ahead of the dying, a forearm deep, because she held that hope wasted medicine and a grave dug late wasted worse. It was not deep enough for this. So Meera knelt in the raw trench with the adze and chipped the frozen clay out in pieces the size of her fist. Rohan stood on the uphill edge, holding the rope slack so it would not drag at her wrist. He did not offer to take the tool. He had learned.

Sunni's mother brought her wrapped in a blue shawl, walking slowly, as if the weight were more than it was. At the edge of the cut she held out her other hand. In it lay the small dented bell.

"She'd have wanted you to have it back. It's not mine."

"It's hers." Meera climbed out and closed the woman's fingers over the brass. "It was Jassi's. Put it in with her."

The mother laughed, one broken sound, and bent to lay the bell on the shawl over the small chest. Then the earth went in. Nobody sang. In Ranu there was no breath to spare for it. When the last clod fell, the women at the row's edge went back to their sick without a word.

Meera stayed, the adze hanging from her fist.

"Thirty-two," said Rohan, quietly.

"Don't count them for me."

"I was only saying it so that it is said."

She turned on him then, because there was no one else, and because she had carried the thing since the stone fold.

"Say it to me. The law. All of it, the way you'd say it to the judge at Taravan." Her voice came out low and level. "Say why a girl of seven has to be in that hole."

He did not step back. She gave him credit for that. "You know the answer I will give."

"I want to hear it with her there."

He looked at the mound. He used the formal voice, every sentence whole, and she understood it was respect and not hiding.

"Without the Closing Law, the Saddle would run all season with the regent's grain wagons and the Salt Guild's long trains. Neshar would buy its bread at whatever price they named." His hands hung open. "The law is cruel. It is also the one wall Pritam cannot buy."

"And the law kills children." She said it flat. The heat had gone out of her somewhere around the hundredth breath beside Sunni's pallet. "Your wall has a shape, Rohan. It's a row on a hillside. Nimmi has buried thirty-two this winter, and every one of them is on the near side of the wall you're keeping for their sake."

"I know."

"Then keep it, but don't tell me it's *for* them."

"It is for the ones who would be buried by the wagons." Each word cost him. "I cannot show you those graves. They have not been dug. I am asked to weigh dead I can see against dead I cannot."

"That's convenient."

"It is the most inconvenient thing I know." His voice cracked, only slightly. "I would give a great deal for it to be convenient."

She looked at the row. "I don't weigh the ones I can't see. I count the ones in front of me, every name, because nobody else will. Your unseen dead are a guess, Rohan, a good one, and you've hanged boys on it." Her voice shook and she let it. "If the wagons come, you'll have graves to show for it, and I would stand at them with you. I would hold the adze. But I won't dig these on the strength of a guess."

"No," he said. "I do not think you should."

That was all. He did not argue. Her hands were bare on the adze, and so were his, and neither pair had whitened. He had told the truth, and so had she, and the mountain was content with both. That was the part she could not forgive. It would have been easier to hate a liar.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The patrol came up from the lower road at mid-morning, and Meera's first warning was a boy running.

He skidded to a stop in the lane, cap in his hand. "Riders. Twelve. Grey coats, with a bell on the shoulder. They're asking for the guide."

"Which guide?"

"Any guide. They said *anyone off the Saddle*."

Rohan looked at the sky as if it might give him the hour. "They have found something at the Teeth. Two men in the lee of a rock, with a splinted wrist."

"Nimmi's goat shed. Now." Meera took a grip on the rope.

They ran, as far as his ribs let him, the grey pattu still on his shoulders because there had been no time to take it anywhere. The shed was a lean-to of timber and plaster behind Nimmi's house, with goats below and a hay loft above, under the roof.

She worked it out on the way, and it was short. The house would be the patrol's first door. A stranger in the yard would be asked a name, and nobody in Ranu could vouch for her and live. With a man who could not run, the loft was the only cover there was.

Rohan went up the ladder one-handed, breathing through his teeth. Halfway up, Meera saw the problem. The grey wool showed bright against the dark boards, plain as a flag from the lane. She came up two rungs behind, caught the pattu at the collar and dragged it off his back.

"Meera—"

"Quiet." She shoved it deep into the hay and pulled straw over it. "That's what they're looking for. Not you. That."

The loft was low enough that she hit her head on the first purlin, and hay-dust rose in a cloud that made them both cough into their sleeves. Under the eaves, where the roof came down to the floor, there was room beside him for exactly one person, and only if she lay on her side.

"Move your arm."

"I am moving it."

"Your *good* arm."

He lifted the right, and she went in under it, spine to his chest, which kept her weight off his ribs. The hay crackled. His coat smelled of camphor and woodsmoke, and below them a goat shifted and sighed. The rope lay between them, red against the straw.

Boots on frozen ground. A bit-chain. A man's voice, bored, carrying across the yard.

Meera put her eye to a knot-hole in the gable plaster.

Twelve riders had pulled up before Nimmi's house. They wore grey wool with a small brass bell stitched on each shoulder, and they were clean in the way of men sent to look at something they will not touch. The captain was heavy, with a red moustache. One soldier had a sack on his saddle bow, another a bucket.

Nimmi stood on her step with her sleeves rolled and a ladle in her hand.

"Healer. You keep the sick book here?"

"I keep a count."

"Good. We want the houses." He swung down. "Every house with a sick person in it. The regent has a care for Ranu, mistress."

"Has he."

"By his order every sick house is to be marked, for the cleansing after the Fair."

The bucket went down on the frozen mud. It held ash, grey and fine, mixed to a paste. The soldier dipped a glove in it, walked to the nearest door and pressed his palm to the lintel, hard. He left a handprint the width of a plate.

Rohan went still behind her.

"They're going house to house," she whispered. "What's cleansing?"

"I do not know." His breath moved the hair at her nape. "I have never heard the word used so."

Below, Nimmi came down off her step. "Mark my door and every house in Ranu knows who to blame. What does it mean? Cleansing."

"It means what it says."

"Say it again with more words."

"The sick houses are marked, and no one goes in or out after the sixth day without a pass." The captain wiped his hands. "You'll be told more when the general rides up."

"The general."

"After the Snow Fair." He said it easily, like the name of a season. "He's coming up himself, once the regent's invested. He'll want it done properly. So the marks are for him, healer, not for you. Where's your guide?"

"What guide?"

"The pair that came down off the Saddle. One has the wardens' grey on his back. We hear a captain of the Shilak post is lost."

"Lost," said Nimmi, "is a word for it. We've had no one. We've had the grey cough. If your general wants to see it done properly, he's welcome to carry a bucket."

She lied flat to the regent's man, and it cost her nothing. She was bound to no one. Meera pressed her forehead to the cool plaster and thought of the grey wool under the hay, an arm's length from her knee.

The soldier with the bucket moved along the lane. Hand, lintel, grey print. At the third house a woman stood in her doorway with a child on her hip. The soldier looked at her and at the child, made the mark above her head, and she did not move.

"Meera." It was hardly a breath.

She turned her head against his arm. His face was very near, grey in the light through the slats, his eyes on her and not on the lane.

"I am going to say something I should not," he whispered. "It is true. I need you to hear it before I decide to take it back."

"Say it."

"I do not know if I can keep the law after this." The words came one at a time, as if he laid them on a table. "I said I would, in the Rope-Hut. I said it at the grave. I have believed it for twelve years, and I stood at that hole this morning and believed it still, and I cannot *find* it. The reasons are where I left them. I can see them. I cannot feel that they are enough." He swallowed. "If I had to sign the blockade a fourth winter, I think I could not hold the pen."

Her fingers lay on the rope. His hand was by hers on the straw, bare, the split knuckles dark. Warm. She did not need to touch it.

"You're telling me this with a dozen of the regent's men in the lane."

"You are the only person I can tell."

"And if I told the judge?"

"Then you would be telling him the truth." The corner of his mouth moved, though it was not a smile. "You have been careful of mine. I am going to be careful of yours. It is a bad habit, I find. It grows."

Below, the captain's voice rose. "We'll want the loft searched. They hide in hay."

Her whole body went to ice, and not the lie kind. Rohan's good arm closed round her without a word. She put her face in the straw and listened to a boot on the first rung of the ladder.

A ladle rang against the shed post, loud as a bell.

"Go up, then," said Nimmi. "My goats have had the grey cough since first snow, and I've been sluicing them with the water I wash the dead in. That hay is black with it. You'll carry it to the general on your sleeves."

The boot stopped.

"Leave it, Pasang," said the captain. "Half-dead goats and a loft full of mould. I'm not climbing up for a warden who got himself lost." A pause. "Mark the shed, though."

The boot went away. A palm hit the shed door, flat, a sound she felt through the boards.

They lay there until the bit-chains faded down the lane. The breath she finally took tasted of dust and camphor.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Nimmi let them down an hour later.

Meera dug the pattu out of the hay and shook it. Her knees shook from the cramped quarter hour and nothing else, and she held the ladder rail until they stopped. Rohan came down one rung at a time, lips pressed white, coat full of straw. In the nearest stall a goat regarded them with a yellow eye and went on chewing.

She had walked down the mountain with one thing clear. He held the noose. He had held it three winters.

She took out the count and thumbed back to the page where she had written him in the Rope-Hut, in her small hard hand. *Warden. The noose.* She uncapped the pen. She meant to add a line.

Nothing came that was true. *Liar* was not true. *Hangs boys* was, and it was not all of it. The nib hung over the page until the ink dried on it. Twelve years of an honest man's certainty had cracked in front of her, and he had not hidden the crack. He had pointed to it.

She shut the book. She did not want him to be the villain. She wanted to want it again.

"Your hair," said Rohan, with great gravity, "has become a haystack."

"So has your coat."

"We are well matched."

It was a bone-dry joke, the first in days, and it took her by surprise. She laughed. It came out as a cough, then something wetter, and she turned her face to the shed wall until it passed. He waited a hand's breadth off, a wall to lean on if she chose. She did not choose, and he let that be.

Outside, the lane was bright with winter sun. Nimmi stood on her step looking at her own front door.

The print was on the lintel, grey on dark wood, four fingers spread, above the room where Kiran lay coughing. Down the lane, house after house wore the same pale hand held up against the timber as if in surrender.

"Cleansing," said Nimmi. She did not turn. "I have been a healer thirty years. I know a word for a cleansed wound and a word for a cleansed well. I don't know one for a village."

Meera went and stood by her and set her own hand beside the print, not touching. The ash was still damp. Hers was smaller by half.

Beyond the last house, a drover in a felt cap was unloading a packhorse at the lane's foot. He stacked split pitch-pine against the last wall, log on log, neat as a man paid by the cord. When he was done he mounted and rode down the road without looking up. The wood was pale and wet with resin.

"Nobody here burns pitch-pine," said Nimmi, as if to the door. "It blacks the pots."

Meera looked from the stack to the row of grey hands, and back.

"How long?" she said. "Until the Fair?"

"Eight days, if the road holds." Nimmi counted it off on her thumb, as she did everything. "And the general after."

Meera counted what she could not take back. Garh Neshar was a day on the road for a sound man and a day and a half for Rohan. The Fair would not wait for a village to be ready. The doors shut on the sixth day. The courses begun yesterday would run out in three days, long before. After that came the twenty-three who had never had a course at all, nursed by one woman behind a boarded door, with no boy to haul water and no one to spell her.

She wrote it standing, on her knee, in the sun. *Sixth day. Twenty-three, no course. Nimmi alone. I go.* Then she read it aloud, so that it was said.

"I'm going," she said. "When the doors shut I walk away from that house and leave her with them. The rope won't let me stay, and it won't let you go without me." She did not look at him. She looked at the print. "So I go."

Rohan drew a slow breath at her shoulder.

"Then we go," he said. "Until then I carry every bucket."

The rope hummed against her wrist, a low note and level, and it did not climb. She felt it through the bone. He had not said *I would*, or *I might*. He had said *I carry*, and the mountain had taken it as a word given. Every bucket until the doors shut was a debt now, written in the cold.

"You shouldn't have said that on the rope," she said.

"I know." He was already lifting the two pails from Nimmi's porch. "It is true, though."

The line drew tight, and she went after him down the path, past the doors. At the lane's foot the pine stood stacked against the last wall, and the grey hands ran toward it, house by house. She began to count the doors between.

# Chapter 20: Across the Rope

Nimmi gave them the storehouse because it was the only room in Ranu without a sick person in it.

It stood against the back wall of her house, a plank room full of sacks of dried walnut leaf, so that the whole dark smelled of bitter tea. Someone had swept a space clear. Someone had left a clay brazier, a heap of blankets and a lamp.

Meera sat on a sack with the count open on her knee and wrote Sunni's name.

She had struck it that morning in the choosing list, straight as a rule. That was the dead column of a different sheet. This was the count itself, the waxed book, where she wrote the ones she carried and the ones she lost on the way. *Sunni, seven, Ranu. Third water, dusk. Died before light.* Under it, in the same hand: *Eighteen courses. Forty-one sick.*

She did not write what she thought about the quarter hours. The page would not hold it.

The rope lay slack between her wrist and Rohan's. He sat on the floor with his back to the wall, a careful distance off, his left arm held against his ribs. He had not asked what she was writing. He had studied her pen for a long time, the way he studied weather.

"May I see it?"

She almost said no. The count was hers, and nobody had read it through. Bindu had never asked. Kiran had joked about it for ten years and never once looked. Rohan had seen one page, in the hut, and she had chosen that page.

She held out the book.

He took it in his right hand and read as he read everything, to the end of the line. His eyes stopped on the last entry. He turned back a page, and another, and found the long run of names: two hundred and six people, each with a date and a price and a village. Some of the prices were crossed out. Beside those, in smaller writing, she had put *free*.

"You charge the rich double," he said.

"The file at your post never says that."

"No. I have read it eleven times." He turned another page. "I was wrong about that. I want it said, and I want it said now, while we are not in a hurry."

"You were wrong about a lot of things."

"I am working through them." He closed the book and gave it back, gently, as you hand over a thing that might wake. "Thank you. That was not a small thing to let me do."

She put the book on her knee and her hand on the book. Through the wall, Kiran coughed, low and wet, and fell quiet.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"A question," said Rohan. "Yours, if you want it. Mine if you do not."

It was the old trade, the one they had made in the hut with sticks of juniper for every refusal. There was no juniper here and no one counting. It had never needed to be counted.

"Yours," she said. "Go on."

"Then I will answer one before you ask." He set his right hand on his knee, palm up, in the lamplight. "I signed the Shilak blockade order. Three winters running, in the autumn, before first snow. The post commander writes it and the captain signs it, and I signed it with a clean pen and slept that night." He did not look away. "Sunni is in my hand too."

The rope stayed quiet. Outside, the wind among the stacked leaf-sacks made a small dry noise.

His palm was warm. The fingers were relaxed, the split knuckles pink and ordinary. She had been waiting, without knowing it, for the whitening that would tell her it was only words.

She said, "Say how many."

"I do not know how many. I only know the winters." He drew breath carefully, around the rib. "Nimmi says thirty-two now, with Sunni, dead this winter before midwinter. I could say that is not mine alone, and it is true. The regent's grain, the Guild, a queen who has signed pardons for thirty winters and never repealed one line of the law. I could say it, and each word would be true, and I would be using them to stand behind. So I am not going to say it."

She had wanted this. For three winters, and especially since the gully, she had wanted to hear him say it. She had imagined a voice full of shame. This was not shame. It was an account, set down plainly by a man who knew the sum.

It did not feel like winning. It did not make Sunni lighter.

"That's not absolution," she said. "I don't have any. I'd like you to know that before you think you've bought some."

"I did not come to buy."

"I know. That's what's so—" She stopped. Her own hands, in her lap, were warm too. She had said nothing but true things all evening. "I know you didn't."

"Then what is this?" he said quietly. "If it is not that."

"It's the count." She opened the book.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


There was a line she had never written a name beside. Near the back, ruled off by itself, it read: *Shilak closed. Three winters. By order.* Under it she had set down, winter by winter, the numbers Nimmi had told her over the years, because someone should. The right-hand margin had stayed empty.

She dipped the pen.

*R., captain, Shilak post. Signed.* She wrote the name in the same letters as everyone else. It looked small on the page.

Then she turned back through the book to the page from the Rope-Hut. Her own small hard hand looked up at her: *Warden. The noose.* Under it, in Nimmi's goat shed, the nib had hung and dried and left nothing. She had not been able to add a line.

She could now. Beneath the old words she wrote, *Said so himself. Carried water, eleven trips.*

She turned the book so he could see.

He read the page twice. His eyes rested on the first line a long moment, and then, to her surprise, the corner of his mouth moved.

"That is also correct," he said.

"I don't strike true things."

"No." When he looked up, his face had the stunned openness of a man who has braced for a blow and been handed a cup. "It is the first time anyone has written me down as a thing that happened, and not a thing that was done to them. And you left the first line standing."

"Don't get fond. It's a ledger." Her voice was not steady. "Everyone in it is someone I couldn't leave off."

He didn't answer. He got up slowly, a hand against the wall, crossed the length of the rope and sat on the sack's edge in front of her. He took her bare right hand in his and turned it over. The knuckles were swollen and split from the Teeth.

"May I?"

"What for?"

"There is goat fat on Nimmi's shelf. I saw it. Your hands are not mending and you have not said so." He waited. "You do not have to say it now. I would like to do it."

She had said once, in the hut, in passing, that the cold got into her knuckles and she never could be bothered. She had forgotten saying it. He had not. She nodded.

He fetched the pot. He warmed the fat between his palms first, so it would not shock the cracks, and worked it into her knuckles one at a time with his thumb. It took a long while. Neither of them spoke. At the end her hand lay in his as if it belonged there. When his thumb stopped, her fingers curled around it before she had decided anything, and held on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"I'm twenty-nine," she said.

His thumb stayed where it was, inside her grip. "Yes. I have read your file." A pause. "Thirty-five. You have it, I think."

"I do." She looked at the hand around hers. "I'm saying it because I want us both to have said it. I'm not cold, and I'm not afraid. I'm not looking for something to hold on to because a girl died this morning—"

She stopped. The tips of her first two fingers had gone white, a faint frost-bloom, there and then deeper. Rohan's eyes went to them and came back to her face.

"That's partly a lie," she said. "I'll start again."

"There is no need—"

She held both hands up between them. The white did not fade. It would sit in the tips for an hour, as it always did, and nothing she said now could hurry it.

"Don't look at those," she said. "They're no use to you for an hour. They'll vouch for nothing I say, and they'll look like a liar's whatever I say." She made herself steady. "So you'll have to take this without them. A girl died this morning and I want to be held, and that's not the whole of it, and it's not the reason. I want this. I want you, tonight, in this room. I've wanted it since the hut and I've been angry about it for days. Is it yours too, or am I the only one who's been counting?"

He looked at her white fingertips, and then he did not look at them again.

"Then I will take it unverified," he said, "on your word, as I would from anyone I trusted." His voice was low and not entirely under his control. "It is mine. It has been mine long enough that I stopped trusting myself in rooms with you." The corner of his mouth moved. "But you have asked me one thing. May I ask you one back?"

"Ask."

"What do you want?" He said it carefully, as you ask the way across a snowfield. "Not whether. I have the whether. I would like the what. I have a rib that will not allow me to guess."

She laughed, startled, and it broke something loose. "You're going to make me say it."

"I would like to do it right."

"Then slowly," she said. "And not on your left side. And I want your coat off, because I've watched that coat steam over the hut fire every night since the Saddle." She put her white-tipped hand flat on his chest, over the good side. "And you tell me if it hurts, and you don't do the thing where you pretend it doesn't."

"Those are rules I can keep." His own hand came up over hers, over the cold tips, and did not flinch. "I will tell you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They untied the rope, because it tangled. The mountain's distance still held them, and neither forgot it, but there was freedom in the length of it, eight spans of slack to be as near as two people can be.

The coat came off in stages. The brown pattu. The shirt, which she eased over his left arm herself, because he could not lift it, and then stopped. The bruise ran from his armpit to his hip, yellow-green at the edges, black at the centre where Jassi's cord had bound him. She laid her fingers beside it, not on it.

"Does this—"

"No. Not there." He caught his breath. "That. Yes. That is fine."

She learned his ribs as she had learned the Teeth, by feel and by patience. Where she could press, where she must not. His good hand moved over her carefully, as if she might be taken from him at any moment, and when it found the burn-scar on her forearm he stopped and kissed it, and she made a sound she had not planned.

"Tell me," she said. "If it's wrong."

"I will tell you." His mouth was against her wrist. "Meera. It is not wrong."

They were clumsy. It was cold, and then it wasn't. At one point she had to stop because he had gone grey and was breathing through his teeth, and she lay with her hand on his chest and her forehead on his shoulder until it eased. She did not feel cheated. He said, without being asked, in a voice that shook, that nobody had ever looked after him like this, and that it frightened him far more than the Teeth had.

She moved over him then, on his right side, where she could take her own weight, and studied his face. She had seen it in the dark of the hut, in fever, in fear. She had not seen it open like this. When he said her name, it was not the formal voice or any voice she had heard from him. It was unguarded and almost surprised, and she answered it, and for a long while there was nothing in the storehouse but the brazier ticking and the two of them.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Afterwards she lay with her back against his good side and the blankets pulled to her chin. The rope was tied again, because it was easier. A single red line lay across the straw between their wrists. Her fingertips had come back to their own colour while she wasn't looking.

"I should say it plainly," said Rohan, into her hair. "I have shared your fire since the waystone. That was harbouring, and I told myself the mountain gave me no choice. Tonight I did it with my eyes open. I know what the law calls it and I know what the rope is for." He was quiet a moment. "I wanted you to hear that from me."

"I heard."

"My writ still orders me to deliver the Salt Fox at the thaw."

"I know."

"I do not know what I will do." His arm tightened a little. "That is the truth. I will not say anything tonight that I cannot hold."

She waited for him to go on. He did not. His hand had found hers under the blanket and his thumb had been moving along her knuckle, slowly, over the goat fat. It stopped.

"Is this only the cold?" she said.

It came out lighter than she meant, a joke with a hook in it, and the rope was quiet. He had gone still behind her.

"No," said Rohan.

She turned her head. His hand lay on the blanket beside her face, open. The fingers were warm, rosy at the tips, as they had been all evening, and they did not move.

"Look at them," he said. "Whatever you want to ask, look first."

She looked. Then she took the hand and held it against her mouth, and said nothing else.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Through the wall, Kiran's cough stopped.

She was awake before she knew why. The lamp had burned low and the brazier was ash. She lay listening to the particular quiet where a sound had been, like a goat-bell stopping on a ridge.

It had gone on all night, the wet low bark she had learned like a song she hated. It was not going now.

She was up, with her shirt half on and her boots in her hand and the rope tight at her wrist. Rohan, already awake, had a hand on the wall and his coat across his shoulders. Neither spoke. She went out through the door in her stockings, across the frozen yard and in by the back, the rope running behind her and his footsteps in her ears.

The room was grey with early light. Nimmi's chair was empty. Kiran lay on the pallet on his side with the quilt thrown back.

His chest was moving.

Slowly. Evenly. His hair was plastered dark to his forehead, and the quilt was soaked through. When she put her palm on his brow, it came away wet.

Cool. His skin was cool and slick with sweat, and under it his pulse went on, slow and steady, like a man walking.

"Didi," he said, without opening his eyes. His voice was a rasp. "You're dripping on me."

She was. She hadn't known. She knelt and put her face against the quilt and shook.

A hand settled on her hair, light and clumsy, and she laughed into the wool. Then the hand went still.

She lifted her head. Kiran's eyes were open, clear for the first time in days, and they were not on her. They had gone past her shoulder to the doorway, where Rohan stood at the end of the rope in his half-buttoned coat.

The joke that should have come died on Kiran's mouth. His gaze dropped to Rohan's hands, the split right knuckles, the fingers hanging open, and stayed there. His other hand slid under the pillow, slowly, the way you reach for something in the dark that you are afraid someone will see. She heard the faint scrape of brass on linen. His fist closed around it, the knuckles pale, and stayed closed.

Meera had seen that edge before: brown, with a small dent in the rim. She did not know whose it was.

Rohan had not moved. He could not see the hand behind her. He could see only the boy's face, and the boy would not look up from his hands, and would not let go.

# Chapter 21: Six Days

The first thing Kiran asked for, on being alive, was that someone take the warden out of his sister's pattu.

"It's brown," he said from the pallet, propped on three sacks of walnut leaf. "It's Didi's second-best brown. It has her hole in the hem. Why is a captain of the queen's pass-wardens wearing it?"

"Because the grey one is at the bottom of a sack of leaf," said Rohan, "under the command of your healer. Twelve riders are looking for a man in grey."

"And so you came as a goatherd's wife."

"A goatherd. The sleeves are short."

"They are." Kiran's grin came slowly, as if it had a long way to climb. His face was the colour of tallow and a wool cap was pulled down over both ears, but the eyes were clear. He had slept two days and half a night, and Nimmi had stood over him with a lamp and pronounced the fever broken, and then said it again, as if she did not trust it. "It suits you. Don't take it as a kindness."

Rohan sat on the upturned water bucket by the pallet, with the rope running out through the door behind him. Meera was in the yard with Nimmi's second pot. He could hear her counting cups under her breath.

Nobody had asked him to sit with the boy. He had come in to see that the broth was warm, and found that he did not want to leave. Kiran had Meera's face in a younger cut, with the same quick mouth. Rohan found it hard to be stern with it, and harder to stop looking.

"You'll want to eat," he said.

"I'll want to be sick, first. Then eat." Kiran took the bowl in both hands. "Do you people ever laugh? Wardens? I've watched you at the Kothi gate for three winters, and I've never once seen it."

"We practise in private."

"That was almost a joke."

"It was entirely a joke. It was a poor one." Rohan looked at the bowl, steaming between the thin hands. "I had a warden once who could not be stopped. He sang a hymn at every watch and believed it was a love song. He had no tune. Dilan used to say that the stones had the better ear."

The spoon stopped.

It was a small stop. Kiran's hand held the spoon in the air above the bowl, and his face went smooth and still, the way a pond does when the wind drops. The pallet creaked once. Then he looked up, and the grin was back, a little too bright.

"Is that salt in the broth, or is Nimmi trying to kill me? Tell me it's salt."

"It is walnut leaf, mostly."

"It's vile." He took a sip and made a face. "My sister says you carried water. Eleven trips. She told me on the way in, as if you'd walked on it."

"I carried it badly. The rope made it slower."

"It would."

Rohan let the talk turn. The boy was tired, he thought, and wary of a warden in a small room, as any smuggler would be. A dead man's name was a hard thing to hear from the likes of him. He put it away and fetched the second blanket.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Nimmi caught him in the lane, a bucket in each of his hands, and she had no patience.

"The grain carter came up the lower road this morning," she said. "First one in a month. The regent's men let him through because he had only turnips." He set both buckets down on the frozen mud. "He talks. I let him. He says there's a woman at Garh Neshar, a crown surveyor with a brass tube and a roll of linen, who's been shut up in the guest-house since the first snow. The regent calls it his hospitality. She calls it something else, loudly. She has measured the whole yard and says the north wall is two spans out of true."

Rohan did not move. He could not.

"Is she tall?" he said.

"He said she laughed in the carter's face when he told her the wall was older than the regent. Then she corrected him on the date." Nimmi looked at him. "You know her."

"I know her." His mouth was dry. "Her name is Ila. She is my sister."

He had named her to Meera only once, in anger, at the stone fold. Ila was a line in a surveyor's roll. She was a girl on a stone wall with a plumb-line, thirty years old and certain the world could be measured. Last spring she had written to him that Neshar was lovely, and that the passes would be fully drawn by midwinter.

Winter had come a month early.

"Then you'll want to be at that Fair," said Nimmi, "rather more than before."

"Yes." His voice came out level. It was the voice he used for bad news. "A crown surveyor who has watched the regent's court all winter and writes everything down is the worst thing for him if the seal is pressed. If it is not pressed, she is the first person he will make disappear. She cannot keep from saying things."

"Is that a failing?"

"It is a family one." He picked up the buckets, because his hands wanted something. "Nimmi. How many days to Garh Neshar?"

"A day on the road for a sound man. A day and a half for you." She glanced at his coat. "You should have gone yesterday."

"I know."

He stood in the lane with the ash handprints on the doors on either side of him, and the rope pulled quietly at his wrist. Wherever he went, Meera went. He could not reach his sister without her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"No," said Meera.

She said it without heat. She was sitting on a sack in the storehouse with the count open, and had finished writing before she answered, which was how he knew she meant it.

"Hear it first," said Rohan.

"I heard it. You have a sister at Garh Neshar and five days till the Fair and a day and a half of road, and you want to go at first light tomorrow." She capped the ink. "That's a good plan. It's the plan I'd make. I'm still not going."

"Then I cannot go either."

"I know that too."

"Meera." He kept his hands open on his knees, where she could see them. "If the assembly sits and I am not in it, Pritam is invested on the claim that the queen is dying. Neshar passes into his hand, and Ila with it. I think he has already decided what to do with her."

"I believe you. Look at me. I believe you." She laid her hand flat on the book. "There are forty-one sick in this village. Eighteen courses. Nimmi can brew six cups at a time, so they go in waves. The first wave is the three youngest and Bholu's mother. They need a third dose, and then a second course for the worst of them, and that's six days, Rohan. Six, to do it properly. If I go tomorrow, every child on the list is half-treated, and half-treated is a very good way to bury someone."

"Six days is longer than the Fair."

"I know what it is." Her voice thinned. "I'm not asking for six. I'm telling you what I'd do if I were free, so you know what I'm giving up."

He had not seen it that way, and he was ashamed of that. He had heard a refusal. She was laying out a cost.

"What can you bear?" he said.

"What can *you*?"

It was not the question he expected. He thought of his sister standing in some stone yard with a measuring tube. He thought of Sunni's mound, and the row of them going up the slope.

"Show me the waves," he said.

She turned the book. She had ruled it into boxes, day by day, with a name in every box. He read it slowly, the way he read an order he was about to sign. Near the bottom of the page the two worst cases sat in a bracket, with a small mark beside them.

"The ones with the mark. When do they take their last dose?"

"Dusk tomorrow."

"And after that Nimmi can carry the rest herself. She has done it for thirty years."

"She hates being told that."

"She will hate it less than a fourth winter." He took a breath around the rib. "One more day, then. We leave at first light the morning after. A day and a half of road puts us at Garh Neshar with one day in hand before the Fair, and time to find where Ila is kept. It is less than I wanted. The road may take the spare day, and I am giving you this one."

She did not answer at once. Her fingers moved along the bracket.

"The patrol," she said. "Sixth day, their rule bites. No one in or out of a marked house without a pass. So I leave at dawn, and by the next night Nimmi finishes my waves behind a shut door, with no one to carry water, and I can't come back to mend what goes wrong. Put that in the cost, Rohan. It's mine and I'm paying it."

"Then I put it there."

"One isn't six."

"No."

"It's the dusk dose for the two with the mark." She breathed out. "I can live with those."

"Then you will give me something," said Rohan.

He had not meant it to come out as a bargain. But it was one, and she set the pen down and sat forward on the sack.

"Name it."

"At Garh Neshar you do not wait at the gate while I go in. You come with me to the meadow. In daylight, in front of the assembly, with the seal in my hand." He held her eye. "I know what that costs you. A smuggler in the open, in front of the bell. I would not ask it if the rope did not make it so, and I would not ask it if I did not think you could do it."

"You want me to walk the seal."

"I want you beside me when it is pressed. You carried it first."

She said nothing for a long time. The brazier ticked. Through the wall, Kiran coughed once and was quiet.

"If I walk it in," said Meera slowly, "I'm in the Captain's keeping, in front of the whole valley. And the writ."

"I cannot promise you the writ." He had known she would come to it. "I will not say a thing I cannot hold. The rope would know it, and so would you. I can only say that I will not take you at the gate, and that I do not know what I will do at the thaw. That is the whole of what is true."

"It's more than I expected." Her mouth twisted. "Fine. Yes. I'll walk it in."

"Meera."

"One day," she said. "I'll hold you to the dawn after. If you pull the rope at tomorrow's dusk because you're frightened for her, I swear I'll put you in the well."

"I will not."

"You'll want to."

"I will want to very badly," said Rohan, "and I will not."

Meera went still. Her head tilted, as it did on the Saddle when she listened for the wind to change. Her left wrist turned a little against the red wool.

"It's humming," she said. "Low. It hasn't climbed."

She uncapped the ink and wrote a line under the bracket, then turned the book so he could read it: *One day. R. promised.*

Then she put out her hand, and he took it.

"Done," she said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He drafted the speech that night, by the lamp, on the back of one of Nimmi's tally sheets.

Meera slept on the blankets with her wrist on the rope, curled round the count like a guard round a gate. She slept as she did everything, with a hand ready.

*Elders and lords of Neshar,* he wrote. *I come under the queen's hand, with the queen's seal.*

That was easy. So was the fact of the regent's claim and the proof that the queen was alive. He was a man who wrote reports. He wrote it as one, in short lines, and was pleased with the shape.

Then he came to the law.

*The Closing Law has stood sixty winters,* he wrote. *It was made to keep the passes from those who would buy them.*

That was true. He read it over and felt the old wall of it, solid under his hand.

*It has also,* he wrote, and stopped.

The lamp hissed. What had to come was not hard to know. *It has also kept medicine from Ranu, three winters, and I signed the order.*

He set the pen on the paper and could not make it go.

He did believe it. He had said it in this room with his hand open, and the rope had let it stand. But that had been a confession to one pair of ears. This was a sheet of paper, to be read aloud in front of Pritam's guard and every elder at the Fair. The moment he wrote it down, it was evidence. The regent would take the page and say: *the queen's own captain confesses that the wardens have killed Neshar.* The seal would be pressed in the ruin of its bearer. And Meera, who had written the worst of him into her book, would have to watch him do it.

She had trusted the book. That was more than he was doing.

He wrote, *It has also,* and struck it. He wrote it again. Three times he got as far as the pen touching, and three times he lifted it.

Beside him, Meera stirred. She did not wake. The rope lay quiet, a thing that wanted no lies from him and was not asking for any now.

He folded the sheet small and put it inside his coat, next to the clean-cut end of Dilan's rope.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The next afternoon, in the grey light, the last of Nimmi's first wave finished their dose, and Rohan went in to Kiran with a decision he did not know how to make easy.

Kiran was sitting up without the sacks. He had walked to the window and back on his own that morning, hand on the wall, and was insufferable about it.

"You look like a man about to bring bad news," he said.

"I am about to bring awkward news. Which is a thing a warden does badly."

"Go on, then. Do it badly."

Rohan sat on the bucket. The rope ran out behind him through the open door, taut and still, which meant Meera was in the yard or on the step, no more than eight spans off. He could hear the clink of the brazier pot. It gave him a courage he had not thought to ask for.

"I am going to ask you to forgive me for something," he said. "Perhaps not now. You are Meera's brother, and the Kothi people are yours, and what I say will not be pleasant for them."

Kiran's face did not change. Only his hands moved, drawing the quilt a little higher.

"Two winters ago I lost a warden on the Saddle. Dilan. I told you about the hymn." Rohan looked at the clean line of the boards. "He was chasing a smuggler in a storm. The smuggler's rope was with his, and the smuggler lived. Dilan did not. I have the end of the rope in my coat. I swore before eleven wardens that I would find the one at the other end of it."

The room was very quiet.

"I told myself it was the law. It was not. It was Dilan." He drew breath. "And I have lived on your sister's mountain for half a month, and I have been fed, and mended, and written in a book, and I have begun to think that when I find that smuggler I will not know what to do. I have never said so to anyone. I wanted to say it to someone who might know the crews. If you know whose rope—"

Kiran had stopped hearing him.

His eyes were on the doorway, over Rohan's shoulder. His face had gone the grey of ash, with the colour dragged out of it from the mouth upward. He made a small sound that was not a word.

Rohan turned.

Meera stood in the doorway with the water jug against her hip, her hair coming down, a question on her face. She looked from one of them to the other.

"What did I miss?"

The quilt went back. Kiran was on his feet, which Rohan would not have said he could manage, one hand clawing at the wall. He went past the bucket and past Rohan's reaching arm. He went past his sister's shoulder so close that the jug struck the frame and slopped, and then he was out across the yard in his stockings, the wool cap jammed low over his ears, running down the lane on the frozen mud.

"Kiran!" Meera dropped the jug. The rope went tight across Rohan's wrist as she turned to follow. "Kiran, *stop*—"

He did not stop.

Rohan stood in the empty room with the sentence still open in his mouth. *If you know whose rope.*

# Chapter 22: The Badge

Kiran made it forty paces.

Meera ran the lane with the rope snapping out behind her, and Rohan's footsteps came fast and uneven at the other end of it. She found her brother where the lane ended, behind Nimmi's goat shed, folded over the woodpile in his stockings. He clung to the split logs with both arms, as if the ground were tilting.

Ten paces back, the storehouse door stood open. The rope ran from her wrist to the doorway and no further. Eight arm-spans. The line went hard at her left wrist and then stopped.

"Stay in there," she called. "Rohan. Shut the door."

A pause. Then the door scraped to, leaving a gap for the rope and no more. He did not argue. He had heard what was in her voice, and he stayed on his side of the plank wall as a man holds still beside a trap.

She knelt in the frozen mud, pulled her pattu off her shoulders and put it round her brother. He was shaking in long hard waves. His cap had slid back. The left ear, the bad one, was a lump of puckered grey-pink, its rim eaten away.

"You'll lose toes," she said. "Put your feet in my lap."

"Didi."

"Feet."

He did it. She took them in her hands, the wool stiff with ice, and rubbed. It gave her something to do with her face.

"What did he say?" Her voice came out flat. "At the end. *If you know whose rope.* I only got the last of it. Whose rope, Kiran?"

He did not answer. He put a hand inside his shirt, slowly, as he had under the pillow, and drew out the badge. It was brass gone brown, palm-wide, the queen's goat head stamped on it, a small dent in the rim. He had worn it against his skin on a string. The string had left a pink line on his neck.

He held it out in his fist, and it shook.

"Take it."

"Whose is it?"

"Take it, Didi."

She took it. It was warm from his body. On the back, scratched with a nail point by an unsteady hand, were three letters. She read them with her thumb before her eyes.

*DIL.*

She knew the name. Rohan had given it to her in the hut, and the rest with it. A warden who sang a hymn at every watch and had no tune. A rope end in a coat, cut clean. *I swore my oath over that rope.*

"Say it," she said, very quietly. "Say who he was."

Kiran's mouth moved. Nothing came. She watched him try, as she had watched him at nine try to say the word for the thing that had frightened him in the dark. His jaw worked. He looked at the sky over the goat shed, then at the frozen ground, then at the badge in her hand. His eyes filled.

"I can't," he said. "I've never. I tried at the cairn last winter, on my own, and it wouldn't come out. It's like a stone in the throat."

"Then don't. Tell it without."

He nodded. He drew his knees up under her pattu, and she thought he would be sick. Then he began, in a voice with all the quickness gone out of it.

"It was the storm night. The one two winters back, the one I said was a bad crossing. I had four loads of salt for the Dev people, and I went up alone. I know. I *know*. I lost the line inside an hour, just like they say you will, and I turned back the way I'd come and found the waystone by feel." He swallowed. "There was a warden there. He'd been following my tracks, and he'd lost the line as fast as I had. His hands were as white as mine. He was young, Didi. Not much older than me. He had no business out in that, and he came right at me, and I thought, that's it, that's the rope. And he didn't even draw. He said, *You're going to die up here, you idiot. Come down with me and be hanged in the morning like a sensible person.*"

A sound came out of her that was nearly a laugh. It hurt.

"He bound us," Kiran said. "Right there, at the stones. His rope, grey wool. Knot on my wrist, knot on his. We said our names to the waystone, and he named the third bend for where we were going, the nearest road with a roof on it. He said it was so I couldn't run. I felt it take. The blue came up on the stones, and I thought, I'll live." His voice cracked. "We were on the line, Didi. We were safe from the mountain. It was the ice that wasn't. We got to the shelf above the third bend and it went. Not a big slide. Just the ground under his feet, and him going, and the rope coming tight, and I went flat and started to slide after him on the glaze. My axe was gone. I could see the lip. I could see him past it, hanging, with the whole weight of me coming down the ice. And he looked up at me, and he said—"

He stopped. His breath came in short bursts.

"He said something," Meera prompted.

"He said *sorry about the hymn.* I don't know what that means. I've never known what it means." Kiran put his fist against his mouth. "Then he had his knife. He cut his own rope close to his wrist, and it went slack, and I stopped. Right at the lip. I stopped with the cut end in my hands. And he was gone."

The goat shed ticked in the wind. In the storehouse, on the other side of a plank wall, a boot shifted on a floor.

"We'd slid clear down onto the road ice, below the bend," said Kiran. "That's why I'm here. The line was gone, with him. I was off the pass, on a plain road, and a man alone can live on a road. I lay there. I couldn't make my arms move. The ice had my ear. It froze to the glaze while I lay on it, and when it got light I had to tear it loose. That's the bad crossing. I told you a bad crossing. You never asked for the rest."

"I should have."

"I'd have told you a lie. I was very good at it by then." He laughed, a dry rattle with no joy in it. "There was a brass thing in the snow by my hand. His badge. The string had broken. I took it. I couldn't leave it on the ice with him down there. And I walked home with it, and I haven't spoken his name since, and I haven't put it down."

She sat with her brother's feet in her lap. The badge was a small cold weight on her palm.

She knew the rest already, and for a moment she let herself not know it. The ice shelf. The hymn. The warden who died with a rope cut clean. Rohan, in the lamplight, retying and untying the same rope end. *To find the one who stood at its far end.* An oath in a warden hall, two winters of searching, and the far end of the rope had lain in the next room of her own house, joking about wardens' laughter.

It had been in her ledger all along. Her brother, beside her, an arm's length from the man who was hunting him.

"He's in there," said Kiran. His voice had gone small. "The captain. I knew when I woke and saw the sleeves. I knew who he was before he opened his mouth, and I thought, if I don't look at him, if I just keep talking about the broth. Then he said the name, and said it so gently. He said *I swore.*" His hand closed on her wrist, hard. "Didi. He doesn't know. He asked me if I knew whose rope."

"I heard."

"A death on an unlawful crossing hangs. The warden died chasing a smuggler in the closed season. That's the law. It doesn't matter that I never touched him. It doesn't matter that he cut it himself. They'll say I led him up there. They'll say I ran." He was weeping now, without sound. "I'm not afraid of dying. I'm only afraid of the deodar at the gate. You know the pegs. I used to count them, going past."

"I know."

"Don't tell him. Please. Didi, I've never asked you for one thing."

She did not say yes.

Her whole body wanted to. The word sat behind her teeth, warm and easy. But she was on the rope. The rope did not distinguish between a promise made to a brother in a woodpile and one made to a lover in a hut. A promise was a contract with the mountain. If she gave it and one day broke it, she would pay as for a great lie, and the cold would not ask her reasons.

"I can't promise," she said. "Listen to me. I can't, and you know why. If I swear and it goes wrong, it takes my hands."

"Then—"

"But I can choose, and I'm choosing now." She took his chin and turned his face to hers. "I'm not going in there tonight to tell him. I'm not saying I never will. That's all I can give you, and I'm giving it with my eyes open."

Kiran's wet eyes searched her face. "That's not *never*."

"No."

"You picked it careful."

"I did." She had. She had built the sentence as she would build a handhold on rotten rock, so the mountain would find nothing in it to grip. Tonight was a thing she could do. Never was a thing the cold could hold her to. "Quiet is free. It always has been. And if he asks, then it'll be a different day. Don't make me have that one now."

He let his head fall against her shoulder. He was thin as a fence-post under the pattu. She held him, and in her other hand the dead boy's badge dug into her palm.

Rohan would have to be told, someday. That was plain, and she made herself look at it. He was a man who signed the blockade and said so. Told the truth, a man like that could do one of two things. He could report Kiran, as the law demanded and as he had demanded of himself for two winters, and see the boy hanged at the peg beside the notice. Or he could break his oath before eleven men, and the law with it, and everything he had built his face around.

*If I bend once, nothing holds.* He had said it in the hut, and the rope had agreed.

She could force him to choose tonight, with Kiran barely mended and the Fair four days off. Or she could say nothing and let him walk on toward the seal and his sister. He had a thing he must do before the Fair. He did not need this on top.

It was not a choice between telling and lying. She would not lie. It was a choice between speaking and keeping quiet, and for as long as he never asked, the quiet cost her nothing.

That was the part that frightened her. It cost her nothing at all.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She got Kiran back on his feet. His cap stayed in the mud by the woodpile. They went in by the back of Nimmi's house, her arm round him, his stockings leaving wet prints on the boards. The passage ran by the storehouse, and the storehouse door opened before she reached it.

Rohan stood there with one hand on the frame. He had not slept, she could tell, or had not wanted to. The rope lay slack across his boots.

Kiran kept his head down and shuffled past, and the bare ear went by at the height of Rohan's chest.

Rohan's eyes followed it.

Meera watched them go. They went to the puckered rim, the missing curl of it, and stayed one breath too long for chance. He had a warden's report in his head, she knew. Blood on the ice, and a scrap of skin frozen fast to it, torn from the rim of an ear. Had the two come together behind his face? She could not tell. His face gave her nothing but a tired man looking at a sick boy. She was afraid they had.

Then Nimmi came out of the sickroom, took one look and took the boy, and said nothing, which for Nimmi was a speech. The door shut. Rohan's eyes came back to Meera's.

"Is he warm?" he said.

"He's warm. He'll keep his toes."

"Good."

That was all. She waited for the rest. He stood there with a question close to the surface of his face, and it did not come. She saw him lay something down, like a man setting a heavy sack on a shelf, with care, and stepping back from it. He had seen something. Whether he knew what it was, she could not say. He had chosen not to ask.

"He ran from me," said Rohan. "I know why a smuggler runs from a warden. I should have known better than to speak of crews in his hearing, while he was ill. I put him in fear, in a bed he could not leave." He looked at her. "Whatever it was he heard, it is his. If he wants to say it, he will. I will not go in and ask him."

"Rohan—"

"You do not have to tell me anything." His voice was low. "You made that rule in the hut. I keep it. I would be a poor sort of man if I only kept it when it suited me."

She had braced for probing. She had not braced for this. It would have been easier to hate.

She stepped in and put her forehead against his chest, on the good side. His hand came up and rested on her back, light, as if she were a thing he had decided not to break.

"Thank you," she said into his coat. It was true. Her hands, against his ribs, were warm. That was what shamed her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The dusk dose went round at the proper hour. Meera carried the cups herself, steadily, two at a time. Rohan walked at her shoulder in the borrowed brown pattu and took the pot when it grew heavy. Neither of them spoke much. Nimmi counted the two marked cases through, one by one, with her lips moving, and wrote the number in chalk on the lintel beside the ash.

Kiran slept through it, or let them think he did. The badge was cold against her ribs.

Later they lay on the blankets in the storehouse with the lamp out and the rope tied between their wrists. Rohan had settled on his right side with his back to the wall and his arm over her waist. His breathing slowed. After a while it stopped trying to be even, and she knew he was asleep.

She lay awake and looked at the dark.

Eight inches from her lay a man who had told her his sins in plain words with his hands open, and carried her water, and said *I do not know what I will do*. Every true thing he had given her, he had given while exposing his own throat. And she, who had asked him for them, now held in her shirt the one thing that was entirely about his wound.

Seven nights ago she had thought honesty was the whole of the rope.
