She understood now that it had a shadow. Silence was free, but free was not the same as clean, and this silence would sit between them every hour that he reached for her. He would be tender, and she would feel the badge, and she would know what he did not. Or what he might. She did not know which was worse. Somewhere ahead was a day when he would stop being kind and become a captain again.

She went through the possible questions as she went through a cornice, looking for the one that would take the ground.

He could ask what the crew did. He could ask about Asha. Either she could meet with a held tongue and a burned stick.

Only one had no way round it.

*Was Kiran on Dilan's rope?*

If she said yes, her brother hanged. If she said no, it was a great lie, about the one thing that would change every choice he had left to make, and she knew what a great lie did. It did what the frost had done to Hakam's hand in the yard of Kothi Shilak: three fingers where there should have been five. And if she said nothing, and burned a stick, and looked at him, he would know that, too.

He did not have the name yet. When he got it, it would be the only thing he needed to ask.

He slept beside her. His thumb moved once against her hip in his sleep, a small, trusting motion, and went still.

Outside the wind came down off the Saddle and rattled the ash-marked lintel. Somewhere beyond the wall, her brother was breathing, slowly, like a man walking.

She lay with her eyes open and counted the one thing she had left to count: how many days there were until he asked.

# Chapter 23: Knots for Children

Meera had spoken six words since waking, and Rohan, who counted what mattered, had counted every one.

*Warm enough. No. Later. Yes. Eat.* The last had been for Kiran, through a wall.

The snow had come in the night and lay knee-deep in the lane. They had meant to be gone at first light. Nimmi came in stamping her boots and said the lower road had drifted shut at the gully bend, that the carter had turned back before dawn, and that no sound man would walk it until tomorrow. Rohan did the sums twice, the way he did them for a burial roll. He told Meera what the delay cost: a day he did not have, spent. She nodded and went on rolling blankets.

She was holding something inside her coat. He had watched her do it all morning, a flat hand pressed to her ribs, as a person holds a bruise they have decided not to mention. She had not shown him what it was. He had a notion about it, and he did not pick the notion up.

He could have asked. The rope would have let him, and she would have answered true or burned a stick for it. That was the rule she had made in the hut, and it was the one thing in this house that was entirely hers. So he handed her the water jug, left his questions on the shelf, and found that holding still cost more than the climb had.

Nimmi laid the last of the redbark on the table.

It was not much to look at: the bottom of one packet and the creased waxed paper of another. "Three doses, what Sunni never finished," she said. "The Dev boy's father. Kamla's two, who came up out of that sleep on walnut leaf and spite. After that I brew walnut leaf and I swear at it."

"Twenty-three went without," said Meera.

"Twenty-three would have gone without if you'd never come. Don't." Nimmi folded her arms. "There's a buyer. Masked. Pays the lower-lane runners for any medicine that crosses, last winter and the one before. Silver, no haggling. Some of it found its way up my back road. I never met him."

Rohan's mind wrote the charge down before he could stop it: *trafficking*, then *harbouring*, in a neat clerk's hand. He watched his own fingers not reach for a pen.

"Does the lane still carry for him?" Meera asked.

"The lane carries for anyone who pays. Kiran's lot tie a cord on my latch when they want a meeting. Two knots, plain, no tail. It means the grove behind the house, by night." Nimmi looked from Meera's face to Rohan's. "I can get word down the lane. I won't promise what comes back."

"Do it," said Meera.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At midday Nimmi let the three well enough to sit up into the kitchen, where the chulha was, and told Rohan to keep them off the floor and out of the cold. He had no talent for it. He sat on the bench with a coil of Nimmi's spare cord and the red rope running out of the door at his wrist, and three pairs of very serious eyes watched it.

Kamla's girl was five and had lost her front teeth. "Is that for hanging?"

"It is for walking."

"It's tied to a lady."

"It is tied to a guide," said Rohan. "She is in charge of me. This is called being in the care of an expert."

The Dev boy, who was eight and had the look of someone who had decided to be unimpressed, said, "Wardens tie people up. Everybody knows."

"Some of us tie knots. Some of us are tied by them." Rohan took the cord and made a bight. "Recruits at the Shilak post learn a game before they learn the real work. One of you ties me. The rest watch for the trick. If I can slip it, I win. If I cannot, you do."

Nobody had asked for a game. But the girl came off the bench and put her small wet hands on his wrist, and he showed her the turn and the tuck, and she tied him with the fierce care of a child wrapping a gift.

He slipped it in four breaths.

"*Again*," said the Dev boy.

They tied him four more times. He taught them the half-turn that looks locked and isn't, and the one that holds against any pull, the double hitch he tied on everything he valued. The Dev boy got it on his second try and turned it over with grim satisfaction. Kamla's boy, who had said nothing and eaten the whole bowl of broth, wound the rest of the cord about Rohan's good wrist and the bench leg and sat on the knot.

"You're arrested," said the Dev boy.

"On what charge?"

The boy thought about it. "Sleeves."

Rohan looked down at the borrowed brown pattu, where the cuffs stopped a hand's width above his wrists. "I admit it," he said. "I plead guilty."

Someone laughed in the doorway.

It was not a polite laugh. It came out rough and surprised, and it bent her over the frame with a hand on her stomach. Meera stood there in the cold with a pot against her hip, laughing until she had to wipe her eyes on her wrist, looking at a captain of the queen's pass-wardens pinned to a bench by a child of three.

He had not heard it since Sunni. He had not known he was waiting to.

"Don't," said Rohan. "I am a prisoner. Mocking me is undignified."

"Plead guilty," she gasped. "To the *sleeves*."

"It is a serious offence."

The children shrieked. The little one bounced on the knot. And Rohan sat there, tied by a child, and let himself look at her face with the colour in it. It lasted perhaps as long as a held breath. Then he saw her catch herself, a hand coming flat against her coat again, the laugh going out of her.

She put the pot down. "I'll see to the water."

He freed himself. It took one pull, because the child had tied it as he had taught her to, and it held.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They packed in the storehouse in the grey afternoon. It took no time at all. There was little enough to carry: the count, the dry sticks of a fire they no longer needed, two sacks of Nimmi's walnut leaf for the lower road. His grey pattu came up from the bottom of its sack smelling of camphor, and he rolled it at his belt.

Meera knelt on the blankets with the red rope across her knees, checking it from end to end for wear. She checked it every night. This time she checked it twice.

"Meera."

She looked up. The distance was already in her eyes. It was not hostility, only a care in how she held herself, a person walking over a floor she knew was thin.

"May I say a thing that is not a promise?"

Something moved at the corner of her mouth. "Those are the only kind that are free."

"I intend to petition the queen to strike the clause from my writ that names the Salt Fox." He said it slowly, so it would all be there. "I will put my name to the petition. It will say the writ was drawn on a false report, and that the Guide of Shilak carried my life across the Saddle and my seal to the Fair. If the queen is in a mood to be offended, it will cost me the post. Perhaps more. I cannot tell you she will grant it. I cannot tell you I will be alive to ask." He held his hands open on his knees. "So I do not promise. I would rather give you a sentence that is true than a larger one that is not."

The rope lay across her knees and made no sound. A promise would have hummed. This did not, and he saw her feel the difference, the small stillness in her wrist as she listened to nothing.

He had weighed the word for an hour that morning, over the dull task of packing. A promise already rode this rope, made in a hut in a storm, to not let her fall. He was careful about adding to it. A man who swears what he cannot guarantee has the cold for a bride, and he had seen how that went. He had watched her hand go white on the Saddle over a lie the size of a pebble.

"You said *I intend*," said Meera.

"Yes."

"You could have made it prettier. You could have said *I swear it* and let me hold it." Her voice was low, and not as steady as she meant it to be. "You said it so I could stand on it."

"I thought you would prefer something that would bear weight."

"I do." Her thumb moved along the wool. "Rohan. Your career. The writ is the only thing you've got besides the seal, and you've just—"

"I know what I have just done."

She put out her hand and laid it over his wrist, the bare skin below the short sleeve. Her fingers were warm. He remembered the pitch above the waystone, when she had taken his cold, and what it had cost her.

"Thank you," she said.

It was the right word. It was said with her whole face. And somewhere beneath it, he heard a door that had been open a hand's width close to a finger's. She looked at his coat, where the cord of the seal and the end of a dead man's rope lay folded together, and then away. She drew her hand back.

He did not reach after it. It was hers to take back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Night came early. Meera sat with Kiran through the wall, the rope threading the gap in the boards between them, and Rohan took the lamp and the back of a tally sheet and wrote to his sister.

He did not know how it would reach her. The carter was gone and the regent's men held the road. He had written to the families of seven buried wardens on much worse terms than these. A letter was a way of putting a thing in order.

*Ila,* he wrote. *I am alive. I am coming. The snow has taken the day I held in hand, so I will reach Garh Neshar only on the evening before the Fair. If the road takes another, I will push through the night and come to the meadow on the morning itself. Say nothing in the yard about me to anyone who sits at his table. You will want to correct someone. Do not.*

*I carry a thing I will not name in a letter. I have been wrong about a number of things this winter, and I am learning to say so aloud, which is harder than the climbing. There is a woman here who has taught me some of it. I will tell you about her when you have sat down.*

*I will tell you about Dilan's rope as well. I think you would have liked him. He could not carry a tune.*

He read it over. It was not a report. He did not know what it was. He struck out *a woman here* and wrote it again, and left it.

*Mind the north wall. It offends me to think of it two spans out of true.*

He folded it into his coat, beside the speech he could not finish. On the other side of the boards, Meera said something low to her brother, and Kiran answered, and Rohan caught the rhythm of it without the words. Siblings, talking low in a dark room. He had gone without that sound for a long time.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The knock came as he was banking the brazier.

Nimmi stood in the passage with her shawl over her head and snow on the shoulders of it. In her hand was a length of undyed wool cord with three hard knots near one end and a long tail ending in a loop.

"On my latch," she said. "A moment ago. No tracks."

Meera was through the door before the rope drew taut. She took the cord from Nimmi's fingers and ran her thumb along it, counting the knots as she counted everything.

"Three," she said. "And a tail." She frowned. "Kiran's crew stop at two, Nimmi, and cut the end square. I've tied it that way myself. This isn't ours."

Rohan knew the two-knot cord. It was in a file at the Shilak post, among the seized things of three winters: *crew signal, two knots, meeting by night.* He knew this one too, from a lesser page: *unknown signal, three-and-tail, twice found on lower-lane latches, never answered.* He had written *seen* beside it more than once. He had never found anyone at the other end.

"Then it's the grove all the same," said Nimmi. "The loop points that way. The runners have a pouch tied just so when the silver comes, and not one of them will say who from." She looked at the cord. "It's him."

"The buyer."

"Nobody pays for medicine and asks nothing," said Nimmi. "Nobody ever has."

"No." Meera's hand closed over the cord. "He doesn't send for me for free. Moonrise."

She tucked it into her coat, beside the thing she had been holding there all day. Rohan watched it go. Then she looked at him across the lamp, and he saw her wait for him to say what a captain would say.

"Then we go to the grove," he said.

It had come out too fast, and he made himself slow down. The cord had hung on lower-lane latches for three winters and no one had answered it. That was what made it a fine thing to leave, if a man wished to learn who would come. The regent's people were hunting a man in grey, and they could hardly ask for better bait than a sick village's need, with the one woman on the mountain who would walk toward it.

"It may be set for me," he said. "Nobody has ever answered it. I would like you to hear that said aloud."

"I heard it the moment Nimmi did." Her mouth was thin. "He still has the medicine."

"Yes."

So he went on, because the next thing was worse. In the hut he had sworn he would not let her fall on this mountain, and the rope had hummed. If he now stood behind a wall with his tongue held while she met a stranger in the dark, those two vows could pull against each other. If they did, one would break and strike him as a great lie strikes. He knew which one he would break. He knew what it would cost him, and he judged the price was his to carry, not hers.

"I will keep behind the wall, at the full length of the rope."

"You know the length."

"Eight spans. I have carried it fifteen days." He did not look away. "I will not interrupt you, and I will not ask afterwards what was said."

The rope hummed.

It was low and level, and it ran up the wool into her wrist. He saw her feel it. Her hand went to her coat and stayed there, flat over the place where the cord had gone, and her face closed, quietly, like a shutter drawn across a lit room. He could not read what lay behind it. He could read only that his promise had weighed more than he had meant it to.

It was a long moment before she nodded, and her nod held less than the one before.

He took the lamp and put it out. In the dark, the red rope drew taut between them and held, and he could not tell whether it was she who pulled.

# Chapter 24: Cut-Corner Silver

The wall ran along the bottom of the grove, dry-stone and chest high, and the red rope ran over it.

Meera walked until the line went taut at her left wrist, then took one more step so the wool bit. Eight arm-spans. On the far side of the stones Rohan sat in the snow with his back against them. She could not see him. She could feel him on the other end, still as a post.

Ahead, the walnut trees stood black under a rising moon. Beneath the largest sat a man on a folded blanket, as if he had come to watch a fair. A pale wool scarf covered his face from the nose down, and a hood hid the rest.

"Guide of Shilak," he said. "You came alone."

"I came with a wall. Don't ask what's behind it."

"Should I?"

His voice was low and unhurried, a voice taught in a good house. She stopped where the rope allowed, a long stride short of him, and kept her hands out of her pockets so he could see them.

"You pay the lower lane for medicine," she said. "For two winters. Why?"

"Why does anyone pay for medicine?"

"That's not an answer."

"Would you trust it if it were?"

She almost smiled. She had dealt with Taravan factors who talked in circles, but they circled toward money. This man circled toward nothing, and he did it politely, which was worse.

"Twelve packets of redbark crossed Shilak in my pack," she said. "Nine came through sound. They're in Ranu. You'll want to know that."

"Am I owed them?"

"No. They were never for sale."

"Then what do you call it, when a man pays for what he was not sold?"

A light was bobbing in the trees behind her.

She turned, hand dropping toward the knife she did not wear. A clay lamp swung up through the walnut trunks at the height of her hip, held in two small mittens, and under it came a face she knew. Nimmi's grandson. Six years old, in his grandmother's second shawl, with the fringe dragging in the snow.

"Pinku," she whispered. "Go back."

"You went out," said Pinku. "Everybody said stay. Nobody said why."

The masked man was already on his feet. He did not hurry, and he did not loom. He folded down onto his heels in the snow so his hood sat below the boy's eyes.

"Does your grandmother know where you are?"

"She said stay."

"And you are a person of great independence." He took the lamp, gently, and held it where the wind would not reach the flame. "Then walk back in my footsteps, so the snow keeps your secret. Can you do that?"

Pinku considered. "Are you a robber?"

"Do I look like a robber?"

"You look like a scarf."

The man's shoulders shook. He put something in the boy's mitten, and when the fingers opened it was a walnut, whole and dark, with a ribbon of red thread knotted round the shell. "For the long walk. Open it when you are inside."

The boy went. The lamp's glow dwindled down the trees, and the buyer watched it all the way, his head turned so Meera saw only the line of his hood.

She had meant to dislike him. She put that down for later.

"Well," he said, rising. "Your silver."

He took a purse from his coat and tipped it into her glove. The coins were thin squares, heavier than lowland bits, and each had one corner clipped off clean, as though a mouse had bitten it. She turned one in the moonlight. The cut was neat. Someone had made it with a tool, forty times in the same place.

"What's wrong with them?"

"What would be wrong with silver?"

"Nothing is wrong with silver. Something is wrong with this." She counted. Nineteen. She closed her fist on them and, from habit, fixed the cut in her mind as she would fix the shape of a face. "I'll write it in the count. I write everything."

"Then write that it spends."

Next he lifted a canvas sack from behind the tree and set it between them. It was heavy and square, bound with tarred cord. On the cloth, inked in black, was a bell: a small brass assembly bell, the seal of a house with a brass bell in its hall.

She did not touch it yet.

"That is the regent's own," she said.

"Is it?"

"It's his store mark. The grain sacks in Ranu carry the same." Her throat had gone tight. "That's his redbark. From his cellar."

"Does a cellar care whom it feeds?" He pushed the sack an inch toward her with his boot. "Twenty-three go without in that village. I would rather they did not. There is enough here to finish them and some over, if your healer does not stretch it into water."

The wall behind her was silent. She could feel Rohan listening to nothing, doing exactly as he had sworn. It was worse than if he had spoken.

"There's more," said the masked man, and the lightness went out of his voice for the first time. "You won't like it."

"I don't like any of this."

"The captain's lieutenant. A tall man, handsome, polite about regulations. Did you think the mountain kept him?"

The cold went through her faster than the wind. "Varun."

"He crossed the Saddle eleven days after you, bound at the Shilak Cairn to a salt-road man who would speak his own name for the price. They came down through Garh Neshar. He sleeps in the regent's guest wing." The wool of the scarf moved with his breath. "A woman called Asha rides with him. The regent has put a warrant in his hand. It names a seal-thief and a traitor, and it names a captain of wardens. They leave Garh Neshar tonight with twenty men. They will reach Ranu at dawn."

She heard the number and could not make it mean anything. Then it did. The drift at the gully bend had turned the carter back, but it would not turn twenty men with shovels and a warrant. The lower road was reopening behind it, dug out for the crown's riders by the regent's own hands.

"Dawn," she said.

"Dawn."

"How do you know?"

"Do you think a regent's hall has thin walls?"

"Do you always do this?" she snapped. "Everything I ask, a question back. People are dying in that house."

"Yes," he said quietly. "That is why I came myself."

It was not an answer, and it silenced her better than one.

"Your name," she said.

He went still.

"I want it. I'll carry your sack into a sick village. I'll spend your silver. I'd like to put a name in the count beside it."

"Why does a smuggler want names?" He tilted his head. "Names are what the pegs in the deodar at your gate are for. You know that better than any woman living."

She did. A deodar full of pegs, and every one begun with a name.

"A debt is a reason," she said.

"Is it?"

"Not for you, apparently."

"Then keep the debt," said the masked man. "It's easier to carry than a name." He bowed, the short bow of a man who had been taught it as a boy and had never found a way to stop. "Walk quickly, Guide. Mind your wall."

He was gone through the trunks before she had finished lifting the sack. She stood with nineteen clipped squares in her glove and a regent's bell on the cloth. Her left hand was pressed flat to her coat, over the brass badge Kiran had pressed into her hand at the woodpile, and the hand was cold with plain night air and nothing else.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Rohan stood when she came over the wall. He had stood before she cleared it, so the rope would not drag her, and his hand was out to take the sack.

He did not ask.

She watched him not ask. It showed in his mouth, in how he held his weight, in how he turned the sack over and saw the bell and read it. His eyes came up with the question plain in them and his lips shut around it. The promise lay on him like a coat he had chosen.

"You said you wouldn't ask," said Meera.

"I did."

"I'm going to tell you anyway." Her voice was steadier than she was. "That's allowed. It's the only part I get to choose."

She told it plainly, and in order. The questions, the silver, the bell. When she reached Varun, his face did not move. Only the hand on the sack tightened until the cloth crackled.

"Alive," he said.

"Twenty men. Dawn."

"Then the slide did not keep him." Rohan said it as a man reads a figure from a roll, flat and low. "I knew it in the Rope-Hut. I think I wanted it to be a thing I only knew in the hut."

"We could be gone in an hour." She had already counted the road. "Not the lower lane. There's a goat track over the shoulder above the walnut terraces, high and bad, and it comes out well away from the road they're digging open. Garh Neshar's a day and a half with you on foot. If we're off the slope before their horns, we've the long start—"

"And Kiran?"

She stopped.

"You mean to leave him behind," Rohan said. His voice was very gentle. "Under that warrant, he is a smuggler in a marked house, and he cannot stand at the woodpile without the wall to hold. He is too weak to flee. You know it. Twenty men will ride whichever road we take, Meera. They will not need to find us. They will find him."

"The soldiers will mark every door."

"Then we will do what is possible before they come." He set the sack down on the wall. "Nimmi can brew six cups to a pot. Four pots before light. Between us, we can carry them."

She looked at him. He had weighed it already, she saw, in the minutes it had taken her to climb a wall. He had counted, the sums did not come out in his favour, and he had set them down anyway.

"If I stay, it may cost me the Fair," he said. "A warrant strips a writ. By noon I may be a prisoner with no paper to show a gate, and the meadow will go on without me. Hear the rest. The seal does not need me to reach the Fair. It needs hands that are not in irons. If it stays on my body, it is Varun's by noon and the mission dies with my arrest. If it is hidden, it outlives me."

Then he put two fingers to the nape of his coat.

The seam gave to the small knife he had once stirred sattu with. He worked the stitches loose without hurry, his good hand steady, and drew out a thing she had held once in a fever hut and had given back. Lapis, blue as a shut eye, the goat cut fine on its face. It hung from a cord tied off in the double hitch.

He held it out on his palm.

"Take it."

"Rohan—"

"Varun will search me. He knows exactly what to look for, because I told him." His mouth moved, bitterly. "I told him in the post room, and I thought it was loyalty. If it is on me, it is his. If it is on you, it may be hidden. Hide it where I can't lie about it."

"You can't lie to me."

"To anyone else I can, and will, and the rope will let me." He did not look away. "I would rather be searched honest. Do not tell me where. If I do not know, I cannot give it up, and I cannot be made to say."

She understood what he was putting in her hand. Not a stone. The whole Fair, every mile of it. He had carried it alone through an avalanche and a fever, and the one time she had held it, while he burned, she had sewn it back into his coat unasked. Now he gave it to the woman he had been ordered to deliver at the thaw, as simply as passing the salt.

She took it. It was cold from the night and warm from him.

The cord had come loose from long wearing. She slid the stone through her fingers, found the standing part, and tied it again, two turns round, a tuck, and the locking half-turn. She did not snug it as hard as she had in the hut, when she had been afraid and angry. She tied it as he did, with a little slack to let it breathe. Three children had learned it from him at midday on a kitchen bench.

He watched her hands.

"You've learned it," he said.

"I was shown by an expert."

His eyes creased, briefly. It did not stay.

Her left hand, the one that held the cord, pressed against her coat, and under the wool she felt the edge of the badge. She did not move it. The rope said nothing. He had not asked her a question, and nothing she had said was false.

But she knew exactly how much she was holding, and in whose hands.

"Now," said Rohan. He turned to the wall, set his forehead near the stones and shut his eyes. "I will count aloud. Go the whole length of the rope. I will not look."

"Rohan."

"One," he said. "Two."

She went. The rope paid out behind her, and he would feel its pull and know which way she had gone. A direction was not a place. At the end of the eight spans lay three choices, each ordinary and none safe: the house wall with its cracks, the goat shed with its loft, the woodpile with its hundred hiding gaps. She thought about who would turn out what first. A soldier checks a loft before he lifts logs. He checks a house before either. She weighed it the way she weighed bales on a salt-house floor.

"Forty," Rohan said behind her, to the wall, in the flat voice he used for burial rolls. "Forty-one."

She chose the woodpile behind the goat shed. She knelt at the third stack, where the split ends faced the wall, and pushed the lapis deep into the gap between two knotted deodar rounds. Then she pulled a curl of loose bark across the hole and set a billet of walnut over it. It took less time than she wanted and more than she could afford. When she came back along the rope her hands were empty and her face was set, and the count had reached a hundred and sixty.

He opened his eyes and did not look at her hands.

"Go and see to Kiran," Rohan said. "I will wake Nimmi and heat the water."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They brewed four pots before light.

Nimmi pinched the redbark out of the new sack with her lips pressed so thin they vanished, and said nothing about the bell on the cloth. Meera carried cups two at a time down the passage. Rohan carried the pots. By the third round the Dev boy's father had colour in his lips, and Kamla's girl had been spoken to sharply for sitting up.

Kiran drank his dose propped against the wall, his eyes on Rohan's back all the way across the room. Meera stood between them for a breath and then moved, so that the line ran through her.

"Didi," said Kiran, very low. "What's that noise?"

"Nothing yet."

"Is it a nothing that's coming?"

"Drink."

The sky in the high window had gone the grey of an old pot. Nimmi was chalking the last tick on the lintel when the sound came down the lane from the lower road, thin and far, rising through the snow-dull air.

A horn. Two notes, falling.

She knew it. She had heard it on the Saddle's lip, climbing out of the dark with a line of torches behind it. Rohan lifted his head at the same moment, and across the passage their eyes met, and neither of them said a word. The red rope between their wrists went taut and quiet.

More horns answered. Boots. The slap of harness on frozen air, and the bells on many shoulders, a thin brass jingle running up the lane like water.

Then a voice, carrying and cheerful, friendly as a neighbour's at a gate. It came from the lower road, and she had last heard it giving orders at a post-room table.

"Captain-ji! Captain-ji, are you in there? It's only me!"

# Chapter 25: Captain-ji

The soldiers were fanning out before the horns had finished, and Rohan counted the doors they were walking toward.

Seven marked houses on the lane. Forty-one sick, twenty-three of them one night into the buyer's redbark, and every one of them lying behind a wall of planks. He had seen what a search did to a sickroom. Boots on the floor. A blanket lifted off a child with the grey cough, to see what lay beneath.

"Meera."

"I heard." She was beside him at the storehouse door, her wrist level with his on the rope. "Goat track. We could still—"

"No. Listen." He kept his voice low and even, the one he used on a burial detail. "You are a guide I hired at Kothi Shilak. You are not a smuggler, you are not the Salt Fox, and you know nothing of Varun. You came because the rope took you. Every word of that is a lie to Varun, and lies to him are free. Say them as if you were bored."

Her mouth twisted. "I've never been bored in my life."

"Then borrow mine."

He stepped out into the lane and raised both hands to the height of his shoulders. The left one would not go higher. He let them see that too.

The lane was full of grey wool and small brass bells. Twenty men, perhaps more, with the red-moustached captain from the first patrol standing at the front. Behind them, at the edge of the trampled snow, stood a knot of people in onion-ochre canvas. Lalit with his spyglass. Two roped pairs. And Asha, arms folded and face turned half away, like a woman waiting for a ferry she had not paid for.

In the middle of them all, on a tall bay horse, sat Varun.

He looked well. That was the first hard thing. His old coat was brushed clean, the pewter buttons dull in a row, the one brass button among them catching the light. He had slept in a guest wing. He swung down with the ease of a man stepping out of a carriage and came across the snow with both arms spread.

"Captain-ji!" He seemed truly glad. "Gods, you look dreadful. Sleeves too short. Is that a woman's pattu?"

"Lieutenant."

"Not anymore, I'm afraid." Varun drew a paper from inside his coat. The wax was red and stamped with a small bell. "I have a duty, and you'll forgive me. I'd rather be reading it to you in the post room over tea."

He read it aloud, in the clear voice of a man who had been praised for reading. The warrant named Rohan, captain of the Shilak wardens, as a thief of the queen's seal and a traitor to the regency of Neshar. It commanded him taken, searched and held for judgment at the Fair.

Rohan looked at the doors. At the soldiers waiting at them with their hands on the latches.

"I surrender," he said.

Varun's eyebrows rose. "I haven't finished the last line."

"I know it. It says all houses harbouring the accused may be searched." Rohan kept his hands where they were. "I am the accused. Search me. Keep your men out of the sick rooms and I will not give you an hour's trouble. You have my word as a captain of wardens, for what it is now worth to you."

"It's worth a great deal." Varun said it kindly. Then he turned his head and raised one finger, and the soldiers at the doors stepped back, and Rohan let out a breath that was only breath.

It had cost him nothing yet. He knew that. It was the first coin of a long account.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They searched him in the lane, in front of everyone.

Two soldiers stripped him of his borrowed brown pattu and his coat and ran their hands down every seam. They found the speech on the back of Nimmi's tally sheet, the folded writ and a flat tin of wax. Varun read the speech, smiled, glanced at the rest as a man glances at a lock without its key, and slid it all back into the lining. They found his knife. They slit the nape of the coat where the stitches had already been picked loose, found the empty pocket, and held it up.

"Nothing, sir."

Varun took the coat. He turned it over and put his thumb in the pocket and felt about, slowly, as if it might be shy. Then he found the last thing, folded in the lining: the forearm's length of grey wool, rolled and tied with its own cord, one end whipped in neat black thread.

"Ah," he said softly.

Rohan did not move. He did not let his face move. But the wool in Varun's hand was Dilan's, and Varun knew it, because Varun had stood in the warden hall with ten others and watched him swear on it.

"I remember this," Varun said. "You wept, a little. You tried to hide it." He studied the clean-cut end. Then he held it out, almost tenderly, and Rohan took it with a hand that did not shake. "Keep it. I'd never take that. I thought it was the stone, Captain-ji, truly. I thought you'd be the sort to hide it in the thing you love."

And then Varun smiled.

It was not the smile of a man who had lost. Rohan watched it spread and knew it for what it was, as a surveyor knows the true shape of a hill once the mist lifts. Varun had not wanted to find the stone on him.

"It's not on you," Varun said. "That's rather lovely. If it were, I'd hang you at noon on the nearest tree and be back in Garh Neshar for supper. But you've put it somewhere." He tapped the empty pocket. "The regent pays for the stone. Not for you. A lordship, you know. The Walnut Terraces, and the deed's already drawn, to be signed when the thing is in his hand. So I need you breathing, and near, and worried." He glanced at the lane, at the doors, at the grey faces behind the shutters. "And I need this village to stay exactly where it is, with all its little hiding places."

*Every house in Ranu is a hole in the ground,* Rohan thought. *And he has just told me he will turn out every one.*

Rohan did not know where the stone was. He had made sure of that. It was the only comfort he had, and it was cold.

"Jassi's second," said Varun. "You walked the gully ahead of me. What are you doing here?"

Meera had come to the storehouse door and stopped at the end of the rope, her face as empty as a plate.

"A guide. Hired at Kothi Shilak."

"Hired, and tied to you by wool?" Varun's gaze dropped to the red rope, running from Rohan's wrist to hers, and he whistled. "A jogini binding! I wore one myself to come over, but I never thought to see you in one." He walked the length of the cord with his eyes. "And it holds you the way it held me? She can't leave you?"

"Eight spans," said Rohan. It was true, and free to tell. "Past that we both freeze."

"Then she's no use to me loose, and no use to you gone." Varun pondered it as a man studies a convenient shelf. "Chain her to your belt, then. Two spans of iron. The rope can stay, I wouldn't dare cut it. Hired guide." He tipped his head to Meera. "No hard feelings, my dear. You're the best-informed witness in the valley, and I'd hate to lose you to a cold night."

"Whatever you pay," said Meera, in a voice so bored that Rohan nearly believed her.

Varun's eyes passed over her without a flicker. He was looking at Jassi's second, a woman in a stained and frayed pattu, and nothing more. He did not know what she was. Rohan saw how thin the single thread of her safety was, and that it ran through him.

The irons were cold. A soldier with a wet cough locked one cuff around Rohan's left wrist, then the right, and a short chain to his belt. Meera got a single band around her left forearm, pulled over her glove, and a length of chain that ended at his waist. She did not look at it. She looked at the middle distance, with the same flat patience she gave a bad rope.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Now," said Varun. "Everyone's out, I think. Everyone who can stand."

They had come to the doors. They always did, in the end, the sick-house people, the nursing ones with grey faces and knotted shawls. Nimmi stood in her own doorway under the fresh-chalked tally. Pinku was at her skirts, mouth open. Behind her, along the lane, a dozen more.

Varun mounted the lowest step of the nearest house and raised his voice, so it would carry.

"Good people of Ranu! I am your regent's officer, and I have come to protect you. I'm sorry for the soldiers. A man in this valley has stolen what belongs to the crown, and he's stood in your kitchens and eaten at your tables." He swept a hand toward Rohan. "That's one. But there's another thing, and I think you ought to hear it, because it concerns your dead."

The lane went still.

"There is a runner of Kothi Shilak," said Varun. "Kiran. His crew sells to a middleman I know well, and I've had many a long winter of their talk, over many a pot. There was a night two winters ago when a young warden died on the road ice below the third bend." He turned to Rohan, and his face softened in the way of a man handing over a hard gift. "Dilan. You remember him, Captain-ji. You loved him. The men I buy from told me the runner who was on his rope that night was Kiran. A boy of twenty-two, with his ear frozen to the shelf, who walked away alive and left your Dilan dead."

Nobody breathed.

"He's in that house." Varun pointed, with the open palm of an honest man. Nimmi's house. The third marked door. "He has been sleeping in your sickhouse, Rohan, on your side of the wall. The smuggler who killed your boy."

Rohan heard the word *Kiran* and the world did a very slow thing.

He saw an ear with its rim gone. He saw a scrap of frozen skin in a warden's report, torn from the rim of an ear. He saw a young man's eyes go to his face on the first day and flinch, and a boy asleep with a fist closed on something. He saw a woman's flat hand pressed to her coat all through a snowed-in morning, as over a bruise she would not name, and the notion he had had about it, the one he had not picked up.

He picked it up now. It was heavy.

He turned his head. He could not help it.

Meera stood a chain's length away with her eyes on nothing. Not the ground. Not the sky. She was looking at a spot in the air between a soldier's shoulder and a roof post, a place with nothing in it, and her face had the stillness of a woman holding a full cup on a staircase.

She did not look at him. That was the answer, or it was not. He could not tell.

A soldier shifted. A child began to cry. At the end of the lane Nimmi said, very clearly, "He's a sick man in my care, and you'll not lay a finger on him," and Varun, with a small bow, said that of course nobody would. A man too ill to run did not need to be chained. A guard on the door would do, and Nimmi could keep her ward.

"For now," he added, lightly, and stepped down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Rohan had thought he would be furious.

He was not. He stood in the trampled snow with Dilan's rope in his hand, and a kind of silence came up in him, the silence of the morning after a burial. There was a name in it now, and a face attached to the name, and the face had been asleep in a room on the other side of a plank wall while he laughed at a bench with three children.

*He chose not to ask,* he thought. *In the passage. He saw the ear and chose not to ask. Why did he choose?*

*Because he did not want the answer.*

He made himself look at the rest of the lane, because it was what he did with things he could not carry. The soldiers had brought buckets of ash. Three of them were working along the row of marked houses, wetting the old handprints with a rag and pressing new ones over them, plate-wide, grey, fresh. The patrol's rule made sense of it. A week's work, done in an hour, for the pleasure of seeing it done.

Nimmi's door took the last print. The soldier smoothed the fingers with his thumb.

Asha stood ten paces off, watching.

She had not said a word since the warrant. She stood with her thumbs hooked in her belt, at the edge of the ochre canvas, and her eyes went to the ash. Then to the lintel, where a child had scratched a lopsided goat into the wood. Then to Varun's back.

The brash set of her mouth slackened, and she went quiet, the way people do who have just done sums and don't like the total.

She had been paid to track a stone, he thought. She had not been paid to stand in a lane of marked doors.

Her eyes met his, briefly. They were hard and pale and unreadable. She looked away first.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The lockhouse was a squat stone store at the foot of the village, built for grain. It had one door, barred outside, and one slit of a window. Someone had swept it for the occasion. The floor smelled of damp oats.

A soldier shoved the door shut and the bar fell, and then it was dark, and then it was not, because Meera had found the slit and stood in the thin grey light with her back to him.

The chain ran from his belt to her arm. It was two spans, and slack, and it lay between them on the oats.

She sat, slowly. He sat. Neither spoke.

He had given his word at the grove that he would not ask what had been said there. He had kept it, to the letter, all night. He turned that over now as a man turns a coin he is afraid is bad. The promise concerned the grove. It did not concern Dilan. It did not concern her brother's ear. It left him free to put a question, and the freedom was no kindness, because the answer scared him.

He knew the rope. He knew its price. A lie would take her hand.

A truth would take her brother.

He could see her breath in the light. She had pulled the left hand inside her pattu, as she had all morning, and was holding it there against her ribs.

"Meera."

"Don't." It was barely a sound. "Not here. They'll hear."

"Then I will keep it low." He moved a little nearer, until the chain lay across his knee and he could speak into the dark beside her ear and no one on the other side of the door could hear a word. He had spent his life asking precise questions, and he made this one the most precise of all, because he found that he could not say it any other way.

"Was Kiran on Dilan's rope?"

# Chapter 26: No

The question sat in the dark between them, and Meera did the sum.

She did it the way she did every sum, fast and in a column. Three lines. Silence, truth, lie.

Silence first, because silence was free. She had leaned on it for sixteen days. But silence given to this question, by this man, in this room, was not silence. It was a yes with the sound cut off. Rohan read a quiet the way he read a ledger. He would hear it and know. Then he would carry it, because he could carry nothing else. He would stand before the assembly with the queen's stone in his hand, and when they asked him what he knew, he would say it. He had never learned how to do anything else with a fact.

The truth was shorter. *Yes.* One word, and a boy who could not stand at a woodpile without a wall would hang from a deodar with a peg hammered in for him. Rohan had sworn on a rope end before eleven wardens to find the man at the far end of it. Varun had already pointed at the house.

Then the third line.

A lie about this would be a great one. She knew the scale, because she had taught it to children at the Shilak Cairn for ten winters. A great lie killed flesh. The cold would go to her hands first.

She had two hands, and she was a guide.

*Two fingers,* she thought. *Three at the worst. I can still hold a rope with the right.*

It was not courage. It was bookkeeping. Kiran's life stood on one side of the ledger, and a part of her body stood on the other, and the numbers were not close.

"No," said Meera.

The word came out flat and ordinary, and she hated how well it sounded.

For a heartbeat nothing happened. Then the rope on her wrist went to ice.

It did not feel like cold. Cold she knew, the plain honest kind that came off the Saddle and could be argued with. This came from inside the wool, from the knot, and ran down her left hand in a single pull, as if someone had drawn a wet thread through her bones. Her palm went numb. Her middle finger stung and let go. The ring finger and the little finger did not sting at all. They simply stopped being hers.

She did not look. She did not need to. Inside the glove, under the iron band they had clamped over it, two fingers had gone white and hard as candle wax, and she could feel exactly where her hand ended.

She made her breath go in through the nose. In, two, three. Out.

She drew the hand off her knee and into her lap, under the fold of her pattu, and laid the good one over it like a lid.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Rohan did not move.

She could not see his face. The slit of window threw its grey light along the wall, not across it, and he sat on the dark side of the chain with his shoulders square. He had gone so still that she heard oats shift under the iron.

She waited for the sound. Something would say it, the wind in a stone, a note climbing. It was what the cairns did. But there was no cairn in a grain store, only the rope, and the rope was only wool, and it lay between them as quiet as a snake in winter. He had not asked for a promise. There was nothing to hum.

*Say something,* she thought at him. *Say you don't believe me. Say anything.*

Her shoulders had begun to shake.

She had not given them leave. It came up through her ribs in small hard jerks, the shaking of a body that has been told a great lie and is trying to throw it off, and she clamped her teeth to keep the noise in. The good hand pressed down on the bad one. Under the pattu she felt nothing at all through the glove, and that was the worst of it. Not pain. A gap, where a hand had been.

In the dark, the chain moved.

It was a small sound, iron on oats. She turned her head. Rohan's right hand had come up off his knee, slowly, the cuff dragging, and it hung in the air in front of her pattu with the fingers a little open.

She knew what it was. She had done it herself, in a hollow under the Saddle's lip, for a man with a bad rib and a worse story. He only had to find her bare skin. A wrist, the soft place under the thumb. He would take the cold off her and into himself, and he would know the whole shape of it in one breath: *she lied, and it was great.* Not what the truth was. He would not need to learn it. The size of the lie would tell him.

She did not pull her hand away. She was too tired and too cold, and part of her wanted to be known by him more than it wanted to be safe.

His hand stayed where it was for three breaths.

Then it went down. It lay flat on the oats beside his knee, palm to the floor, as if he had put it somewhere to keep it out of trouble.

"Thank you for answering," said Rohan.

His voice was low and perfectly level. She would have known nothing from it if she had not listened to him lie to himself for three weeks.

"Don't," she said. It scraped. "I didn't do it for you."

"No." A pause. "That I believe."

It was true. She'd said it clean and the rope took it clean, and she was grateful for the one small thing that was free.

He said no more. He moved his weight a hand's width away on the oats, no further than the chain allowed, and sat looking at the wall. It was the courtesy of a man leaving a room without leaving it.

She could not tell what it was. She turned it over, and over, and it would not settle. Either he had decided she was telling the truth, and had spared her the shame of being tested, or he had known she wasn't, and had let her keep the lie because the alternative was a rope end and a boy with half an ear. She had no way to find out. Asking would cost her another lie, or another truth. And he would not say, because he would not say what he did not mean.

*Mercy,* she thought, *or belief.*

*Both of them look the same from here.*


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The light moved.

It came slowly up the wall, a long grey bar, and stopped on the lintel, and sat there. The lockhouse smelled of damp oats and iron and, faintly, of his coat, which he no longer wore. They had taken that. He was in his shirt and a borrowed felt jerkin, and he had not said he was cold.

She did not say she was.

Her left hand had stopped being a thing she could picture. The numbness had climbed past the knuckles and was working in at the wrist, under the iron band. The metal was colder than her skin now, which was a strange thing to know. She pressed her elbow against her ribs and felt the brass badge under the wool, Dilan's, with the nail-scratched letters on its back. She pressed it with her arm because she could no longer feel it with the hand.

She hoped the dead boy had understood her. She had never asked him anything.

Outside, boots crossed the frozen snow. Two men, by the sound, coming to stand at the door and settle. One had a wet cough she knew. He was the soldier who had locked the cuff on Rohan.

"Gods, I'm froze," said the cough, and spat.

"Then stamp. You'll be warm soon enough."

"Tonight, they're saying?"

"Pasang says tonight." There was a pause. She heard a stopper pulled, a swallow. "Not the lieutenant. The captain's got the other paper, the sealed one, from the hall. Lieutenant wants it all held till the stone turns up. Captain says the paper doesn't care what the lieutenant wants."

"What's the paper say?"

"Burn the cough."

Meera's good hand went still on the bad one.

"Burn it how?"

"How d'you think? Houses are marked for a reason. Ash on every lintel, so we know which to light and which to leave." The man laughed, short and without much in it. "Pasang says it's the cleanest way. A fire takes the sick and the sickness both. And whoever's been sitting up with them. Healer, the woman, the hired hands. Anyone who's seen what the regent's grain sacks look like in a house where the children are dying."

"Seen what?"

"Ask the regent."

"I'd sooner not."

"Aye. Me neither."

They stopped talking. She heard the cough go, and the stopper again.

Seven houses, she thought. Forty-one sick in the lane, and the ones who tended them. Kiran, in the third marked house, on a pallet by the plank wall with a guard on the door and a chalk tally overhead that Nimmi had made herself. Pinku, six, with a clay lamp and a walnut tied up in red thread. A woodpile behind a goat shed, and in the third stack, between two deodar rounds, a stone as blue as a shut eye.

Fire would not burn lapis. But a cord could burn. A shed could come down across a woodpile. A soldier with a torch had no reason to be gentle with logs.

She had lied to save a boy from a rope. Tonight they would put him in a furnace, and she had spent two fingers.

*That's not how the count goes,* she told herself. *You don't get to say it was for nothing. Not until the night's out.*

Beside her, Rohan said in his burial-roll voice, "Seven houses."

"I heard."

"Forty-one in the lane. Nimmi has eight she would not have moved at all." The chain made a small sound. "There is no way to move them."

"Then we don't move them."

"Meera—"

"I'm thinking." It was a poor answer, and she heard how it sounded. Her breath fogged in the slit of light. It went up pale and thin and hung there.

He turned his head toward her. In the dark she felt the look land and did not meet it, and then she did.

He was looking at her face, not at her hand. His eyes were dry, very steady, the colour of slate at dusk. There was a question in them, the same one, but different now. He had asked it once and had his answer. This one was of another kind. It asked whether she was cold.

"I'm here," said Meera.

Nothing happened. No flare of white, no climb in the air. A small thing. It was true by the measure of the rope. She was here, and breathing, though she was a long way from well, and she had not said *well*. She had picked the one word the mountain could not argue with.

His mouth moved. It was not a smile. "You have a gift," said Rohan, "for the kind of truth the cold can't catch."

"I've had practice."

"Yes." He studied the oats between his boots. "So have I."

It was the nearest he came. He did not say *I know you lied.* He did not say *I believe you.* She waited for it, hungry as she was, and it stayed unsaid, and he went back to looking at the wall.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Time went strangely in the lockhouse.

The bar of light slid across the lintel and down the far wall. Somewhere a bell rang once, a goat's, tinny and unconcerned. Nimmi's voice rose in the lane, sharp, and was silenced. A cart rolled past with something heavy in it. She heard the soldiers grunt and counted twelve strokes of a mallet, a hammering sound she could not place until she did. They were nailing planks across Nimmi's goat shed door. They were sealing it for the night.

At some point the wrist went.

She knew it the way she knew a crossing going bad, by the thing that was missing. The iron band had been cold against her skin, and then it was no colder than the skin. She tried to flex the hand inside the glove. The thumb moved. The first finger moved. The middle, slowly. The other two lay as if they belonged to a stranger sleeping.

*Ring and little,* she thought. *Left.*

She'd tied her last knots with the right. Hadn't she? She'd drawn a knife in the Rope-Hut one-handed, swung an adze on the Teeth, written the count. She'd been right-handed for twenty-nine winters. It would be a nuisance. It would be less than a nuisance. Hakam in Kothi Shilak had three fingers and ran a goat string with a quickness that made young crossers swear.

*Hakam's were the mountain's doing,* said a small cold voice. *Yours are your own.*

She shut her eyes and put the voice in a column. Not now.

The shaking had stopped. That, she knew from experience, was not good. The body threw the cold off in shudders as long as it could afford them. When the shudders stopped, the heat for them had run out.

Fire does not warm lie-cold. She'd told that to a hundred crossers. She'd told them as one tells children not to lick the iron post, a small clean fact with a lesson in it. Only time or the loophole would do it, and time was a thing she did not have, and the loophole was a hand lying flat on the oats an arm's length away.

She did not ask for it.

Rohan had not offered. She would remember that, whatever it meant. Twice in one day he had stood at the edge of a thing and turned his own hand back. Each time he had given her the dignity of keeping her own skin. It might be love. It might be a man declining to open a door he did not want to see behind. It might be the pure cold courtesy of a warden who knew the law and would not lay hands where he'd been told not to.

*You could ask him,* she thought.

*And he'd say he doesn't know. And that would be true too.*

Her head had begun to swim. She put her back against the stone and made herself look at the slit again. The grey had gone to blue at the edges. Dusk, then. How many hours since the question? Six? She had thought it was three.

*Count.*

It was what she did. She'd counted goats and sacks and fingers and graves. She counted breaths now, because they were the one thing nobody had taken.

One. The oats smelled of mouse and damp.

Two. Iron lay cold against her forearm.

Three. A boot scraped. The sentry shifted on the other side of the door.

Four. Somewhere past the walls a child coughed, a long wet rattle, and was hushed.

Five.

She breathed out, and the fog of it was thinner than before. She watched it go.

Six. The brass badge pressed her ribs where she could still feel it.

Seven. There was a seam of warmth along her right side where Rohan's shoulder was, an arm's width off, not touching.

Eight.

Nine. They would come with torches after full dark. She tried to think what Nimmi would do, and could not; her thoughts slid like wet wool on a slope.

Ten.

*Keep counting,* said the guide in her, the one who had walked a hundred people over the Saddle in a whiteout, who knew that the thing that killed you was not the mountain but the moment you stopped naming steps. *Name them. Don't let go of the number.*

Eleven.

Twelve.

The light in the slit went from blue to black, and Meera counted on.

# Chapter 27: Torches

Meera's counting stopped somewhere past four hundred.

Rohan had been listening to it for an hour. It was a whisper at the edge of hearing, the numbers falling further apart, like water from a failing roof. Four hundred and eleven. Then a long gap. He counted ten of his own and nothing came.

"Guide."

"Mm." It was the sound of someone answering from the bottom of a well.

He got up. The door was four paces off, inside the chain, and he went to it with the iron dragging at his belt and struck the planks with the flat of both cuffed hands.

"Sentry."

"What." The wet cough, thick and sour.

"The woman is dying of cold. Your lieutenant wants me breathing, and I am chained to her." All of it was true, and he said it in the voice he used for burial rolls. "Give me my coat and the brown pattu. If you're asked why, say a captain of wardens gave you an order and you chose to hear it."

A silence on the other side. A swallow from a flask. Then the bar scraped, the door opened a hand's width, and a bundle struck him in the chest. The door shut. The bar fell.

His coat. The borrowed pattu with its hole in the hem. And in the right pocket, where he had left them three days ago, his gloves.

He put them on with the cuffs in the way, working the leather over the iron one finger at a time. Then he went back across the oats and knelt behind her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She sat against the wall with her chin on her chest and her good hand clamped over the bad one. He touched neither.

"I am going to move you," he said. "I will not touch your skin. If I do by accident, say so and I will stop."

No answer. He took that for leave.

He laid the coat over her shoulders and drew the brown pattu across it. He tucked the folds under her knees, then slid in behind her, his back to the stone, and pulled her gently between his knees until she lay along his chest with her head under his chin. Layer on layer, wool and leather and his own body. His gloved hands closed over the front of the coat, and he kept his mouth well off the bare nape of her neck.

*Skin is the door,* he thought. *Keep the door shut.*

The iron band was the worst of it. It lay around her left forearm over a hand she could no longer feel, and the metal had gone colder than the night. He tore a strip from the hem of the pattu, where the hole already was, and packed it between the iron and her sleeve with the tips of two gloved fingers. The band sat looser. She made a small noise, not a word.

Fire did not warm lie-cold. He knew that as well as he knew the Closing Law. Only time or a hand could draw it off. But everything around the lie-cold could be kept away from her: the draught under the door, the stone at her back, the plain cold that would finish the work the rope had begun. If he kept that out, the rope's share might stay a share. It might not kill her.

It was the only arithmetic he had.

"I will talk," he said. "You need not answer. You need only stay on this side of my voice."

For a long time there was only her breath, shallow, and shallower than it had been.

Then, thin and almost cross: "Talk about what?"

*She asked.* He closed his eyes. "True things. They cost nothing tonight."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Dilan laughed like a goat sneezing," said Rohan. "It came before the joke did. He'd be laughing at a sentence he hadn't finished, and then he'd have to apologise to us for ruining it. He laughed in the middle of the oath at the post, in front of the post commander. I gave him a week of latrine duty. He sang the whole week."

A small movement against his chest. He could not tell if it was a shiver.

"That hymn. You've heard me say. He sang it every watch, because he thought it was a love song. He could not carry a tune in a bucket. It went up where it should have gone down and the men used to cover their ears. And he'd look hurt, and then do it again." He stopped. His throat had closed on the last word and he waited for it to open. "I miss it. I did not know that I would. I expected to miss the boy. I did not think I would miss the noise."

Her right hand moved inside the pattu and found his gloved one, and held on to the leather. He let it.

"Ila measures," he said, after a while. "My sister. When she was seven she paced our house in her bare feet and told my father the kitchen was a hand shorter than the builder promised. She was right. He never forgave her or the builder. If she were in this room she'd have your chain at two spans and four fingers, and she'd say so aloud and laugh so loudly the sentries would think it was at their expense."

"Which it would be," said Meera. It was a thread of a voice, and it was entirely hers.

"Which it would be." He felt his mouth move. "She names the mountains for people she likes. There is a ridge on the survey sheet she calls Tamar's Shoulder, after a cook. I have asked what the cook did. She says, 'Fed me.'"

He told her about the old post room before it had a stove. He told her what his father said about the rope end, the one time he saw it: *Burn it, or carry it, but don't turn it into a kind of priest.* He told her he had carried it. He did not say *and now I have lost the man who stood at the other end of it,* because he did not know yet if that was true.

"My grandfather," he said, "went out in a storm to look to the cairns, for a party that was lost. They found him at the thaw. My father told it as a kind of prayer, and I never asked what it was like to find him." He breathed. "But he told me one thing once, when he'd been drinking. They found my grandfather's gloves lying on a flat stone beside him, one on the other, very neat. I've never worked out who he was tidying them for."

Silence. Then he heard it at the very bottom of the quiet, the way you hear a thread in a cloth: her breathing slowing.

"No," he said. "Meera. Stay."

He did the one thing he had not done since the Saddle. He hummed.

It was Dilan's hymn, and it came out badly. It rose where it should have fallen and cracked on the high note. He had no voice. He had never had one. But he knew the tune the way he knew his own boot size, and he went through the verse that began *keep me till the morning, love,* and into the next, flat, and getting flatter.

"That's," said Meera, against his collar, "flat."

"I know."

"It's a hymn." A pause, and a breath. "You can't help it. It's not even... it's not a love song, Rohan."

*Rohan.*

"He didn't know that," said Rohan, and went on humming.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It began in the ends of his fingers.

A pinch first, as when you carry cold iron too long. It was inside the gloves, in the pads of all ten fingers, a tightness that spoke of blood being asked to go somewhere it didn't want to go. He flexed them. The tips had a dull, wooden quality.

He had told no lie. He had touched no skin. This was neither of the two doors the cold used.

He went through the third.

*I will not let you fall on this mountain.*

He had said it in the Rope-Hut on a night of storm, with her mouth still warm against his, and the rope had hummed low and level under it. A promise made while bound. If it broke, it would strike him as a great lie struck. And Meera was slipping, and the slipping was a fall, and the rope, which had no opinion on how far Ranu stood from the mountain, was beginning to count.

*The spirits' jurisdiction is wider than I'd like,* he thought, dry as dust. It was the nearest he came to a joke that night.

He could end it. He had only to shuck a glove and lay his bare hand on her wrist. The cold would run up his arm, and he would know her lie in one breath, and its size, and the whole thing would be borne and ended.

And he would know. Not the truth, but that the truth was large.

He sat with his hands knotted over her coat and his fingertips going white in the leather, and he did not take off the glove.

"Four hundred and twelve," said Meera, very faintly. Then, a long time after: "Thirteen."

"Fourteen," said Rohan.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The first light came through the slit window at some hour past midnight, and it was orange.

He had not meant to look. The shutter was a ruin of warped slats that let a stripe of the lane through, and he held very still, because she had gone limp against him again and he did not want to wake the thing in her that was holding on.

Torches.

They came down the lane in a slow file, soot and sparks, and spread along the marked houses like a string of beads being laid out. A man to each door. A man at each corner. He counted forty before he ran out of the stripe to count them in. Behind them came a handcart with straw on it, and a second, and men with their arms full of bundles, which they stacked against the planks, up to the sill, up to the lintel where the ash handprints showed pale.

Straw against the wall. Forty torches held steady. The red-moustached captain stood at the front with a paper in one hand, waiting for the hour.

Behind the walls, a child coughed. A woman said something low. Rohan could not hear the words and did not need to.

Seven houses. Forty-one sick. The ones who tended them. A boy named Kiran, on a pallet by a plank wall.

*Thirty-two new graves,* he thought, *and I signed the road shut that put them there.* He had not said it aloud yet. He had meant to say it before the assembly, with the speech in his coat. Now he sat in that coat, in a grain store, with a woman dying in his arms and a lane of torches, and nobody on earth to say it to.

In the middle of the lane, outside the third marked door, Nimmi stood.

She had come out with no lamp and no shawl. She stood on her own step with her arms at her sides, beside the lintel with its fresh grey hand, and she did not plead and she did not run. She looked at the line of torches the way she looked at a patient who would not be saved. She was counting them.

"Don't," he said, softly, to no one. "Go in."

She did not go in.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The horse came down from the upper road at a walk.

It was a tall grey, not hurried. Its rider wore a plain cloak the colour of old slate, with no bell stitched on the shoulder, and he sat the saddle like a man long used to it. He rode into the circle of torches and stopped between the captain and the first house. He had a grey beard cut close, and the hard, tired face of a man who has done a great many things that someone else wrote down.

"Captain," he said.

His voice was low. It carried the way a good officer's does, without being raised, across the whole frozen lane.

"Sir." The red moustache had straightened. "The order's under the regent's own hand, sir. Burn the cough. I have it here."

"I know whose hand." The grey beard did not look at the paper. He looked at the houses. He looked for a long time at the straw, and the lintels, and the small shape of a woman standing very still on a step. "How many are in them?"

"The sick, sir. And whoever's nursing."

"Children."

"Some, sir."

"Yes," said the rider. He spoke the single word as though he were setting it down on a table. Then, with no change at all: "Lower the torches."

A shuffle. The captain's mouth opened. "Sir, the order—"

"I will answer for the order. Lower them."

For a breath no one moved. Rohan had seen that breath at the edge of a hanging, the moment before a man steps up or does not.

The nearest soldier put his torch to the snow.

It hissed. Then the next, and the next, down the long file, a ragged hiss of fire going out, until the lane was full of grey smoke and the smell of wet pitch. Somewhere a man coughed. Somewhere another began dragging straw back into the carts, with the slow, grateful, ashamed motion of a man putting down a load he was glad not to have had to light.

The rider stayed on his horse. He did not look at the captain again. He looked once, directly, at the woman on the step. Nimmi looked back at him, long and level, as if she were learning his face by heart.

He turned the grey and walked it back up the lane, into the dark.

Nimmi watched the place where he had gone until the smoke covered it. Then she turned and went in, and the door closed.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Rohan found that his hands had closed hard on the coat.

It was not a pardon. It was a night. At dawn the captain might send for a different officer, or the paper might be carried to someone with a harder face, and the straw would come back out of the carts. He knew that. A stranger had spoken one word and a lane had put its fire out, and Rohan did not know his name.

*A man obeyed his conscience in the regent's grey,* he thought. *Here, in this valley. On this night.* He had not thought it possible, or had not wanted to believe it, and it undid something in his chest that he had not known was tied.

He had spent twelve years believing that if he bent the law once, nothing would hold. He had just watched a man bend a sealed order, and forty torches had held, and had gone out.

Meera lay against him with her breath a ribbon. The orange was gone from the slats. The lane was a smudge of smoke under a lid of stars.

He bent his head until his mouth was a finger's width from the wool of her hood, and not a hair nearer.

"Live, Guide," said Rohan. "I won't ask you anything else about that rope."

The rope on his wrist hummed. Low, and level, and steady as a held note.

He felt it all the way up his arm. He had made another contract with the mountain, and this one he knew he could keep. It cost him a man's name and a promise made over a rope end before eleven wardens. His thumb found the clean-cut wool inside his coat and rested on it. He did not draw it out.

"Rohan," said Meera, from very far down.

"I'm here."

She said nothing more. But her good hand tightened on his glove, and the numbers came again, one by one, as faint as breath on glass, and by the time the stars paled behind the slats his fingertips had begun to ache with returning blood.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The blue came before dawn.

He had not slept. The sentry's cough stopped first, which was wrong, and then he heard nothing at all from the other side of the door. No boots. No flask.

A key slid into the bar's iron lock.

Rohan eased Meera against the wall, tucking the coat under her chin, and rose with the chain dragging at his belt. The bar lifted. The door swung out on the blue of the hour before the light, and the cold came in around the shape standing in it.

Asha. Onion-ochre canvas, her hair lank with the night, her face drawn and pale. A heavy iron key in one hand and a long knife in the other, the blade held low and loose.

She looked at Rohan. She looked past him at the shape in the corner, wrapped in a coat, and the sound of its breathing. Her eyes did not soften. They narrowed, as if she were adding up a column she did not want to add.

Behind him, Meera's boot scraped the oats. She was trying to stand.

"Don't," said Asha, "do that, Guide. Not yet." She did not step inside. She held the key where Rohan could see it, and she held the knife where he could see that as well.

"Price first."

# Chapter 28: Asha's Price

"Price first," said Asha, and she did not move out of the doorway.

Meera got her back flat to the wall and stood, because she would not bargain from the floor. The stone slid under her shoulder blades. The room tilted, righted itself, and tilted again. Rohan's coat hung off her like a tent.

"You have a key," she said. "That's a good start."

"It's a start." Asha glanced at the chain between them, then at the red rope under it. "A crown pardon is what I came over the Saddle for. A paper with my crew's names on it." She said it flatly, as if reading a bill. "I stood in the lane last night with my four. I watched the torches go down the line."

"We saw them," said Rohan.

"Then you saw a man with a paper tell forty men to burn children." Asha's knife hand stayed where it was. "I took Varun's silver because he paid on the nail and never haggled. I thought that was manners. It's just a man who'll never need to pay the full bill." She breathed out, and it smoked. "That pardon is the same noose with a nicer rope. When he's done with the seal, he's done with me."

"So you don't want the pardon," said Meera.

"I want my crew alive. Lalit, the ochre four, the girl on my second rope." She ticked them off on the fingers of the knife hand without lowering the blade. "And I want my name out of his books. He writes every payment in a tidy hand, because he believes in tidy hands. While my name's in it, I'm a hired knife for a traitor. I want it somewhere else."

"A warden can't pardon," Rohan said. "I'll not say otherwise to you."

"I know the law."

"Only the queen can. What I can do is stand before the assembly and say that you came in the dark, with a key, against the man who paid you. That is true. I will say it as loudly as I'm able." He kept his voice level and his hands still. "I can't promise what the assembly does with it."

Asha studied him. "By my grandmother's left boot. You've got a face that can't say a fine thing it can't back."

"No."

"That's rare. I'd charge extra for it, if it weren't so inconvenient." She looked at Meera. "And you?"

*She wants a pledge,* Meera thought, *and I am bound.* A promise made on the rope and broken would strike her like a great lie, and she had one good hand left to spend. She would give no word she could not stand behind.

So she did the other thing.

She put her right hand inside her shirt, past the brass badge, and drew out the count. The wax cover was warm from her skin. It had survived the chain, the iron band and the sentries, who had never thought to look where a woman kept a thing she loved. She opened it on her knee and found the page by feel.

She tore it out along the stitching, with her teeth and one hand.

"Dates," she said. "The middleman. Ten winters of drops, the day and the hour, in my hand. Crate marks: a bell scored through the stamp, then the stamp scraped off with a spoon, and the scrape is always a left-handed one. Where I paid, how much, what I was told when I asked who sold." She held it out. "It's a ledger. It's the only kind of evidence I've got."

Asha didn't take it. "That's your neck."

"It's his." Meera turned the count over and set her thumbnail to the spine, where the binding thread ran in tight little stitches. She had re-stitched that spine after every drop for ten winters. The night after the last one she had sewn the button in under the thread, so nobody would find it unless he meant to take the book apart. She picked the first stitch loose. Then the second. The thread was waxed and fought her. She worked the third out with a tooth and the point of her nail, while Asha watched and Rohan held his breath, and the binding gaped like a mouth. A little thing slid out onto her palm. Brass, domed, plain, with a shank that had never held a thread.

"And this. The crate man left it when I paid. His courier was meant to carry the twin. I never saw the courier. I never saw the man." She turned it so the lamp caught the dome. "But I've seen this pattern. A brass one, new among the pewter, on Varun's coat in the yard at Kothi."

Asha stared at it. Her thumb had been rubbing the pewter button on her own cuff, round and round. It stopped.

"I did too," she said slowly. "I watched Varun thumb a button like that the day he hired me. New, and not matching the rest. He said a pedlar sold it to him."

"Say it plain," said Rohan. His voice had dropped.

Asha said it plain, to him, not to Meera. "Varun paid me. Four silver a week and a promise of a paper. And the lowland man who sold your guide her bark was his runner, a thin one with a bad left foot who drank in the salt-road inns. I've seen the two of them at the same table." She took the page then, folded it twice and slid it into her sleeve. "That's the bill. A name for a name."

"Take the button too."

"No." Asha pushed Meera's hand closed over it. "You'll want it in front of a regent." The knife went into her belt. She fitted the iron key to the cuff on Rohan's wrist, and it turned with a click Meera felt in her teeth.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Both sentries sat against the lockhouse wall with their hands tied in three nested loops, snug as a bird's nest. The wet cough had a strip of ochre canvas in his mouth and the resigned look of a man who'd rather be tied than asked.

Meera stopped. She knew that knot. Three loops, one inside the next. It had hung in the juniper outside the Rope-Hut with sattu and apricots in it. It was carved on the cairn's inner face, two hands and a rope. *Same as cairn,* she had written in the count, and had not known why she wrote it.

"Asha," she said. "That knot. It's the one on the—"

Asha's glare came round, sharp as a nail. Not here, it said, and not before him. Meera shut her mouth. Silence was free. She put the question in a column and left it there.

Lalit stood at the corner with his spyglass hanging from his neck and a pony's lead in his fist. A heap of taken gear lay by the wall. He dragged her pack and her knife out of it with his boot and held them out. He wouldn't look at the captain of wardens. He looked at Meera, once, and then at the ground.

"The lane's quiet," he said. "The red moustache is asleep in a chair. The lieutenant went up the Garh road at midnight, with his twenty. He thinks the village will be ash by morning. He wanted to be seen far away."

Asha's key took the forearm band off in two turns. The iron slid from Meera's arm with a clank, and the torn strip of pattu fell out with it. Cold air touched her sleeve, and she could not feel it on the left hand at all.

She put the hand in her coat and said nothing. She slung the pack on her good shoulder, and the knife went into her boot.

"There's a door I want," she said. "Nimmi's house. The woodpile behind the goat shed."

Rohan said nothing. He had never asked her where she put it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Nimmi opened before she knocked.
